diffeomorphism groups of solid tori and the rational pseudoisotopy stable range
João Lobo Fernandes, Samuel Muñoz-Echániz

Abstract
abstract. We compute the rational homotopy groups of the classifying space BDiff ∂ ⁡ ( S 1 × D d − 1 ) \BDiffb(S^{1}\times D^{d-1}) of the topological group of diffeomorphisms of S 1 × D d − 1 S^{1}\times D^{d-1} fixing the boundary for d ≥ 6 d\geq 6 , in a range of degrees up until around d d . This extends results of Budney–Gabai, Bustamante–Randal-Williams, and Watanabe. As consequences of this computation, we determine the rational pseudoisotopy stable range for compact spin manifolds with fundamental group 𝐙 \mathbf{Z} of dimension d ≥ 6 d\geq 6 to be [ 0 , d − 5 ] [0,d-5] , and compute in this range the rational homotopy groups of BDiff ∂ ⁡ ( S 1 × N ) \BDiff_{\partial}(S^{1}\times N) for compact simply-connected spin ( d − 1 ) (d-1) -manifolds N N . Finally, by combining our results with work of Krannich–Randal-Williams and Kupers–Randal-Williams on BDiff ∂ ⁡ ( D d ) \BDiffb(D^{d}) , we compute the rational homotopy groups of the space Emb ∂ ⁡ ( D d − 2 , D d ) \Emb_{\partial}(D^{d-2},D^{d}) of long knots in codimension 2 2 for d ≥ 6 d\geq 6 , again in the same range.



1
Introduction

The homotopy type of the classifying space
\BDiffb(M)
of the topological group of diffeomorphisms of a smooth manifold
1
1

1

By
manifold
, we will always mean
smooth
manifold.

M
fixing its boundary has become one of the main objects of interest in the interplay between geometric topology and homotopy theory. Classically, this has been studied via the
surgery-pseudoisotopy approach
, a two-step program that, first, approximates
\BDiffb(M)
by a
larger
classifying space of
block diffeomorphisms

\BbDiffb(M),
which can be understood via
surgery theory
, and second, describes the failure of this approximation in a range around
\frac{d}{3}
, where
d
is the dimension of
M
, in terms of Waldhausen’s algebraic
K
-theory of
M
(more on this in the next subsection). While this program has been very useful and successful in providing low degree information about
\BDiffb(M)
(see e.g.
[
32
,
18
,
16
]
), it fails to address this homotopy type away from the aforementioned range of
\frac{d}{3}
.

In recent years, and out of the vision of Michael Weiss (as seen in
[
78
]
), a new program for studying
\BDiffb(M)
has emerged, which, in principle, allows one to understand this homotopy type in an unlimited range of degrees. Among several successes of this program, we highlight the computations of Kupers–Randal-Williams
[
60
]
, Krannich
[
57
]
, and Krannich–Randal-Williams
[
55
]
on the rational homotopy groups
\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\BDiffb(D^{d}))
in ranges far from the one mentioned above. In turn, hinging on these computations, one can obtain information about
\BDiffb(M)
for any compact simply-connected spin manifold
M
(see e.g.
[
38
, Cor. C]
). We can thus view
\BDiffb(D^{d})
as a
stepping stone
to understanding the diffeomorphism group of any compact simply-connected spin manifold.

In this work, we focus on diffeomorphism groups of compact spin manifolds with fundamental group
\mathbf{Z}
. For this goal, the
solid torus

S^{1}\times D^{d-1}
is a natural analog to
D^{d}
. By an adaptation, due to Bustamante–Randal-Williams
[
22
]
, of the program just mentioned, together with recent advances of the first-named author
[
63
]
to treat odd-dimensional manifolds and of the second-named author
[
66
]
to simplify the approach via
trace methods
, we carry out a computation of the rational homotopy groups of
\BDiffb(S^{1}\times D^{d-1})
in a certain range, and derive several corollaries. In the following sections, we present our main results.

1.1
Diffeomorphisms and concordances of solid tori

The homotopy type of
\BDiffb(S^{1}\times D^{d-1})
has previously been studied by Budney–Gabai
[
15
]
, Bustamante–Randal-Williams
[
22
]
, and Watanabe
[
75
]
. Our first main result extends our current knowledge of its rational homotopy type in high-dimensions—that is,
d\geq 6
—in the following way. Throughout this paper, we will denote the rational homotopy groups of a space
X
by
\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X)
.

Theorem A
.

For
n\geq 3
,
d\in\{2n,2n+1\}
and
1\leq*\leq 2n-3
, there is a surjective homomorphism

\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\BDiffb(S^{1}\times D^{d-1}))\longrightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&\text{if $d=2n$,}\\
\K^{\mathbf{Q}}_{*+1}(\mathbf{Z}[\mathbf{Z}])&\text{if $d=2n+1$,}\end{array}\right.

which is an isomorphism in degrees
1\leq*\leq 2n-4
, where
\K^{\mathbf{Q}}_{*}(\mathbf{Z}[\mathbf{Z}])
denotes the rationalised algebraic
K
-theory groups of the ring
\mathbf{Z}[\mathbf{Z}]
of Laurent polynomials. Moreover, the kernel of this homomorphism is a countably infinite-dimensional
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space in degree
*=2n-3
.

Remark 1.1.1

(Relation to previous work)
.

A
recovers
[
22
, Thm. B]
of Bustamante–Randal-Williams by setting
d=2n
and
*\leq n-3
. Thus, our result improves loc.cit. in two ways: first, it effectively doubles the range, and second, it includes the odd-dimensional case
d=2n+1
. In a different direction, Budney–Gabai and Watanabe
[
15
,
75
]
discovered that the
(d-3)
-rd rational homotopy group of
\BDiffb(S^{1}\times D^{d-1})
is infinitely generated for all
2
2

2

Although stated only for
d=4
, their proof shows that
\pi_{d-3}^{\mathbf{Q}}\bigl(\BDiffb(S^{1}\times D^{d-1})\bigr)
is infinitely generated for all
d\geq 4
.

d\geq 4
.
A
recovers this result for even
d\geq 6
, and presents a new source of infinite generation for odd
d\geq 7
, namely in degree
d-4
.

Remark 1.1.2
.

The rationalised algebraic
K
-theory groups
\K^{\mathbf{Q}}_{*}(\mathbf{Z}[\mathbf{Z}])
featuring in
A
above can be completely computed by combining Borel’s computation
[
11
]
of
\K^{\mathbf{Q}}_{*}(\mathbf{Z})
with the Bass–Heller–Swan splitting
[
4
]
. Explicitly, there is an isomorphism

\K^{\mathbf{Q}}_{*}(\mathbf{Z}[\mathbf{Z}])\cong\begin{cases}\mathbf{Q}&\text{if $*=0,1$ or $*\equiv 1,2$ (mod $4$) with $*\geq 5$,}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Remark 1.1.3
.

Given a compact manifold
M
, its diffeomorphism group
\Diffb(M)
is an infinite-dimensional Frechét manifold and hence, by work of Palais
[
68
]
, has the homotopy type of a countable CW complex. In particular, its (rational) homotopy groups are countably generated. Since all countably infinite-dimensional
\mathbf{Q}
-vector spaces are abstractly isomorphic to
\mathbf{Q}^{\infty}
, exhibiting an infinitely generated subspace of
\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\Diffb(M))
in effect determines this vector space up to abstract isomorphism. (The same applies to other automorphism spaces considered in this text.) From now on, by
infinite-dimensional
or
infinite generation
we shall always mean
countably
infinite.

Remark 1.1.4
.

By smoothing theory, there is a similar description for the rational homotopy groups of the classifying space
\BTop_{\partial}(S^{1}\times D^{d-1})
of the group of homemomorphisms of solid tori—see
6.6.1
.

In the remainder of this introduction, we discuss applications of this computation to the rational homotopy type of diffeomorphism groups of more general manifolds, as well as to other variants of moduli spaces of manifolds. We begin by focusing on the topological group
\operatorname{C}(S^{1}\times D^{d-1})
of
concordance diffeomorphisms
(or
pseudoisotopies
) of solid tori, where
\operatorname{C}(M)\coloneq\Diff_{M\times\{0\}}(M\times[0,1])
. This group serves as a bridge between diffeomorphism groups of manifolds of different dimensions, as it fits into a fibre sequence

\Diffb(M\times[0,1])\longrightarrow\operatorname{C}(M)\longrightarrow\Diffb(M).

(1.1.1)

Moreover, its classifying space
\BC(M)
plays an essential role in the surgery-pseudoisotopy approach mentioned above, as we will discuss at length in the next section. Our second main result is a computation of its rational homotopy groups in a range of degrees when
M=S^{1}\times D^{d-1}
.

Theorem B
.

For
n\geq 3
,
d\in\{2n,2n+1\}
and
1\leq*\leq 2n-3
, there is a surjective homomorphism

\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\BC(S^{1}\times D^{d-1}))\longrightarrow\K^{\mathbf{Q}}_{*+1}(\mathbf{Z}[\mathbf{Z}])

which is an isomorphism in degrees
1\leq*\leq 2n-4
. Moreover, the kernel of this homomorphism is infinite-dimensional in degree
*=2n-3
.

Remark 1.1.5
.

Our methods can also analyse the restriction map
r:\BC(S^{1}\times D^{d-1})\to\BDiffb(S^{1}\times D^{d-1})
:

(i)

When
d=2n+1\geq 7
, it essentially follows from
A
(see
6.3.1
) that
r
is rationally
(2n-1)
-connected. In particular, since
\pi_{2n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\BDiffb(S^{1}\times D^{2n}))
is infinite-dimensional by
[
15
,
75
]
, the same holds for
\smash{\pi_{2n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\BC(S^{1}\times D^{2n}))}
.

(ii)

When
d=2n
, by
A
and
B
, the rational homotopy groups in degree
2n-3
of domain and target of
r
are both infinite-dimensional
\mathbf{Q}
-vector spaces. We can additionally prove that the homomorphism
\smash{\pi_{2n-3}^{\mathbf{Q}}(r)}
is surjective with infinitely generated kernel (see
6.2.2
).

Both of these can be seen as an improvement of
[
75
, Thm. 1.3 and Cor. 1.5]
and
[
13
, Thm. 1.3]
.

Remark 1.1.6
.

Technically,
B
is not directly deducible from
A
. In fact, the former will be a key step in showing the latter. We refer to
Section

1.6
for an explanation of the strategy of proof.

1.2
Rational pseudoisotopy range for manifolds with infinite cyclic fundamental group

One immediate observation from
B
is that, in the range of the theorem, the rational homotopy groups of
\BC(S^{1}\times D^{d-1})
are independent of the dimension
d\geq 6
. In a considerably lower range, this phenomenon is explained by an important result in geometric topology:
Igusa’s stability theorem

[
49
]
. This result is concerned with the connectivity of the
concordance stabilisation map

s_{M}:\operatorname{C}(M)\longrightarrow\operatorname{C}(M\times[0,1])

for a compact manifold
M
(see e.g.
[
49
, Chapter II, §1]
), and, in particular, establishes
\frac{\dim(M)}{3}
as an asymptotic lower bound for the
concordance
(or
pseudoisotopy
)
stable range
of
M
, denoted

\phi(M)\coloneq\max\{k\in\mathbf{Z}~~|~~s_{M\times[0,1]^{m}}\text{ is $k$-connected for all $m\geq 0$}\}.

(Replacing
k
-connected above by
rationally

k
-connnected gives the
rational concordance stable range

\phi^{\mathbf{Q}}(M)
.) The (rational) concordance stable range plays a central role in the
surgery-pseudoisotopy approach
for the (rational) homotopy type of
\BDiffb(M)
. For example, it is precisely the range in which the failure of approximating the latter space by its block analogue (as mentioned above) is controlled by Waldhausen’s algebraic
K
-theory of
M
, as a consequence of the work of Weiss–Williams
[
76
]
.

An important property of
\phi(M)
, established by Goodwillie–Krannich–Kupers
[
38
]
, is that it essentially only depends on the tangential
2
-type of
M
(see p.25 in loc.cit.). As a consequence of this and the work of
[
55
]
on the rational concordance stable range of
D^{d}
for
d\geq 6
, it was established in
[
38
, Cor. C]
that the rational concordance stable range for compact simply-connected spin manifolds
M^{d}
with
d\geq 6
is
\phi^{\mathbf{Q}}(M)=d-4
. Similarly, by a refined version of
B
, we deduce an analogous result for compact spin manifolds with infinite cyclic fundamental group.

Theorem C
.

Let
d\geq 6
, and let
M
be a connected compact spin
d
-dimensional smooth manifold (possibly with boundary) with
\pi_{1}(M)\cong\mathbf{Z}
. Then, the concordance stabilisation map

s_{M}:\operatorname{C}(M)\longrightarrow\operatorname{C}(M\times[0,1])

is rationally
(d-5)
-connected
3
3

3

For
d\geq 6
, the group of path-components
\pi_{0}(\operatorname{C}(M))
is an abelian group (hence rationalisable) by work of Hatcher–Wagoner
[
41
]
and Igusa
[
48
, Thm. 8.a.1]
(see (
4.3.10
)). Thus, the notion of
rational connectivity
is well-defined in this case.
, but not rationally
(d-4)
-connected. That is,
\phi^{\mathbf{Q}}(M)=d-5
. Moreover,

\pi_{d-4}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\operatorname{C}(M\times[0,1]),\operatorname{C}(M))

is an infinite-dimensional
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space.

Exploiting, once again, the tangential invariance of the concordance stable range, we obtain the following upper bound to the rational concordance stable range, as suggested to us by Manuel Krannich.

Corollary D
.

Let
d\geq 6
and
M
be a connected compact spin
d
-dimensional smooth manifold (possibly with boundary) such that
\H^{1}(\pi_{1}(M);\mathbf{Q})\neq 0
. Then, the rational vector space

\pi_{d-4}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\operatorname{C}(M\times[0,1]),\operatorname{C}(M))

is infinite-dimensional. In particular, we have
\phi^{\mathbf{Q}}(M)\leq d-5
.

Just like embedding spaces play an important role in the study of diffeomorphism groups, spaces of concordance diffeomorphisms are closely related to spaces of
concordance embeddings
. Fix an embedding of compact manifolds
M\subset N
, and let
\CEmb(M,N)
denote the space of embeddings
M\times[0,1]\hookrightarrow N\times[0,1]
fixed on
M\times\{0\}
and that send
M\times\{1\}
to
N\times\{1\}
(see (
4.1.11
) for details). There is a stabilisation map

s_{M,N}:\CEmb(M,N)\longrightarrow\CEmb(M\times[0,1],N\times[0,1]),

and Goodwillie–Krannich–Kupers studied in
[
38
]
the connectivity of this map—roughly, the
concordance embedding stable range
of
M\subset N
—when the handle codimension of
M
in
N
is at least
3
. As a corollary of
C
and the parametrised isotopy extension theorem, we determine the rational concordance embedding stable range for certain embeddings of submanifolds
M\subset N
of handle codimension
\leq 2
.

Corollary E
.

Let
d\geq 6
,
N
be a
d
-dimensional compact simply-connected spin smooth manifold and
M
a compact neat submanifold of
N
(both possibly with boundary), such that
N-M
is connected with
\pi_{1}(N-M)\cong\mathbf{Z}
. Then, the concordance embedding stabilisation map

s_{M,N}:\CEmb(M,N)_{\iota}\longrightarrow\CEmb(M\times[0,1],N\times[0,1])_{\iota}

where
(-)_{\iota}
stands for the path-component of the standard inclusion
M\times[0,1]\subset N\times[0,1]
, is rationally
(d-4)
-connected, but not rationally
(d-3)
-connected. In fact, the rational vector space

\pi_{d-3}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\CEmb(M\times[0,1],N\times[0,1])_{\iota},\CEmb(M,N)_{\iota})

is infinite-dimensional.

Remark 1.2.1
.

The standard inclusion
M=D^{d-2}\subset D^{d}=N
, for
d\geq 6
, satisfies the hypothesis of
E
.

Remark 1.2.2
.

Writing
d=\dim(N)
and
p
for the handle dimension of
M
in
N
, the main result
[
38
, Thm A]
states that if
p\leq d-3
, then
s_{M,N}
is
(2d-p-5)
-connected. Somewhat unexpectedly, we see from
E
that this formula does not generalise to
p=d-2
, as in this case
2d-p-5=d-3
.

1.3
Diffeomorphism groups of
S^{1}\times N
for
N
simply-connected spin

As mentioned before, we view
\BDiffb(S^{1}\times D^{d-1})
as a
stepping stone
to understanding
\BDiffb(M)
for any compact spin manifold
M
with fundamental group
\mathbf{Z}
. As a consequence of the surgery-pseudoisotopy approach together with our results on the rational concordance stable range, we can now justify this belief.

In the following result, write
L(-)\coloneq\Map(S^{1},-)
for the
free loop space
functor, let
\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}
denote the
free loop space involution
induced by complex conjugation on
S^{1}\subset\mathbf{C}
and, given an involution
\tau
on a
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space
A
, let
[A]_{\tau}
denote the
C_{2}
-coinvariants of
A
with respect to the involution
\tau
.

Corollary F
.

Let
N^{d-1}
be a compact simply-connected spin smooth manifold (possibly with boundary) of dimension
d-1\geq 5
. Then, in degrees
2\leq*\leq d-5
, there is an isomorphism

\displaystyle\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\BDiff_{\partial}(S^{1}\times N))\cong

\displaystyle\hskip 4.0pt\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&\text{if $d$ even,}\\
\K^{\mathbf{Q}}_{*+1}(\mathbf{Z}[\mathbf{Z}])&\text{if $d$ odd,}\end{array}\right.\oplus\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbf{Q}&\text{if $\partial N=\emptyset$ and $*=2$,}\\
0&\text{otherwise,}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\oplus\pi_{*-1}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}_{\partial}(N))\oplus\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}_{\partial}(N))

\displaystyle\oplus\left[\frac{\H_{*}(LN;\mathbf{Q})}{\H_{*}(N;\mathbf{Q})\oplus\H_{*+1}(N;\mathbf{Q})}\right]_{(-1)^{d+1}\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}}\oplus\bigoplus_{r=1}^{\infty}\H_{*}(LN;\mathbf{Q}).

Here, the quotient is the cokernel of certain monomorphism
\widetilde{\H}_{*}(N;\mathbf{Q})\oplus\widetilde{\H}_{*+1}(N;\mathbf{Q})\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\H}_{*}(LN;\mathbf{Q})
(see
Lemma

6.7.2
), and the first summand is the image of the map
\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\BDiff_{\partial}(S^{1}\times D^{d-1}))\to\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\BDiff_{\partial}(S^{1}\times N))
induced by any embedded disc
D^{d-1}\subset N
.

In particular, for
2\leq k\leq d-5
, the rational vector space
\smash{\pi_{k}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\BDiffb(S^{1}\times N))}
is finite-dimensional if and only if
\H_{k}(LN;\mathbf{Q})=0
. Additionally,
\smash{\pi_{d-4}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\BDiffb(S^{1}\times N))}
is infinite-dimensional if
\H_{d-4}(LN;\mathbf{Q})\neq 0
.

Remark 1.3.1
.

By surgery theory, understanding the homotopy type of
\BbDiffb(N)
, especially rationally, is often feasible—in fact, explicit rational models for this homotopy type have already been developed
[
6
, Thm. 4.24]
. See Examples
6.7.4
and
6.7.5
for the special cases
N=\mathbf{CP}^{n}
and
N=\Sigma
a homotopy sphere.

Remark 1.3.2
.

The group
\pi_{1}(\BDiffb(S^{1}\times N))
is abelian (or nilpotent) precisely when
\pi_{0}(\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}_{\partial}(N))
is so, in which case, the formula in the statement above extends to
*=1
.

Remark 1.3.3
.

The conclusion of
F
is only new in degrees above Igusa’s lower bound for the (integral) concordance stable range (roughly
\smash{d/3}
). Nevertheless, the result has several new consequences beyond exceeding this range. For instance, given that
\H_{*}(N;\mathbf{Q})
is a split summand of
\H_{*}(LN;\mathbf{Q})
, in
6.7.6
we show that for essentially all simply-connected spin closed manifolds
N
of dimension
d-1\geq 5
, the
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space
\smash{\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\BDiffb(S^{1}\times N))}
is infinitely generated in some degree
2\leq*\leq d-4
.

Thus, one could argue that infinite generation of the rational homotopy groups
\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\BDiffb(S^{1}\times N))
is
to be expected
, and far from a striking phenomenon. In the (rational) concordance stable range, the source of infinite generation is the
S^{1}
-equivariant homology of the free loop space
L(S^{1}\times N)
, i.e. the
cyclic homology
of
S^{1}\times N
, which is always either zero or infinitely generated. See
[
33
]
for a closely related result.

1.4
Long knots in codimension
2

Let us consider another object of interest in geometric topology, namely the space
\Emb_{\partial}(D^{d-k},D^{d})
of
long knots
of codimension
k\geq 0
. In high dimensions, the homotopy type of these spaces has been extensively studied for
k\neq 2
(when
k=2
, attention has so far mainly focused on its set of path components, as studied in high-dimensional knot theory; see e.g.
[
69
]
). For
k=0
, this space is simply the diffeomorphism group of the disc
\Diffb(D^{d})
, which, as already explained, is understood rationally
[
60
,
57
,
55
]
in an extensive range. For
k=1
, the component of the unknot is equivalent to the classifying space
\BDiffb(D^{d})
as a consequence of Cerf’s lemma, and is therefore covered by the previous case. For
k\geq 3
, the rational homotopy type of this space is accessible via embedding calculus and its relation to the little discs operad and graph complexes
[
31
,
1
,
2
,
35
,
9
]
), or integrally via the embedding surgery-pseudoisotopy program
[
66
]
. Both of these approaches are unavailable in codimension
k=2
.

Nevertheless, the parametrised isotopy extension theorem yields a fibre sequence

\Emb_{\partial}(D^{d-2}\times\mathbf{R}^{2},D^{d})_{u}\longrightarrow\BDiffb(S^{1}\times D^{d-1})\longrightarrow\BDiffb(D^{d}).

The natural restriction map
\Emb_{\partial}(D^{d-2}\times\mathbf{R}^{2},D^{d})\to\Emb_{\partial}(D^{d-2},D^{d})
is an equivalence for
d\geq 4
(the homotopy fibre is the space of framings
\Omega^{d-2}\mathrm{O}(2)
, which is contractible since
\mathrm{O}(2)
is
1
-truncated; see
Section

6.8

2

). Thus, our results on solid tori in
A
, together with the work of
[
60
,
57
,
55
]
on
\BDiffb(D^{d})
, yield the following computation.

Corollary G
.

For
d\geq 6
and
{*}\geq 1
, we have an isomorphism

\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\Emb_{\partial}(D^{d-2},D^{d}),u)\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&\text{for }d\text{ even and }{*}<d-3,\\
\mathrm{\K}^{\mathbf{Q}}_{*}(\mathbf{Z})&\text{for }d\text{ odd and }{*}<d-4,\end{array}\right.

where
u
denotes the unknot. Furthermore, in degree
{*}=d-3
for
d
even and degree
{*}=d-4
for
d
odd, the left-hand side is an infinite-dimensional
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space.

Remark 1.4.1
.

Fresse–Turchin–Willwacher
[
35
]
studied the embedding calculus approximation of the rationalised presheaf
\Emb^{\mathbf{Q}}_{\partial}(-,D^{d})
, denoted
T_{\infty}\Emb^{\mathbf{Q}}_{\partial}(D^{d-k},D^{d})
. Its homotopy groups are rational vector spaces which are, roughly and up to a degree shift, isomorphic to the homology of the hairy graph complex in codimension
k
–see Corollary 3 and equation (10) of loc.cit. By inspection in the case
k=2
, one verifies that
\pi_{*}(T_{\infty}\Emb^{\mathbf{Q}}_{\partial}(D^{d-2},D^{d})_{u})
is finite-dimensional in degrees
1\leq*\leq 2d-7
(see Corollary 4 and equations (1) and (2) there). By the last part of
G
, it follows that the natural comparison map
\Emb_{\partial}(D^{d-2},D^{d})_{u}\to T_{\infty}\Emb^{\mathbf{Q}}_{\partial}(D^{d-2},D^{d})_{u}
is not a rational equivalence if
d\geq 6
. See also
[
54
, Cor. 8.20]
.

1.5
On the orthogonal functor of topological Stiefel manifolds

In
[
55
, Thm. D]
, the authors use their results on the rational homotopy type of
\BC(D^{d})
to determine that of the second derivative spectrum of the orthogonal functor
\mathsf{Bt}(V)\coloneq\BTop(V)
; see Section 1.5 of loc.cit. for a brief account of Weiss’
orthogonal calculus

[
77
]
. Analogously, our results admit a reinterpretation in this framework (see
Section

6.8.2
for the explicit connection)–the relevant orthogonal functor is now

\mathsf{Vt}_{2}:V\longmapsto\Top(V\oplus\mathbf{R}^{2})/\Top(V,V\oplus\mathbf{R}^{2})\simeq\hofib(\BTop(V,V\oplus\mathbf{R}^{2})\to\BTop(V\oplus\mathbf{R}^{2})).

Here,
\Top(V,V\oplus\mathbf{R}^{2})
denotes the topological group of homeomorphisms of
V\oplus\mathbf{R}^{2}
that fix the subspace
V
pointwise. In particular,
\mathsf{Vt}_{2}(V)
is the
topological Stiefel manifold
of
V
-planes in
V\oplus\mathbf{R}^{2}
. What is currently known about this orthogonal functor is limited to the following:

•

The zero-th orthogonal approximation of
\mathsf{Vt}_{2}
is
P_{0}\mathsf{Vt}_{2}\simeq\Top/\Top(2)
by
[
51
, Thm. B]
.

•

The first derivative of
\mathsf{Vt}_{2}
is
1
-connective
4
4

4

That is, its homotopy groups vanish in degrees
*\leq 0
.
and satisfies
\Omega^{\infty+1}(\Theta\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(1)})\simeq\Omega^{\infty}(\K(\mathbf{S}))
; see
Lemma

6.8.3
.

As a consequence of
E
, we obtain the first results concerning the second derivative of
\mathsf{Vt}_{2}
.

Corollary H
.

The second Weiss derivative
\Theta\mathsf{Vt}^{(2)}_{2}
is rationally (at least)
(-1)
-connective.

Remark 1.5.1
.

The proof of
H
actually shows that, for
d\geq 4
, the unstable derivatives
\smash{\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(2)}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}
are rationally
(2d-2)
-connected but not rationally
(2d-1)
-connected. With this alone, we cannot conclude that
\smash{\Theta\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(2)}}
is not rationally
0
-connective, since this would also require understanding the structure maps
\smash{\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(2)}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\to\Omega^{2}\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(2)}(\mathbf{R}^{d+1})}
on
\smash{\pi_{2d-1}^{\mathbf{Q}}}
. Nevertheless, one can show that these homomorphisms for
d\geq 6

even
are injective on an infinite-dimensional subspace of the domain.

1.6
Strategy of the proof of
A

As mentioned at the beginning of this introduction, most of our results are consequences of
A
. We now outline the strategy of its proof. In the even-dimensional case
d=2n
, we follow the approach of
[
22
]
by studying a variant of the
Weiss fibre sequence
. More precisely, we look at a certain fibre sequence

\BDiffb(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1})\longrightarrow\BDiffb(X_{g})\longrightarrow\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{{\cong}}}(X_{g}),

(1.6.1)

or rather, the framed variant (
6.1.1
) of it, where
X_{g}
denotes the connected sum of
S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}
with
(S^{n}\times S^{n})^{\#g}
. This reduces the study of
\BDiffb(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1})
to that of the remaining spaces in this sequence. The homology of the total space can be completely understood for
g
large enough, as a consequence of the seminal work of Galatius and Randal-Williams
[
36
,
37
]
on stable moduli spaces of even-dimensional manifolds. Our main contribution lies in the computation of the homology of the base space in a range doubling the one obtained in
[
22
]
. The main novelty of our approach in this even-dimensional case is to use the work of the second named author
[
66
]
to simplify the analysis of this embedding space by using
trace methods
.

Remark 1.6.1

(On embedding calculus)
.

The approach proposed in
[
22
]
is analogous to the highly successful one of Kupers–Randal-Williams
[
60
]
(building on insights of Weiss
[
78
]
) for computing the rational homotopy groups of
\BDiffb(D^{2n})
. In
[
60
]
, the authors study the corresponding embedding space via
embedding calculus
. Although a similar strategy could in principle be applied in our setting, we encountered significant computational obstacles in carrying it out. The main difficulty is the ubiquitous appearance of infinitely generated
\mathbf{Q}
-vector spaces, which makes it hard to analyse various differentials in the relevant spectral sequences. In the range of
A
, our approach avoids many of these obstacles.

Our strategy for the odd-dimensional case
d=2n+1
is analogous to the one above, and is inspired by the work of Krannich–Randal-Williams
[
55
]
. Given our knowledge of
\BDiffb(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1})
by the previous case, and in view of the fibre sequence (
1.1.1
), instead of studying
\BDiffb(S^{1}\times D^{2n})
directly, we focus on the concordance space
\BC(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1})
. This space features also in another
Weiss-type
fibre sequence

\BC(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1})\longrightarrow\BDiff_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g})\longrightarrow\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{{\cong}}}(Y_{g};X_{g}),

(1.6.2)

where
Y_{g}
denotes the boundary connected sum of
S^{1}\times D^{2n}
with the handlebody
(S^{n}\times D^{n+1})^{\natural g}
. In turn, the homology of the total space of this fibre sequence can be completely understood for
g
large enough, now as consequence of the work of first named author
[
63
]
on stable moduli spaces of odd-dimensional triads. We approach the base space of this sequence with the same method as above, relying on the results in
[
66
]
. This strategy allows us to deduce
B
for
d
even. By combining the fibre sequence (
1.1.1
) with the computations above, we can deduce both
A
and
B
for
d
odd.

Structure of the paper.
For the reader’s convenience, we provide a brief overview of the paper.

Section

2
introduces the notation, spaces, and constructions that will play a role throughout. In particular,
Section

2.3
studies the homotopy type of the space of homotopy automorphisms of the pair
(Y_{g};X_{g})
. We also recall the analysis of the automorphism space of
X_{g}
by Bustamante–Randal-Williams
[
22
, §3.1]
.

Sections
3
,
4
, and
5
study the self-embedding spaces of
X_{g}
and of the pair
(Y_{g};X_{g})
(more precisely, their stably framed versions) appearing in the Weiss fibre sequences (
1.6.1
) and (
1.6.2
). Section
3
focuses on their path-components, i.e. the associated mapping class groups. The case of
X_{g}
was analysed in
[
22
]
using surgery theory; in
Section

3.3.2
we adapt related methods, based on
[
57
]
, to treat the case of
(Y_{g};X_{g})
.

In
Section

4
we investigate the rational homotopy and homology type of the self-embedding space of
X_{g}
in degrees up to roughly
2n-2
. We begin by analysing its higher rational homotopy groups as representations of the mapping class group, using a new approach based on trace methods and the embedding Weiss–Williams theorem of
[
66
]
. An overview of this approach is given in
Section

4.1
, which we expect may be of independent interest. In
Section

4.6
we then use this analysis to compute the homology of the embedding space. Section
5
carries out the analogous analysis for the self-embedding space of the pair
(Y_{g};X_{g})
.

In
Section

6
we prove all of the main theorems stated in the introduction, along with several related results.
Appendix

A
addresses some technicalities in the embedding Weiss–Williams approach of
Section

4
.

4.4.1
and
D
, grew out of illuminating conversations with him. Oscar Randal-Williams suggested to SME the application of
[

, Thm. A]
to the study of diffeomorphisms of solid tori, pointed out a mistake related to

6.8.2

2
Preliminaries

Notation 2.0.1
.

Let
n\geq 3
and
g\geq 0
be integers. We consider the following families of manifolds.

(i)

W_{g,1}
is the
g
-fold boundary connected sum of
S^{n}\times S^{n}\backslash\int(D^{2n})
, where
D^{2n}
is an embedded
2n
-disc. We consider
W_{g,1}
as a triad with the decomposition
\partial_{0}W_{g,1}\coloneq D^{2n-1}_{+}
and
\partial_{1}W_{g,1}\coloneq D^{2n-1}_{-}
of its boundary
\partial D^{2n}
. Here,
D^{2n-1}_{+}
stands for the subspace of those tuples
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\partial D^{2n}
where
x_{2n}\geq 0
and
D^{2n-1}_{-}
for the subspace of those tuples where
x_{n}\leq 0
.

(ii)

V_{g}
is the
g
-fold boundary connected sum of
S^{n}\times D^{n+1}.
Its boundary is the
g
-fold connected sum of
S^{n}\times S^{n}
. Notice that
W_{g,1}
embedds into
\partial V_{g}
and its complement is a
2n
-disc
D^{2n}.
We consider
V_{g}
as a
4
-ad with the decomposition
\partial_{0}V_{g}\coloneq D^{2n}_{+}
,
\partial_{1}V_{g}\coloneq D^{2n}_{-}
and
\partial_{2}V_{g}\coloneq W_{g,1}
. Here
D^{2n}_{+}
stands for the subspace of
D^{2n}
of those tuples
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in D^{2n}
where
x_{2n}\geq 0
and
D^{2n-1}_{-}
for the subspace of those tuples where
x_{n}\leq 0.
Denote by
\partial_{ij}V_{g}
the intersection of
\partial_{i}V_{g}
and
\partial_{j}V_{g}
for
i,j\in\{0,1,2\}
and
\partial_{012}V_{g}
for the common intersection
\partial_{0}V_{g}\cap\partial_{1}V_{g}\cap\partial_{2}V_{g}.

(iii)

X_{g}
is the boundary connected sum of
S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}
with
W_{g,1}.
We consider
X_{g}
as a triad with the following decomposition of its boundary
\partial(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1})\#\partial D^{2n}
:
\partial_{0}X_{g}\coloneq(S^{1}\times D^{2n-2}_{+})\natural\partial_{0}W_{g,1}
and
\partial_{1}X_{g}\coloneq(S^{1}\times D^{2n-2}_{-})\natural\partial_{1}W_{g,1}
. The boundary connected sum above is assumed to be at a point in
S^{1}\times S^{2n-3}=(S^{1}\times D^{2n-2}_{+})\cap(S^{1}\times D^{2n-2}_{-}).

(iv)

Y_{g}
is the boundary connected sum of
S^{1}\times D^{2n}
with
V_{g}.
Note that
X_{g}
embeds into
\partial(Y_{g})
with complement diffeomorphic to
S^{1}\times\operatorname{int}D^{2n-1}
, by seeing
S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}
as a subspace of the boundary of
S^{1}\times D^{2n}
given by the upper hemisphere
D^{2n-1}_{+}
in the
D^{2n}
-coordinate. We consider
Y_{g}
as a
4
-ad with the following decomposition
\partial_{0}Y_{g}\coloneq S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}_{+}
,
\partial_{1}Y_{g}\coloneq S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}_{-}
and
\partial_{2}Y_{g}\coloneq X_{g}
. Similarly, denote the subspaces
\partial_{ij}Y_{g}
and
\partial_{012}Y_{g}
defined in the same way.

By definition, we have an embedding of triads
\iota:(W_{g,1};\partial_{0}W_{g,1},\partial_{1}W_{g,1})\hookrightarrow(X_{g};\partial_{0}X_{g},\partial_{1}X_{g})
and an embedding of
4
-ads
\hat{\iota}:(V_{g};\partial_{0}V_{g},\partial_{1}V_{g},\partial_{2}V_{g})\hookrightarrow(Y_{g};\partial_{0}Y_{g},\partial_{1}Y_{g},\partial_{2}Y_{g})
extending
\iota
on
\partial_{2}.
We fix a basepoint
x\in\partial_{01}(W_{g,1})
which induces basepoints on
X_{g},V_{g}
and
Y_{g}
. As mentioned in
[
22
, Section 2.1]
, the inclusion
S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}\subset D^{2n}
induces an embedding of triads
\kappa:(X_{g};\partial_{0}X_{g},\partial_{1}X_{g})\hookrightarrow(W_{g,1};\partial_{0}W_{g,1},\partial_{1}W_{g,1})
such that
\kappa\circ\iota
is isotopic to the identity. Similarly,
\kappa
extends to an embedding of
4
-ads
\hat{\kappa}:(Y_{g};\partial_{0}Y_{g},\partial_{1}Y_{g},\partial_{2}Y_{g})\hookrightarrow(V_{g};\partial_{0}V_{g},\partial_{1}V_{g},\partial_{2}V_{g})
and
\hat{\kappa}\circ\hat{\iota}
is isotopic to the identity.

2.1
Embedding and automorphism spaces

Let
(M;\partial_{0}M,\partial_{1}M)
be a compact smooth manifold triad, we denote by
\Embo(M)
the space of self-embeddings of
M
extend the identity map on a neighbourhood of
\partial_{0}
(but might send
\partial_{1}
to the interior of
M
), equipped with the
C^{\infty}
-topology. Composition gives a (topological) monoid structure to this space. As mentioned in
[
21
, Section 5.1]
, we have a fibre sequence, usually called the
Weiss fibre sequence
,

\BDiffb(\partial_{1}M\times[0,1])\to\BDiffb(M)\to\BEmbop(M)

(2.1.1)

which deloops once to the right, where
\BEmbop(M)
denotes the classifying space of the group-like submonoid
\Embop(M)
of those path components of
\Embo(M)
, which are hit from
\Diffb(M).
Denote also by
\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}_{\partial}(M)
and by
\bEmbo(M)
for the (realisation of the simplicial) spaces of block diffeomorphisms and self-embeddings of
M
, and by
\smash{\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\cong}_{\partial_{0}}}(M)
the union of all path components hit by
\bDiffb(M)
(see
[
57
, Section 1.4]
for definition of block diffeomorphisms and embeddings) Finally, we denote by
\Aut_{\partial}(M)
and
\Aut_{\partial_{0}}(M)
for the group-like monoids of self homotopy equivalences of
M
fixed in
\partial M
or
\partial_{0}
, respectively. As observed in
[
57
, Section 1.5]
, the map
\Diffb(M)\to\Aut_{\partial}(M)
factors through
\bDiffb(M)
up to homotopy, since the analogous map
\Aut_{\partial}(M)\to\widetilde{\aut}_{\partial}(M)
is a weak equivalence.

2.1.1
4
-ads

Similarly, for a compact smooth manifold
4
-ad
(N;\partial_{0}N,\partial_{1}N,\partial_{2}N)
, we denote by
\Embo(N;\partial_{2}N)
the space of self-embeddings of
N
which are the identity on a neighbourhood of
\partial_{0}
and fix
\partial_{2}N
setwise (but once again,
\partial_{1}N
is allowed to go to the interior of
N
, as long as
\partial_{12}N
goes to the interior of
\partial_{2}N
). Denote the topological group of diffeomorphisms of
N
which are the identity on
\partial^{v}N\coloneq\partial_{0}N\cup\partial_{1}N
by
\Diff_{\partial^{v}}(N).
As pointed out in
[
55
, Section 2.3]
, there exists an analogous
"Weiss fibre sequence"
of the form

\BDiff_{\partial^{v}}(\partial_{1}N\times[0,1])\to\BDiff_{\partial^{v}}(N)\to\BEmbop(N;\partial_{2}N)

(2.1.2)

where
\partial^{v}(\partial_{1}N\times[0,1])\coloneq\partial_{1}N\times\{0\}\cup_{\partial_{01}N\times[0,1]}\partial_{1}N\times\{1\}
, which deloops once. Here,
\Embop(N;\partial_{2}N)
denotes the path components of
\Embo(N;\partial_{2}N)
hit by
\Diff_{\partial^{v}}(N).
The sequences (
2.1.1
) and (
2.1.2
) are compatible along the maps that restrict to
\partial_{2}N.
Denote the block variants by
\smash{\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}_{\partial^{v}}}(N)
,
\bEmbo(N;\partial_{2}N)
and
\smash{\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\cong}_{\partial_{0}}}(N;\partial_{2}N)
, defined in the same way as before. Denote by
\Aut_{\partial^{v}}(N;\partial_{2}N)
the space of self-homotopy equivalences of the pair
(N,\partial_{2}N)
fixed in
\partial^{v}N
, with the compact-open topology.

2.1.2
Stable framings

Consider the map
\mathrm{sfr}_{d}:\oo(d)\to\BO(d)
given by the homotopy fibre of
\BO(d)\to\BO
. A
stable framing
on a
d
-dimensional smooth manifold is a fibrewise isomorphism
\ell:TM\cong(\mathrm{sfr}_{d})^{*}\gamma_{d},
where
\gamma_{d}
denotes the universal
d
-dimensional vector bundle over
\BO(d)
. If
K\subset\partial M
is a codimension
0
submanifold, a stable framing
\ell
on
M
induces a fibrewise isomorphism
\ell|_{K}:TK\times[0,1]\cong TM|_{K}\cong(\mathrm{sfr}_{d})^{*}\gamma_{d},
which induces an (essentially unique) stable framing
\ell_{K}:TK\cong(\mathrm{sfr}_{d-1})^{*}\gamma_{d-1}
on
K
: this follows by the fact that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oo(d-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BO(d-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oo(d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BO(d)}

is homotopy cartesian. Given a
d
-dimensional triad
(M;\partial_{0}M,\partial_{1}M)
and a stable framing
\ell_{\partial}
of
\partial M
, the spaces
\Diffb(M)
and
\bDiffb(M)
act (in the
A_{\infty}
sense) on the space
\Bun_{\partial}(TM,(\mathrm{sfr}_{d})^{*}\gamma_{d};\ell_{\partial})
, since the structure
\mathrm{sfr}_{d}
is
stable
in the sense of
[
57
, Section 1.8.1]
. We denote their homotopy quotients by
\BDiffsfr(M;\ell_{\partial})
and
\BbDiffsfr(M;\ell_{\partial})
, respectively. Similarly, the spaces
\Embop(M)
and
\smash{\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\cong}_{\partial_{0}}}(M)
act on
\Bun_{\partial_{0}}(TM,(\mathrm{sfr}_{d})^{*}\gamma_{d};\ell_{0})
given a stable framing
\ell_{0}
on
\partial_{0}M
. We denote the respective homotopy quotients by
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(M)
and
\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(M).
Similarly, for
d
-dimensional
4
-ad
(N;\partial_{0}N,\partial_{1}N,\partial_{2}N)
, we denote the homotopy quotients
\BDiff^{\mathrm{sfr}}_{\partial^{v}}(N;\ell_{v})
and
\smash{\BbDiff^{\mathrm{sfr}}_{\partial^{v}}}(N;\ell_{v})
of the actions of
\Diff_{\partial^{v}}(N)
and
\smash{\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}_{\partial^{v}}}(N)
on
\Bun_{\partial^{v}}(TN,(\mathrm{sfr}_{d})^{*}\gamma_{d};\ell_{v}).
Denote the homotopy quotients
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(N;\partial_{2}N;\ell_{0})
and
\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(N;\partial_{2}N;\ell_{0})
of the actions of
\Embop(N;\partial_{2}N)
and
\smash{\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\cong}_{\partial_{0}}}(N;\partial_{2}N)
on
\Bun_{\partial_{0}}(TN,(\mathrm{sfr}_{d})^{*}\gamma_{d};\ell_{0}).
As mentioned in
[
55
]
, we have a map of fibre sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BDiff^{\mathrm{sfr}}_{\partial^{v}}(\partial_{1}N\times[0,1];\ell_{v})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BDiff^{\mathrm{sfr}}_{\partial^{v}}(N;\ell_{v})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(N;\partial_{2}N;\ell_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BDiffsfr(\partial_{12}N\times[0,1];\ell_{v})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BDiffsfr(\partial_{2}N,\ell_{v})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(\partial_{2}N,\ell_{0})}

(2.1.3)

with compatible deloopings once to the right, given a stable framing
\ell_{v}
on
\partial^{v}N
.

We now specify to the
4
-ad
Y_{g}.
Start by observing that
\partial(\partial_{1}Y_{g}\times[0,1])
is diffeomorphic to the union
S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}_{-}\cup_{S^{1}\times D^{2n-2}}S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}_{+},
which is diffeomorphic to
S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}.
Thus, the space
\Diff_{\partial^{v}}(\partial_{1}Y_{g}\times[0,1])
is equivalent to the space
\Diff_{S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}}(S^{1}\times D^{2n})
and thus equivalent to the concordance space
\conc_{\partial}(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}).
Moreover, since the map
\partial^{v}(S^{1}\times D^{2n})\to S^{1}\times D^{2n}
is a homotopy equivalence, forgetting stable framings

\BDiff^{\mathrm{sfr}}_{\partial^{v}}(S^{1}\times D^{2n};\ell_{v})\to\BDiff_{\partial^{v}}(S^{1}\times D^{2n})

is an equivalence, as the fibre is equivalent to
\Bun_{\partial^{v}}(T(S^{1}\times D^{2n}),(\mathrm{sfr}_{2n+1})^{*}\gamma_{2n+1},\ell_{v})
is contractible.

2.1.3
Block embeddings spaces of
X_{g}
and
Y_{g}

In this section, we combine results of
[
22
,
57
]
to describe the rational homotopy type of the block embedding spaces defined above in terms of certain spaces of homotopy automorphisms. Consider the block version of the sequence (
2.1.2
) for
N=Y_{g}.
Observe that the block concordance space
\smash{\BbDiff_{\partial^{v}}}(S^{1}\times D^{2n})
is contractible, since
\BbDiffb(S^{1}\times D^{2n})\simeq\Omega\BbDiffb(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}).
Thus, we have an equivalence
\smash{\BbDiff_{\partial^{v}}}(Y_{g})\simeq\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\cong}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g}),
and thus we get a map

\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\cong}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})\to\BAut_{\partial^{v}}^{\cong}(Y_{g};X_{g})

(2.1.4)

to the classifying space of the group-like submonoid of those path components of
\Aut_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g};X_{g})
hit by
\smash{\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}_{\partial^{v}}}(Y_{g}).
Moreover, in
[
22
, 1650]
the authors explain how to get an analogous map

\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\cong}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\to\BAutbp(X_{g})

(2.1.5)

using that
\Aut_{\partial}(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1})
is contractible. Moreover, the maps (
2.1.4
) and (
2.1.5
) are compatible with respect to restricting from
Y_{g}
to
X_{g}.
Let
\ell
be a stable framing on
Y_{g}
, we use the subscript
(-)_{\ell}
for the path component of
\ell
in
\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g};\ell_{0})
and
\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g};\ell_{0})
, where
l_{0}
denotes the restriction of
\ell
to
\partial_{0}
.

Proposition 2.1.1
.

For all
n\geq 3
and
\ell
a stable framing on
Y_{g}
, the homotopy fibres of the horizontal maps induced by (
2.1.4
) and (
2.1.5
) in the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g};\ell_{0})_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BAut_{\partial^{v}}^{\cong}(Y_{g};X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g};\ell_{0}|_{X_{g}})_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BAutbp(X_{g})}

are nilpotent and have finite homotopy groups, and thus are rationally contractible.

Proof.

We start by proving the claim for the upper horizontal map. Since
\smash{\BbDiff_{\partial^{v}}}(S^{1}\times D^{2n})\simeq\smash{\BbDiff^{\mathrm{sfr}}_{\partial^{v}}}(S^{1}\times D^{2n})
is contractible, we see that the map

\smash{\BbDiff^{\mathrm{sfr}}_{\partial^{v}}}(Y_{g};l_{0})_{\ell}\to\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell}

is a weak equivalence. The claim now follows from
[
57
, Cor. 2.4]
, since
X_{g}\hookrightarrow Y_{g}
induces an equivalence of fundamental groupoids. By
[
22
, Cor. 6.2]
, the homotopy fibre of the lower horizontal map is equivalent to
\Map_{*}(W_{g,1},\mathrm{G})_{J}
, where
\mathrm{G}\coloneq\hocolim_{n}\Aut_{*}(S^{n})
and
J
are the union of components of maps
W_{g,1}\to\mathrm{G}
which factor up to homotopy through the
J
-homomorphism
J:\mathrm{O}\to\mathrm{G}
. Since
W_{g,1}
is equivalent to a wedge of
n
-spheres, this mapping space is a product of
n
-fold loop spaces and thus nilpotent. Moreover, its homotopy groups are
2g
-fold products of shifts of homotopy groups of
\mathrm{G}
. These identify with certain stable homotopy groups of spheres, which are finite by Serre.
∎

2.2
Quadratic modules

Let
\pi\coloneq\pi_{1}(X_{g},x)
and
\bar{(-)}:\mathbf{Z}[\pi]\to\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
be the ring involution induced by
\bar{g}=g^{-1}
, for
g\in\pi
.

Convention 2.2.1
.

By
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
-module
, we mean a left
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
-module, and we see any such module
M
as a right module by setting
x\cdot a\coloneq\bar{a}\cdot x
, and vice-versa. Given a module
M
, we define its dual
M^{\vee}
to be the right
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
-module of left
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
-module maps
\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}(M,\mathbf{Z}[\pi])
, where the right module structure on this abelian group is given by
\alpha\cdot r(m)\coloneq\alpha(m)r.
Given another module
N
, we can form the tensor product
M^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}N\coloneq M^{\vee}\otimes_{\pi}N
, which we see as an abelian group, and define the canonical map of abelian groups

M^{\vee}\otimes_{\pi}N\to\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}(M,N)
that takes
\alpha\otimes n
to the map
(m\mapsto\alpha(m)\cdot n).
This map is an isomorphism, whenever
M
is a finitely generated projective
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
-module.

Notation 2.2.2
.

We now set the notation of various modules that we will consider throughout the entire paper. First, consider the following
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
and
\mathbf{Q}[\pi]
-modules.

\displaystyle\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\coloneq\pi_{n}(X_{g})\qquad\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\coloneq\pi_{n}(Y_{g})\qquad\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\coloneq\pi_{n+1}(Y_{g},X_{g})\cong\ker(\iota_{*}:\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})

\displaystyle\smash{H_{X_{g}}}\coloneq\pi_{n}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g})\qquad\smash{H_{Y_{g}}}\coloneq\pi_{n}^{\mathbf{Q}}(Y_{g})\qquad\smash{K_{X_{g}}}\coloneq\pi_{n+1}^{\mathbf{Q}}(Y_{g};X_{g})\cong\ker(\iota_{*}:\smash{H_{X_{g}}}\to\smash{H_{Y_{g}}})

where
\iota_{*}
is the map on (rational) homotopy groups of the inclusion
X_{g}\hookrightarrow Y_{g}
. We also set the analogous notation for the pair
(V_{g};W_{g,1})
, similar to the one in
[
55
, Section 2.5]
.

\displaystyle\smash{H_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\coloneq\H_{n}(W_{g,1};\mathbf{Z})\qquad\smash{H_{V_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\coloneq\H_{n}(V_{g};\mathbf{Z})\qquad\smash{K_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\coloneq\H_{n+1}(V_{g},W_{g,1};\mathbf{Z})\cong\ker(\iota_{*}:\smash{H_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to\smash{H_{V_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})

\displaystyle\smash{H_{W_{g}}}\coloneq\H_{n}(W_{g,1};\mathbf{Q})\qquad\smash{H_{V_{g}}}\coloneq\H_{n}(V_{g};\mathbf{Q})\qquad\smash{K_{W_{g}}}\coloneq\H_{n+1}(V_{g},W_{g,1};\mathbf{Q})\cong\ker(\iota_{*}:\smash{H_{W_{g}}}\to\smash{H_{V_{g}}})

Observe that the pair
(Y_{g};X_{g})
is equivalent to the pair
(S^{1}\vee V_{g},S^{1}\vee W_{g,1})
, from which we see that the first (resp. second) row of the notation for
(Y_{g};X_{g})
, as
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
-modules, is obtained from the first (resp. second) row of the notation of
(V_{g};W_{g,1})
by applying
(-)\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
(resp.
(-)\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbf{Q}[\pi]
). Since
\iota:W_{g,1}\simeq\bigvee_{2g}S^{n}\hookrightarrow V_{g}\simeq\bigvee_{g}S^{n}
is given by collapsing the summands represented by the spheres
\{*\}\times S^{n}\subset W_{g,1}
, the inclusion of the remaining summands defines a homotopy section of
\iota.
Thus, we have splittings
\smash{\smash{H_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\cong\smash{H_{V_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\oplus\smash{K_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}}
as
\mathbf{Z}
-modules and
\smash{\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\cong\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\oplus\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}}
as
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
-modules, and also analogous splittings for the rationalised versions. Finally, we fix the
standard basis
of
\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}

\{a_{i},b_{i}\}_{i=1}^{g}
, where
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
are the classes representing, respectively, the inclusions of
S^{n}\times\{*\},\{*\}\times S^{n}\subset W_{1,1}=(S^{n}\times S^{n})\backslash\int(D^{2n})
of the
i
-th connected summand
W_{1,1}\subset X_{g}
. Note that
\{a_{i}\}_{i=1}^{g}
is a basis of the summand
\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
, and
\{b_{i}\}_{i=1}^{g}
of the summand
\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}.

In
[
22
, Section 2.2]
, the authors associate a quadratic module
(\smash{\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}},\lambda_{X},q_{X})
over
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
with form parameter
((-1)^{n},\Lambda_{n})
in the sense of Bak
[
3
]
, where
\Lambda_{n}
is either
\Lambda^{\text{min}}_{n}\coloneq\{a-(-1)^{n}\bar{a}:a\in\mathbf{Z}[\pi]\}
if
n\neq 3,7
, and
\langle\Lambda^{\text{min}},e\rangle
where
e\in\pi
is the identity element. By definition, this is the data of
\mathbf{Z}
-linear maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{X}:\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\otimes\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{X}:\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to\mathbf{Z}[\pi]/\Lambda_{n}}

such that, for every
x,y\in\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
and
a,b\in\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
, we have:

(i)

\lambda_{X}(a\cdot x,b\cdot y)=a\cdot\lambda_{X}(x,y)\cdot\bar{b},

(ii)

\overline{\lambda_{X}(x,y)}=(-1)^{n}\lambda_{X}(y,x),

(iii)

q_{X}(a\cdot x)=a\cdot q_{X}(x)\cdot\bar{a},
and

(iv)

q_{X}(x+y)-q_{X}(x)-q_{X}(y)=\lambda_{X}(x,y)\text{ mod }\Lambda_{n}.

In this case,
\lambda_{X}
is given by the equivariant intersection form on the universal cover
\widetilde{X}_{g}.
This quadratic module is
non-degenerate
, in the sense that, the right
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
-module map
\smash{\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})^{\vee}}
sending
x
to
\lambda_{X}(-,x)
is an isomorphism. Since
\pi_{1}(X_{g})\cong\pi_{1}(Y_{g}),
observe that
\smash{\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}}
and
\smash{\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}}
are isotropic subspaces with respect to
\lambda_{X},
that is,
q_{X}(x)=0
and
\lambda_{X}(x,y)=0
for
x,y
in
\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
or
\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
. Moreover, the duality map
\smash{\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})^{\vee}}
restricts to isomorphisms
\smash{\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to(\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})^{\vee}}
and
\smash{\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to(\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})^{\vee}}
of right
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
-modules.

Similarly, we have a quadratic module
(\smash{H_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\lambda_{W},q_{W})
over
\mathbf{Z}
with form parameter
((-1)^{n},\Lambda_{n})
where
\bar{(-)}:\mathbf{Z}\to\mathbf{Z}
is the identity and
\Lambda_{n}
is defined in the analogous way. Here, we have the isotropic subsets
\smash{K_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
and
\smash{H_{V_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
. We shall need the following improvement of
[
22
, Lemma 2.2]
.

Lemma 2.2.3
.

Let
n\geq 3
. An element
e\in\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
may be represented by a smooth embedding
\hat{e}:S^{n}\times D^{n}\hookrightarrow X_{g}
if and only if
q_{X}(e)=0
. Moreover, every element
f\in\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
may be represented by a smooth embedding of pairs
\hat{f}:(D^{n+1}\times D^{n},S^{n}\times D^{n})\hookrightarrow(Y_{g},X_{g})
. The analogous claim holds for
W_{g,1}
and
V_{g}.

Proof.

The first claim is a special case of
[
22
, Lemma 2.2]
, so we focus on the second claim. Let
f\in\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
be an element represented by a map of pairs
f:(D^{n+1},S^{n})\to(Y_{g};X_{g}).
By a result of Hudson
[
46
]
(best stated for our purposes in
[
12
, Thm. 5.13]
), the map
f
is homotopic to an embedding
f^{\prime}:(D^{n+1},S^{n})\hookrightarrow(Y_{g};X_{g})
since
n\geq 3
,
(D^{n+1},S^{n})
is
1
-connected, and
(Y_{g};X_{g})
is
2
-connected. Additionally, as
D^{n+1}
is contractible, the normal bundle of
f^{\prime}
is trivial, and hence,
f^{\prime}
may be extended to an embedding
\hat{f}:(D^{n+1}\times D^{n},S^{n}\times D^{n})\hookrightarrow(Y_{g},X_{g})
, by choosing a framing of the normal bundle (here we also use that the normal bundle of
f^{\prime}|_{S^{n}}
in
X_{g}
is the restriction of the normal bundle of
f^{\prime}
to
S^{n}
). The same proofs work verbatim for
W_{g,1}
and
V_{g}
.
∎

Denote the orthogonal groups of these quadratic modules by
\mathrm{U}_{g}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})
and
\mathrm{U}_{g}(\mathbf{Z},\Lambda_{n}).
The maps
\mathbf{Z}\to\mathbf{Z}[\pi]\to\mathbf{Z}
induced by the unique maps
0\to\mathbf{Z}\to 0
of groups induce maps
\mathrm{U}_{g}(\mathbf{Z},\Lambda_{n})\to\mathrm{U}_{g}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})\to\mathrm{U}_{g}(\mathbf{Z},\Lambda_{n}).
By naturality, self-embeddings of
X_{g}
fixing
\partial_{0}X_{g}
preserve this quadratic module. Moreover, if such self-embedding extends to
Y_{g}
, we conclude that the isotropic subspace
\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
is fixed set-wise. Denote by
\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})
the subgroup of
\mathrm{U}_{g}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})
consisting of those
A
such that
A(\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})\subset\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}.
Define
\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z},\Lambda_{n})
in the analogous way. From
Lemma

2.2.3
, we deduce the following consequence.

Lemma 2.2.4
.

Let
n\geq 3
. The horizontal maps of the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Embo(Y_{g};X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Embo(X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{U}_{g}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})}

are surjective. The analogous claim holds for
W_{g,1}
and
V_{g}.

Proof.

The surjectivity of the bottom map is the content of
[
22
, Prop. 5.2]
. We focus on the surjectivity of the top map. Let
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
denote the elements of the standard basis of
\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
from
2.2.2
. By definition, the classes
b_{i}
lie in
\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}.
Let
\rho\in\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})
. Since
\rho(b_{i})\in\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
, we can apply
Lemma

2.2.3
to represent this class by an embedding
\beta_{i}:(D^{n+1}\times D^{n},S^{n}\times D^{n})\to(Y_{g};X_{g})
. Moreover, we can isotope the embeddings
\beta_{i}
to be pairwise disjoint, by applying
[
12
, Thm. C.3]
, so we can assume that we have chosen
\beta_{i}
above pairwise disjoint. This result only applies to
n\geq 4
, but it was communicated to us by Manuel Krannich and Alexander Kupers that loc.cit. can be extended to dimension
7
, using different methods, and will appear as part of forthcoming work of these authors. On the other hand, by following the proof of
[
22
, Prop. 5.2]
, we can represent
\rho(a_{i})
by embeddings
\alpha_{i}:S^{n}\times D^{n}\hookrightarrow X_{g}
, which are pairwise disjoint, and such that
\alpha_{i}|_{S^{n}\times\{0\}}
and
\beta_{j}|_{S^{n}\times\{0\}}
intersect transversely at exactly
1
point if
i=j
, and are disjoint if
i\neq j.
By proceeding as loc.cit., plumbing assembles the embeddings
\alpha_{i}
and
\beta_{i}
into an embedding
e:(V_{g};W_{g,1})\hookrightarrow(Y_{g};X_{g})
which sends
(\partial_{0}V_{g},\partial_{02}V_{g})
to
(\partial_{0}Y_{g},\partial_{02}Y_{g})
. Hence, we can extend it to an element of
\Embo(Y_{g};X_{g})
, whose induced map on
n
-th homotopy groups is
\rho.

∎

Remark 2.2.5

(On dimension
7
)
.

In the proof above, we relied on an extension of
[
12
, Thm. C.3]
to dimension
7
that is not yet available. This is however the only step where this extension is used. If one does not assume it, one must assume additionally that
n\geq 4
or
d\geq 8
in
A
,
B
and
C
and Corollaries
D
–
H
. Additionally,
A
and
G
are also valid for
d=6
, without assuming this extension.

2.3
Homotopy automorphisms of
X_{g}
and
Y_{g}

In this subsection, we study the spaces of homotopy automorphisms of
X_{g}
and of the pair
(Y_{g};X_{g})
. The former was understood in
[
22
]
, whose results we recall in the first subsubsection. We study the homotopy type of the latter using similar methods as the ones from loc.cit. The results we obtain will be used both in
Section

3
to understand the group of components of certain embedding spaces (once again, the case of
X_{g}
is merely a recollection of the work of
[
22
]
), and in
Sections

4
and
5
to compute the higher rational homotopy groups of these embedding spaces.

2.3.1
The case of
X_{g}

In this section, we recall the main result of
[
22
, Section 3.1]
on the homotopy type of the space
\Aut_{\partial}(X_{g})
. The authors consider the following fibre sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{\partial}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{S^{1}}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{S^{1}}(S^{1}\times S^{2n-2},X_{g})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{res}_{X}}

(2.3.1)

given by restriction of maps, where
S^{1}\subset S^{1}\times S^{2n-2}
is given by the inclusion of
S^{1}\times\{*\}
induced by a fixed basepoint of
S^{2n-2}.
We extract the following result from the main result Theorem
3.1
of loc.cit.

Proposition 2.3.1

(
[
22
]
)
.

The following statements hold for
k\geq 0:

(i)

There is an isomorphism of
\mathbf{Z}[\pi_{0}(\Aut_{S^{1}}(X_{g}))]
-modules

\pi_{k}(\Map_{S^{1}}(X_{g}),\id)\cong\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\pi_{k+n}(X_{g}))

where
\pi_{0}(\Aut_{S^{1}}(X_{g}))
acts on the left group using the conjugation action of
\Aut_{S^{1}}(X_{g})
on
\Map_{S^{1}}(X_{g})
and on the right group by post-composition with the induced map on
\pi_{k+n}(X_{g})
and pre-composition by the induced map on
\pi_{n}(X_{g})
.

(ii)

There is an isomorphism of abelian groups

\pi_{k}(\Map_{S^{1}}(S^{1}\times S^{2n-2},X_{g}),\iota)\cong[\pi_{k+2n-1}(X_{g})]_{\pi}

where
\iota:S^{1}\times S^{2n-2}\hookrightarrow X_{g}
denotes the inclusion and
[\pi_{k+2n-1}(X_{g})]_{\pi}
denotes the coinvariants of the action of
\pi=\pi_{1}(X_{g})
on
\pi_{k+2n-1}(X_{g})
.

(iii)

After applying
(-)\otimes\mathbf{Q}
, the map
\pi_{k}(\mathrm{res}_{X})
is surjective for every
k>0.
In particular, we have an isomorphism
\pi_{k}(\Map_{\partial}(X_{g}),\id)\otimes\mathbf{Q}\cong\ker(\pi_{k}(\mathrm{res}_{X})\otimes\mathbf{Q}).

Proof.

The first two claims are precisely the first two isomorphisms of
[
22
, Thm. 3.1]
(the equivariance claim in (i) follows directly from the construction of the isomorphism in the proof of loc.cit), so we focus on the third claim. We briefly recall the description of
\pi_{k}(\mathrm{res}_{X})
from loc.cit. The quadratic pairing on
\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
is non-degenerate and hence we have the duality isomorphism of
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
-modules
\smash{\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})^{\vee}}
given by taking
x
to
\lambda_{X}(-,x)
. Under the canonical isomorphism

\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\otimes_{\pi}\pi_{k+n}(X_{g})\to(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})^{\vee}\otimes_{\pi}\pi_{k+n}(X_{g})\to\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\pi_{n+k}(X_{g})),

induced by the duality isomorphism and the canonical map in
2.2.1
(where we use that
\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
is free and finitely generated), and the isomorphisms of (i) and (ii), the map
\pi_{k}(\mathrm{res}_{X})
agrees with the map
[-,-]:\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\otimes_{\pi}\pi_{k+n}(X_{g})\to[\pi_{k+2n-1}(X_{g})]_{\pi},
given by taking
x\otimes y
to the Whitehead product
[y,x]
in
X_{g}
. We now show that
[-,-]
is surjective after rationalising. Since the universal cover of
X_{g}
is a wedge of spheres
\smash{\bigvee_{\mathbf{Z}}\bigvee_{i=1}^{2g}S^{n}}
, the rational homotopy groups
\smash{\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g})}
in degrees
*\geq 2
are given by
(*-1)
-graded piece of the free graded Lie algebra
\mathrm{Lie}(\smash{H_{X_{g}}})
over
\mathbf{Q}
, where
\smash{H_{X_{g}}}
is seen as a graded vector space concentrated in degree
n-1
. Additionally, the Whitehead product on
X_{g}
corresponds to the bracket on this free Lie algebra. Our claim now follows from the following classical fact about Lie algebras: Let
V
be a graded vector space, then the bracket map
[-,-]:V\otimes\mathrm{Lie}(V)\to\mathrm{Lie}^{\geq 2}(V)
is surjective, where the target is the subspace of decomposable elements.
∎

2.3.2
The case of
(Y_{g};X_{g})

In this section, we analyse the homotopy groups of the space
\Aut_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g};X_{g})
of self-homotopy equivalences of the pair
(Y_{g},X_{g})
which fix the pair
(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}_{+},S^{1}\times S^{2n-2})
pointwise. This is essentially analogous to
[
22
, Section 3]
. We have a fibre sequence of the following form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g};X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{S^{1}}(Y_{g};X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{S^{1}}((S^{1}\times D^{2n-1},S^{1}\times S^{2n-2}),(Y_{g},X_{g}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{res}_{Y}}

(2.3.2)

given by restriction of maps. Here, the leftmost space is the endomorphism space of the pair
(Y_{g},X_{g})
under
(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1},S^{1}\times S^{2n-2})
, the middle space is the endomorphism space of
(Y_{g},X_{g})
under
(S^{1},S^{1})
and the rightmost space is the mapping space of pairs under
(S^{1},S^{1}),
where the inclusion of
(S^{1},S^{1})
in
(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1},S^{1}\times S^{2n-2})
is induced by a fixed basepoint of
S^{2n-2}.

Proposition 2.3.2
.

The following statements hold for
k\geq 0:

(i)

There is an isomorphism of abelian groups

\pi_{k}(\Map_{S^{1}}(Y_{g};X_{g}),\id)\cong\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\pi_{k+n}(X_{g}))

where
(-)^{\mathrm{ext}}
denotes the subgroup of those maps taking
\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
to the subgroup
\pi_{k+n+1}(Y_{g},X_{g})\subseteq\pi_{k+n}(X_{g}).

(ii)

There is an isomorphism of abelian groups

\pi_{k}(\Map_{S^{1}}((S^{1}\times D^{2n-1},S^{1}\times S^{2n-2}),(Y_{g},X_{g})),\iota)\cong[\pi_{k+2n}(Y_{g},X_{g})]_{\pi}

where
\iota:(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1},S^{1}\times S^{2n-2})\hookrightarrow(Y_{g},X_{g})
denotes the inclusion and
[\pi_{k+2n}(Y_{g},X_{g})]_{\pi}
denotes the coinvariants of the action of
\pi=\pi_{1}(X_{g})
on
\pi_{k+2n}(Y_{g},X_{g})
.

(iii)

After applying
(-)\otimes\mathbf{Q}
, the map
\pi_{k}(\mathrm{res}_{Y})
is surjective for every
k>0.
In particular, we have an isomorphism
\pi_{k}(\Map_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g};X_{g}),\id)\otimes\mathbf{Q}\cong\ker(\pi_{k}(\mathrm{res}_{Y})\otimes\mathbf{Q}).

Under these isomorphisms, the maps induced by restriction
\pi_{k}(\Map_{S^{1}}(Y_{g};X_{g}),\id)\to\pi_{k}(\Map_{S^{1}}(X_{g}),\id)
and
\pi_{k}(\Map_{S^{1}}((S^{1}\times D^{2n-1},S^{1}\times S^{2n-2}),(Y_{g},X_{g})),\iota)\to\pi_{k}(\Map_{S^{1}}(S^{1}\times S^{2n-2},X_{g}),\iota)
agree with the inclusions, induced by the splittings
\pi_{*}(X_{g})\cong\pi_{*}(Y_{g})\oplus\pi_{*+1}(Y_{g},X_{g})
,

\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\pi_{k+n}(X_{g}))\hookrightarrow\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\pi_{k+n}(X_{g}))

and
[\pi_{k+2n}(Y_{g},X_{g})]_{\pi}\hookrightarrow[\pi_{k+2n-1}(X_{g})]_{\pi}
, respectively.

Proof.

We start by proving the first claim, which is essentially a relative version of the analogous claim in
[
22
, Thm 3.1]
. Start by observing the there is an equivalence
\Map_{S^{1}}(Y_{g};X_{g})\simeq\Map_{*}((V_{g};W_{g,1}),(Y_{g};X_{g}))
by restricting, where the latter space is the space of based maps of pairs. We can observe that the pair
(V_{g};W_{g,1})
is equivalent to the wedge sum

\bigvee_{\mathrm{rk}(\H_{n+1}(V_{g},W_{g,1}))}(D^{n+1},S^{n})\vee\bigvee_{\mathrm{rk}(\H_{n}(V_{g}))}(S^{n},S^{n})

of pairs. Hence, by taking homotopy groups at the constant map, we have an isomorphism

\pi_{k}(\Map_{*}((V_{g};W_{g,1}),(Y_{g};X_{g})),\mathrm{const})\cong\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}}(\smash{K_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\pi_{n+1+k}(Y_{g},X_{g}))\oplus\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}}(\smash{H_{V_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\pi_{n+k}(X_{g})),

using the following two facts, for any based pair of spaces
(X,X^{\prime})
:

1.

We have an isomorphism
\pi_{k}(\Map_{*}((D^{n+1},S^{n}),(X,X^{\prime}),\mathrm{const})\cong\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}}(\H_{n+1}(D^{n+1},S^{n}),\pi_{n+k}(X,X^{\prime}))
.

2.

The restriction map
\Map_{*}((S^{n},S^{n}),(X,X^{\prime}))\to\Map_{*}(S^{n},X^{\prime})
is an equivalence, and hence the homotopy group in degree
k
of either of the spaces is isomorphic to
\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}}(\H_{n}(S^{n}),\pi_{n+k}(X^{\prime})).

To compute the homotopy groups of the space
\Map_{*}((V_{g};W_{g,1}),(Y_{g};X_{g}))
based at the inclusion map, one observes that, using the wedge summand decomposition of above, the pair
(V_{g};W_{g,1})
admits a co-
H
-space structure, i.e. it is a comonoid in the homotopy category of based pairs of spaces, which implies that the mapping space above is a group-like
H
-space, and hence all its path components are equivalent. Now, using the isomorphisms
\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\cong\smash{K_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
and
\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\cong\smash{H_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{Z}[\pi],
we deduce the first claim from the isomorphism of abelian groups
\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}}(A,B)\cong\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}(A\otimes\mathbf{Z}[\pi],B)
, which holds for any abelian group
A
and
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
-module
B
. Moreover, one checks that the restriction map
\pi_{k}(\Map_{S^{1}}(Y_{g};X_{g}),\id)\to\pi_{k}(\Map_{S^{1}}(X_{g}),\id)
agrees with the inclusion map in the statement, since it is given by restricting the maps of pairs to the source of the pair.

The second claim follows analogously to the analogous claim in loc.cit., using the pushout square of pairs

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(D^{2n-1},S^{2n-2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(D^{2n},D^{2n-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{1}\vee D^{2n-1},S^{1}\vee S^{2n-2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1},S^{1}\times S^{2n-2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

where
\alpha
is a map of pairs representing the unique homotopy class
[\alpha]\in\pi_{2n-1}(S^{1}\vee D^{2n-1},S^{1}\times S^{2n-2})
mapping to the Whitehead product
[\iota_{1},\iota_{2n-2}]\in\pi_{2n-2}(S^{1}\vee S^{2n-2})
of the inclusion maps
\iota_{1}:S^{1}\hookrightarrow S^{1}\vee S^{2n-2}
and
\iota_{2n-2}:S^{2n-2}\hookrightarrow S^{1}\vee S^{2n-2}
under the connecting map of the long exact sequence of the pair
(S^{1}\vee D^{2n-1},S^{1}\vee S^{2n-2}).
We leave the check to the reader. One also checks that the restriction map
\pi_{k}(\Map_{S^{1}}((S^{1}\times D^{2n-1},S^{1}\times S^{2n-2}),(Y_{g},X_{g})),\iota)\to\pi_{k}(\Map_{S^{1}}(S^{1}\times S^{2n-2},X_{g}),\iota)
agrees with the inclusion
[\pi_{k+2n}(Y_{g},X_{g})]_{\pi}\hookrightarrow[\pi_{k+2n-1}(X_{g})]_{\pi}
.

We move now to the third claim. To do so, we consider the following commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom^{\mathrm{ext}}_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\pi_{k+n}(X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\pi_{k+2n}(Y_{g},X_{g})\right]_{\pi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\pi_{k+n}(X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\pi_{k+2n-1}(X_{g})\right]_{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k}(\mathrm{res}_{Y})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k}(\mathrm{res}_{X})}

and proceed in the following way: First, we identify the images of the vertical maps after rationalisation and second, we show that the image of the left map is hit by the image of the right map under the bottom map. The proof of the first step will hinge on the following two classical facts about free graded Lie algebras: Let
V,A,B
be graded
\mathbf{Q}
-vector spaces:

1.

As a
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space,
\mathrm{Lie}(V)
is generated by right bracketed words
[v_{1},[v_{2},[\cdots[v_{k-1},v_{k}]\cdots]]]
for
v_{1},\cdots,v_{k}\in V
. This follows by inductively using the Jacobi identity.

2.

The kernel of the projection map
\mathrm{Lie}(A\oplus B)\to\mathrm{Lie}(A)
is generated by right bracketed as above, for
V=A\oplus B
, where at least one of the entries is an element of
B.
This follows by the following argument: Any element
x
in the kernel can be written as a sum of right bracketed words, as in
1
, where
v_{i}
is either in
A
or
B
. The image of such a sum in
\mathrm{Lie}(A)
is the sum of those words where
v_{i}\in A
for all
i
. Since
x
is in the kernel, the latter sum vanishes in
\mathrm{Lie}(A)
and hence it also vanishes in
\mathrm{Lie}(A\oplus B)
(since the map
\mathrm{Lie}(A)\to\mathrm{Lie}(A\oplus B)
is a section of the projection, and hence injective). Thus, each word present in the sum decomposition of
x
must contain at least on element in
B
.

We start by identifying the images of the vertical maps in the square above. For the left map, we identify this image under the identification
\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\otimes_{\pi}\pi_{k+n}(X_{g})\cong\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\pi_{k+n}(X_{g})).
By seeing
\Hom^{\mathrm{ext}}_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\pi_{k+n}(X_{g}))
as the direct sum

\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}(\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\pi_{k+n+1}(Y_{g},X_{g}))\oplus\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}(\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\pi_{n+k}(X_{g})),

we observe that the image of the left vertical map under the identification above is the sum of
\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\otimes_{\pi}\pi_{k+n+1}(Y_{g},X_{g})
and
\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\otimes_{\pi}\pi_{n+k}(X_{g})
, using the duality isomorphisms above
Lemma

2.2.3
(see also
2.2.1
). Observe that
\smash{\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(Y_{g};X_{g})}
is, as a
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space, the kernel of the map of Lie algebras
p:\mathrm{Lie}(\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\oplus\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})\to\mathrm{Lie}(\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})
, where we see
\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
and
\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
as graded
\mathbf{Q}
-vector spaces concentrated in degree
n-1
. Thus, by
2
, it is generated by words of the form
[a,b]
where either
a\in\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
or
b
is in this kernel. We now proceed with the second step. We recall that, under the identification above, the map
\pi_{k}(\mathrm{res}_{X})
is given by the Whitehead product map (see proof of
2.3.1
). Observe that the image of the Whitehead bracket map of
\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\otimes_{\pi}\pi_{k+n+1}(Y_{g},X_{g})
are those classes which can be decomposed as sums of words
[x,y]
where
y\in\pi_{k+n+1}(Y_{g},X_{g})
. Similarly, the image of
\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\otimes_{\pi}\pi_{n+k}(X_{g})
are those classes which can be decomposed as sums of words
[x,y]
where
x\in\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
. Thus, the kernel of
p
is hit by the Whitehead bracket map, once restricted to the image of the left vertical map. We conclude that the map
\pi_{k}(\mathrm{res}_{Y})
is surjective, since taking coinvariants by
\pi
does not affect surjectivity.
∎

The same proof as before gives the following analogous result. We leave the details to the reader.

Proposition 2.3.3
.

The following statements hold for
k\geq 0:

(i)

There is an isomorphism

\pi_{k}(\Map_{*}(V_{g};W_{g,1}),\id)\cong\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\smash{H_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\pi_{k+n}(W_{g,1})).

(ii)

There is an isomorphism

\pi_{k}(\Map_{*}((D^{2n},S^{2n-1}),(V_{g},W_{g,1})),\iota)\cong\pi_{k+2n}(V_{g},W_{g,1})
where
\iota:(D^{2n},S^{2n-1})\hookrightarrow(V_{g},W_{g,1})
denotes the inclusion.

2.4
The reflection involution on
(Y_{g};X_{g})

In this subsection, we define an involution
\rho_{g}
on the
4
-ad
(Y_{g};\partial_{0}Y_{g},\partial_{1}Y_{g},\partial_{2}Y_{g})
of
2.0.1
(iv). Given any such involution, then conjugation by
\rho_{g}
induces an involution on the spaces
\Diff_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g})
,
\Diffb(X_{g})
,
\smash{\Emb_{\partial_{0}}^{{\cong}}}(Y_{g};X_{g}),
and
\smash{\Emb_{\partial_{0}}^{{\cong}}}(X_{g})
, and their block analogues (recall
Section

2.1
). Taking classifying spaces induces a compatible
A_{\infty}
-involution
5
5

5

An
A_{\infty}
-involution
on a space
X
is a morphism of
A_{\infty}
-algebras
C_{2}\to\End(X)
to the endomorphism monoid of a space
X
on the spaces
\BDiff_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g})
,
\BDiffb(X_{g})
,
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{{\cong}}}(Y_{g};X_{g}),
and
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{{\cong}}}(X_{g})
, and their block analogues. We will define
\rho_{g}
with the following design criteria, which will be the only properties from
\rho_{g}
used in later sections:

(\rho 1)

The action of
\rho_{g}
on the standard
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
-basis
\{a_{i},b_{i}\}_{i=1}^{g}
of
\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
(recall
2.2.2
) takes
a_{i}
to
-a_{i}
and fixes
b_{i}.

(\rho 2)

There exists a stable framing
\ell
on
Y_{g}
, such that the
A_{\infty}
-involutions by conjugation of
\rho_{g}
on the spaces above admit compatible lifts to
A_{\infty}
-involutions on the spaces
\BDiff^{\mathrm{sfr}}_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g})_{\ell}
,
\BDiffsfr(X_{g})_{\ell}
,
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell},
and
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}
, and their block analogues.

To this end, we will first fix a model of
(Y_{g};\partial_{0}Y_{g},\partial_{1}Y_{g},\partial_{2}Y_{g})
as a submanifold of
\mathbf{R}^{2n+1}
together with fixed embeddings
e_{i},f_{i}:S^{n}\hookrightarrow Y_{g}
representing the classes
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
, respectively, such that
(Y_{g};\partial_{0}Y_{g},\partial_{1}Y_{g},\partial_{2}Y_{g})\subset(Y_{g+1};\partial_{0}Y_{g+1},\partial_{1}Y_{g+1},\partial_{2}Y_{g+1})
and these inclusions preserve the embeddings
e_{i}
and
f_{i}.
We will then define
\rho_{g}
as reflection on the first coordinate of
\mathbf{R}^{2n+1}
, which will fix
(Y_{g};\partial_{0}Y_{g},\partial_{1}Y_{g},\partial_{2}Y_{g})
setwise. Hence, the following design criterion will also hold.

(\rho 3)

The inclusions
(Y_{g};\partial_{0}Y_{g},\partial_{1}Y_{g},\partial_{2}Y_{g})\hookrightarrow(Y_{g+1};\partial_{0}Y_{g+1},\partial_{1}Y_{g+1},\partial_{2}Y_{g+1})
are equivariant with respect to the actions of
\rho_{g}
and
\rho_{g+1}
, respectively.

2.4.1
A standard model

We start by modelling
Y_{g}
as a submanifold of
\mathbf{R}^{2n+1}
. This will be done using the
standard model
of
V_{g}
as a submanifold of
\mathbf{R}^{2n+1}
, defined in
[
55
, Section 2.5]
. We briefly recall the properties of this construction that we will need. In loc.cit., the authors model
V_{g}\subset\mathbf{R}^{2n+1}
and define embeddings
e_{i},f_{i}:S^{n}\to\partial V_{g}
representing the standard basis
a_{i},b_{i}\in\smash{H_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
of the embeddings
S^{n}\times\{*\},{*}\times S^{n}\subset W_{1,1}
(see
2.2.2
) satisfying the following properties:

(2.4.1.a)

The reflection along the first coordinate of
\mathbf{R}^{2n+1}
restricts to a diffeomorphism of
V_{g}
. This diffeomorphism fixes
f_{i}
pointwise, and once restricted to
e_{i}
is given by precomposition with the reflection along one coordinate of
S^{n}
(in particular, it has degree
-1
).

(2.4.1.b)

The submanifold
V_{g}
is contained in
\mathbf{R}^{2n}\times[\frac{1}{2},2g)
, and
V_{g}
is contained in
V_{h}
, for
g\leq h
.

(2.4.1.c)

The point
\frac{1}{2}\cdot\vec{e}_{2n+1}
is in
\partial V_{g},
where
\vec{e}_{i}
denotes the
i
-th basis vector of
\mathbf{R}^{2n+1}.

These properties follow directly by the construction in
[
55
, 14]
, by unravelling definitions. We now define a model of
S^{1}\times D^{2n}
as a submanifold of
\mathbf{R}^{2n}\times(-2,0).
Denote the standard inclusion
S^{1}\subset\mathbf{R}^{2}
by
\iota_{S^{1}}
and see its normal bundle as a subset of
\mathbf{R}^{2}
. We define the embedding
\phi_{S^{1}}:S^{1}\times D^{1}\times D^{2n-1}\to\mathbf{R}^{2n-1}\times\mathbf{R}^{2}
defined by
\phi_{S^{1}}(z,t,x)=(x,\frac{1+t}{2}\iota_{S^{1}}(z))-\frac{3}{2}\vec{e}_{2n+1}
to see
S^{1}\times D^{2n}
as a submanifold of
\mathbf{R}^{2n+1}
. Notice also that the image of
\phi_{S^{1}}
is contained in
\mathbf{R}^{2n}\times(-2,0)
, contains the point
-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\vec{e}_{2n+1},
and is preserved setwise by the reflection along the first coordinate of
\mathbf{R}^{2n+1}.
Now, by choosing an appropriate tubular neighbourhood of the path
[0,1]\ni s\mapsto\frac{t-1}{2}\cdot\vec{e}_{2n+1}
connecting
V_{g}
with the image of
S^{1}\times D^{2n}\subset\mathbf{R}^{2n+1}
(and in
(0,1)
is disjoint from both images by
(2.4.1.b)
), which is axially symmetric around the
\vec{e}_{2n+1}
-axis, then observe that the union of
S^{1}\times D^{2n}
with
V_{g}
along this tubular neighbourhood is diffeomorphic to
Y_{g}
. We define the submanifold
Y_{g}\subset\mathbf{R}^{2n+1}
to be this union. Observe that, by the definition above and
(2.4.1.a)
, the reflection along the first coordinate restricts to a diffeomorphism of
Y_{g}
.

Definition 2.4.1
.

(Reflection involution)
We define the involution
\rho_{g}:Y_{g}\to Y_{g}
to be the reflection on the first coordinate of
\mathbf{R}^{2n+1}
, restricted to the submanifold
Y_{g}\subset\mathbf{R}^{2n+1}
.

We can fix a
4
-ad structure on
Y_{g}
by fixing a decomposition of
S^{2n-1}\cong\partial(D^{1}\times D^{2n-1})
as two
(2n-1)
-dimensional discs
D_{\pm}
such that the reflection on the first coordinate restricted to
S^{1}\times D^{2n}\subset\mathbf{R}^{2n+1}
restricts to a reflection on each disc, and setting
\partial_{0}Y_{g}
and
\partial_{1}Y_{g}
to be, respectively,
S^{1}\times D_{+}
and
S^{1}\times D_{-}
. Using this
4
-ad structure, the involution
\rho_{g}
is a diffeomorphism of
4
-ads. We observe that the design criteria

(

⁢
ρ
1

)

and

(

⁢
ρ
3

)

follow directly from
(2.4.1.a)
and
(2.4.1.b)
. In the next subsubsection, we show that

(

⁢
ρ
2

)

holds.

Remark 2.4.2
.

The embedding
\phi_{S^{1}}
extends to
D^{2}\times D^{2n-1}\cong D^{2n}
, using the standard inclusion to
\mathbf{R}^{2n+1}
translated by
-\frac{3}{2}\vec{e}_{2n+1}.
If we take the union of this extended embedding with
V_{g}
along the tubular neighbourhood of the path used above, we obtain another model
V_{g}^{\prime}
for
V_{g}
as a submanifold of
\mathbf{R}^{2n+1}
. By retracting the former embedding, we see that these two submanifolds are ambient isotopic. Moreover, this ambient isotopy can be arranged while preserving the symmetry along the first axis. Thus, we have
\langle\rho\rangle
-equivariant embeddings
V_{g}\subset Y_{g}\subset V_{g}^{\prime},
where the composite
V_{g}\subset V_{g}^{\prime}
is a
\langle\rho\rangle
-equivariant ambient isotopy.

2.4.2
Action on stable framings

In this section, we lift the action of
\rho_{g}
on various automorphism and embedding spaces of
Y_{g}
by conjugation to an action on the its variants with stable framings, as defined in
Section

2.1.2
. We fix once and for all a standard stable framing on
Y_{g}
.

Convention 2.4.3
.

(Standard framing)
For the entirity of this paper, the stable framing
\ell
on
Y_{g}
will denote the stable framing induced by the inclusion
Y_{g}\subset\mathbf{R}^{2n+1}
and the standard framing on
\mathbf{R}^{2n+1}
. This framing will be called the
standard stable framing
on
Y_{g}
. Observe that the restriction of
\ell
to
V_{g}\subset Y_{g}
is the stabilisation of the
standard framing
in the sense of
[
55
, Section 2.5.1]
. By
2.4.2
, we see that the inclusion
Y_{g}\subset V_{g}
takes the standard stable framing on
V_{g}
to a stable framing homotopic to
\ell.

To lift the action of the reflection involution to stable framings, we will proceed analogously as
[
55
, Section 2.8.1]
. We briefly recall their strategy, applied to our setting. We focus on lifting the action along the map
\BDiff^{\mathrm{sfr}}_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g})_{\ell}\to\BDiff_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g})
, as the remaining cases are analogous. We will use the following facts:

(a)

We have a fibre sequence

\BDiff_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g})\to\BDiff(Y_{g};\partial^{v})\to\mathrm{BDiff_{\partial}^{ext}}(\partial^{v}Y_{g})

where the total space is the classifying space of the topological group of diffeomorphisms of
Y_{g}
that fix
\partial^{v}Y_{g}
setwise, the base space is the classifying space of the subgroup of
\Diffb(\partial^{v}Y_{g})
given by the union of the path components of those diffeomorphisms that extend to
Y_{g}
, and the right map restriction of diffeomorphisms. As for any fibre sequence of spaces, the loop space of the total space acts in the
A_{\infty}
-sense on the fibre by pointed maps. For the specific case of the sequence above, and under the canonical equivalence
\Diff(Y_{g};\partial^{v})\to\Omega\BDiff(Y_{g};\partial^{v})
, this action agrees with the action of
\Diff(Y_{g};\partial^{v})
on
\BDiff_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g})
by conjugation of diffeomorphisms. Thus, the reflection involution on
\BDiff_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g})
agrees with the action of
\Omega\BDiff(Y_{g};\partial^{v})
on this space, once restricted to
\Omega\text{{B}}\langle\rho_{g}\rangle.

(b)

The group
\Diff(Y_{g};\partial^{v})
acts on
