\Bun(TY_{g};\mathrm{sfr}^{*}\gamma_{2n})
, so we denote its homotopy orbits by
\BDiff^{\mathrm{sfr}}(Y_{g};\partial^{v})
. Note that we have an analogous fibre sequence as above, by replacing the spaces with their stably framed analogs. Thus, to lift the action of
\langle\rho_{g}\rangle
on
\BDiff_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g})
to
\BDiff^{\mathrm{sfr}}_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g})_{\ell}
, it suffices to solve the following lifting problem

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BDiff^{\mathrm{sfr}}(Y_{g};\partial^{v})_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{{B}}\langle\rho_{g}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BDiff(Y_{g};\partial^{v})}

(2.4.1)

by using the canonical action of
\Omega\BDiff^{\mathrm{sfr}}(Y_{g};\partial^{v})_{\ell}
on
\BDiff^{\mathrm{sfr}}_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g})_{\ell}
, and precomposing it with the lift
\langle\rho_{g}\rangle\to\Omega\text{{B}}\langle\rho_{g}\rangle\to\Omega\BDiff^{\mathrm{sfr}}(Y_{g};\partial^{v})_{\ell}
.

One can observe that the strategy of proof of
[
55
, Lemma 2.12]
also extends to our setting so to show that the lift of (
2.4.1
) exists: one simply replaces
V_{g}
by
X_{g}
, framings by stable framings and the structure
\pm\mathrm{fr}
by
\pm\mathrm{sfr}:\oo(1)\to\oo(2n+1)
, using the fact that, by definition, the stable framing
\ell
extends to
\mathbf{R}^{2n+1}.
One can check that such lifts can be found in a compatible way for the various diffeomorphism and embedding spaces used in this work, hence establishing

(

⁢
ρ
2

)

. We leave this check to the reader. We fix
once and for all
such choice of lifts.

3
Mapping class groups and their homology

In this section, we study various mapping class groups of (stably framed) self-embeddings of
X_{g}
and
Y_{g}
. We start by recalling the work of Bustamante and Randal-Williams
[
22
]
on the former case, and later obtain an analogous result for the odd-dimensional case, whose proof is heavily inspired by Krannich’s work of the case for
V_{g}
in
[
57
]
.

3.1
The case of
X_{g}

In this subsection, we recall the work in
[
22
, Section 6.2]
. Recall that we have fixed the standard stable framing
\ell
on
Y_{g}
, which induces a stable framing on
X_{g}
, which we will also denote by
\ell.
Denote the group
\pi_{1}(\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g};\ell_{0}),\ell)
by
\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}
(recall
Section

2.1.2
). Given any stable framing
\ell
on
X_{g}
, there exists a unique framing which stabilises to it. This produces a map
\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to\pi_{n}(\Fr^{+}(TX_{g}))\cong\text{Imm}^{\text{fr}}_{n}(X_{g})
from
[
22
, Section 2]
. Here, the rightmost identification follows from Hirsch-Smale theory and depends on the choice of a framing on
S^{n}\times D^{n}
. We impose that this framing extends to
D^{n+1}\times D^{n}
, which is always possible (take for example the framing induced by the embedding
S^{n}\times D^{n}\subset\mathbf{R}^{2n})
. Using this map, one can lift the map
q_{X}:\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to\mathbf{Z}[\pi]/\Lambda_{n}
to a map
q_{X}^{\ell}:\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to\mathbf{Z}[\pi]/\Lambda^{\text{min}}_{n}
, by taking generic self-intersections of immersions (see loc.cit.). By unwrapping the definition of
q_{X}^{\ell}
, we see that
(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\lambda_{X},q_{X}^{\ell})
is a hyperbolic module with respect to the basis
\{a_{i},b_{i}\}_{i=1}^{g}
of
2.2.2
, that is,
q_{X}^{\ell}(a_{i})=0=q_{X}^{\ell}(b_{i})
,
\lambda_{X}(a_{i},a_{j})=0=\lambda_{X}(b_{i},b_{j})
and
\lambda_{X}(a_{i},b_{j})=\delta_{ij}
(see also the proof of Lemma 6.6 of loc.cit). The hyperbolic basis
\{a_{i},b_{i}\}_{i=1}^{g}
induces a map

\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}\to\mathrm{U}_{g}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n}^{\min})

whose image we denote by
\Omega^{\min}_{g}.
We state now the main input from
[
22
]
regarding this map given by Corollary 5.8 and Proposition 6.7 in loc.cit.

Theorem 3.1.1
.

Let
g\geq 8
if
n
is even and
g\geq 2
if
n
is odd. We have an extension

1\to L_{g}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}\to\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}\to\Omega^{\min}_{g}\to 1

where
L_{g}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}
is a finite group. Moreover, the subgroup
\Omega^{\min}_{g}\subset\mathrm{U}_{g}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n}^{\min})
is a normal subgroup and the quotient
\mathrm{U}_{g}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n}^{\min})/\Omega^{\min}_{g}
is a finite abelian group independent of
g
.

3.2
The case of
(Y_{g};X_{g})

We now investigate the analogous mapping class group of stably framed self-embeddings of the pair
(Y_{g};X_{g})
. Our strategy is heavily inspired by and relies on both the even-dimensional case of the last subsection, and the work of Krannich in
[
57
]
for the analogous problem for the pair
(V_{g};W_{g,1}).

3.2.1
Homotopy mapping class groups of
Y_{g}

Recall the map
\pi_{0}(\Embo(X_{g}))\to\mathrm{U}_{g}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})
given by the action on the quadratic module
(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\lambda,q).
The discussion in
[
22
, Section 3.4]
proves that this map factors through forgetting to
\pi_{0}(\Autb(X_{g})).
In other words, homotopy automorphisms of
X_{g}
fixing the boundary preserve the intersection form. By pre-composition, we obtain a map
\pi_{0}(\Aut_{\partial^{v}}(V_{g};X_{g}))\to\mathrm{U}_{g}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})
. It is clear that the image lies in the subgroup
\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})
and that this map factors
\pi_{0}(\Embo(Y_{g};X_{g}))\to\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n}).
The analogous claim holds once
X_{g}
and
Y_{g}
are replaced by
W_{g,1}
and
V_{g}.

Proposition 3.2.1
.

The square of groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Aut_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g};X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Aut_{\partial^{v}}(V_{g};W_{g,1}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z},\Lambda_{n})}

induced by the inclusion of
4
-ads
Y_{g}\hookrightarrow V_{g}
is a pullback square with surjective horizontal maps.

Proof.

To prove surjectivity of the horizontal maps, observe that they factor the maps present in
Lemma

2.2.4
, which are surjective. We now prove that the square is a pullback of groups. We start by reducing this statement to proving that the canonical map between the horizontal kernels is an isomorphism. This follows from the following standard observation in group theory: a commutative square of groups whose horizontal maps are surjective

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

is a pullback square if and only if the map
\ker(f)\to\ker(g)
is an isomorphism. Recall that
\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})
is a subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}(\pi_{n}(X_{g}))
and similarly for
\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z},\Lambda_{n})
. By
[
22
, Thm. 3.1]
, we have the action of
\Map_{S^{1}}(X_{g})
on
\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
induces an isomorphism
\pi_{0}(\Map_{S^{1}}(X_{g}))\cong\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}).
The analogous result for
W_{g,1}
is given in
[
22
, Thm. 3.3]
. Therefore, we can verify that the horizontal kernels of the square above are isomorphic by checking the same property for the following square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Aut_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g};X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Map_{S^{1}}(X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Aut_{\partial^{v}}(V_{g};W_{g,1}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Map_{*}(W_{g,1}))}

whose horizontal maps are induced by restricting automorphisms to
\partial_{2}.
This restriction map factors through
\Map_{S^{1}}(Y_{g};X_{g})
from
2.3.2
. Moreover, the map
\Map_{S^{1}}(Y_{g};X_{g})\to\Map_{S^{1}}(X_{g})
is injective on
\pi_{0}
: in the description of
2.3.2
and
[
22
, Thm. 3.1]
, the induced map on
\pi_{0}
is the inclusion
\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})\to\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})
, and the same holds for
W_{g,1}.
We conclude that we can test isomorphic horizontal kernels of the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Aut_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g};X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Map_{S^{1}}(Y_{g};X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Aut_{\partial^{v}}(V_{g};W_{g,1}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Map_{*}(V_{g};W_{g,1}))}

to deduce that the kernels of the original square are isomorphic. The top horizontal map fits in the long exact sequence of the fibre sequence

\Aut_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g};X_{g})\to\Aut_{S^{1}}(Y_{g};X_{g})\to\Map_{S^{1}}((S^{1}\times D^{2n-1},S^{1}\times S^{2n-2}),(Y_{g},X_{g}))

and similarly for the bottom map. Consider the following portion of this map of long exact sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(\Map_{S^{1}}(Y_{g};X_{g}),\iota)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\pi_{2n+1}(Y_{g},X_{g})\right]_{\pi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Aut_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g};X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Map_{S^{1}}(Y_{g};X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(\Map_{*}(V_{g};W_{g,1}),\iota)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2n+1}(V_{g},W_{g,1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Aut_{\partial^{v}}(V_{g};W_{g,1}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Map_{*}(V_{g};W_{g,1})).}

To show that the kernels of the rightmost horizontal maps are isomorphic, it suffices to show that the cokernels of the leftmost horizontal maps are isomorphic. We shall first describe these maps using
[
22
, Thm. 3.1]
. We start with the top map. The restriction map to
\partial_{2}
induces the following commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(\Map_{S^{1}}(Y_{g};X_{g}),\iota)\cong\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\pi_{n+1}(X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\pi_{2n+1}(Y_{g},X_{g})\right]_{\pi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(\Map_{S^{1}}(X_{g}),\iota)\cong\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\pi_{n+1}(X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\pi_{2n}(X_{g})\right]_{\pi}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{res}_{Y}}

where
\mathrm{res}_{Y}
denotes the induced map
\pi_{1}(\mathrm{res}_{Y})
from (
2.3.2
). Once again, both vertical maps in this square are injective. From the description of the bottom map in
[
22
, Thm. 3.1]
, we can describe the top map as taking
\phi
to the sum
\sum_{i=1}^{g}[a_{i},\phi(b_{i})]+(-1)^{n}[\phi(a_{i}),b_{i}],
where
a_{i}
denote the generators of
\smash{H_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\subset\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
representing the classes
S^{n}\times\{*\}
in the
i
-th copy of
S^{n}\times S^{n}
and
b_{i}
the classes representing
\{*\}\times S^{n}
. Note that
b_{i}
generate
\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
and
a_{i}
generate
\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
as
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
-modules. From the computation of
\pi_{k}(X_{g})
in the metastable range from
[
22
, Appendix A]
, we see that

[\pi_{2n+1}(Y_{g},X_{g})]_{\pi}\cong\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}g+1\leq i\leq 2g\end{subarray}}\pi_{2n}(S^{n})\{x_{i}\}\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq 2g\\
g+1\leq j\leq 2g\\
a\in\mathbf{Z},\ (a,i)<(0,j)\end{subarray}}\pi_{2n}(S^{2n-1})\{t^{a}x_{i}\otimes x_{j}\}

where the first term is given by
x_{i}\circ f
for
f\in\pi_{2n}(S^{n})
and
x_{i}=a_{i}
for
i\leq g
and
x_{i}=b_{i-g}
for
g+1\leq i
, and the second term is given by
[t^{a}x_{i},x_{j}]\circ f^{\prime}
for
f^{\prime}\in\pi_{2n}(S^{2n-1}).
We compute now the cokernel of
\mathrm{res}_{Y}.
This is heavily inspired by
[
22
, Thm. 3.5]
. We start by showing that the image of
\mathrm{res}_{Y}
spans the second summand. For fixed
1\leq i,j\leq g
and
a\in\mathbf{Z},
consider the morphism
\phi:\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to\pi_{n+1}(X_{g})
defined by
\phi(b_{i})=t^{-a}b_{j}\circ\eta
and that sends all the other generators to
0
, where
\eta\in\pi_{2n}(S^{2n-1})
is the suspension of the Hopf fibration element. This is in
\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\pi_{n+1}(X_{g}))
since all the elements
b_{i}
are sent to
\pi_{n+2}(Y_{g},X_{g}).
Thus,
\mathrm{res}_{Y}(\phi)=[a_{i},t^{-a}b_{j}\circ\eta]
, which equals
[a_{i},t^{-a}b_{j}]\circ\eta
, since
\eta
is a suspension, by
[
22
, Section A.2]
. On the other hand,
[a_{i},t^{-a}b_{j}]\circ\eta=[t^{a}a_{i},b_{j}]\circ\eta
, since we took coinvariants. Since
\eta
generates
\pi_{2n}(S^{2n-1})
, the second factor when
i\leq g
is spanned by the image of
\mathrm{res}_{Y}.
For
g<i
, simply choose
\phi
to be defined by
\phi(a_{i})=t^{a}b_{i}\circ\eta
and the remaining generators to
0
, which again lies in
\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\pi_{n+1}(X_{g})).
By the same argument of
[
22
, 1644]
, the remaining elements generate
x_{i}\circ f
for
f
in the kernel of the suspension map
\Sigma:\pi_{2n}(S^{n})\to\pi_{n+1}(S^{n+1}).
We conclude that the cokernel of
\mathrm{res}_{Y}
identifies with
\smash{K_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\otimes\Sigma\pi_{2n}(S^{n}).
The exact same argument along with the computations in
[
22
, Appendix A.1]
identifies the cokernel of
\mathrm{res}_{Y}
in the
W_{g,1}
case with the same group. As mentioned in this reference, under these identifications the map between the cokernels can be seen to be the identity. Thus, we conclude that the horizontal kernels of the square in the claim are isomorphic and thus the square is a pullback square. This finishes the proof.
∎

3.2.2
Isotopy classes of self-embeddings of
Y_{g}

In this section, we describe the group
\pi_{0}(\Embop(Y_{g};X_{g}))
up to extensions. We shall do so by combining
3.2.1
and results from
[
57
, Section 3]
and
[
22
, Section 3]
. Let
S\pi_{n}(\SO(n))\subset\pi_{n}(\SO(n+1))
denote the image of the stabilisation map
\pi_{n}(\SO(n))\to\pi_{n}(\SO(n+1))
. Let
M_{g}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi])
be the abelian group of sesquilinear forms
\mu:\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\otimes\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
(that is,
\mu(a\cdot x,b\cdot y)=a\mu(x,y)\bar{b}
for all
a,b\in\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
and
x,y\in\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
) which are
(-1)^{n+1}
-symmetric (that is,
\mu(x,y)=(-1)^{n+1}\overline{\mu(y,x)}
for all
x,y\in\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
) and whose values
\mu(x,x)
lie in
\Lambda_{n}
for all
x\in\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}.
We have the following result.

Proposition 3.2.2
.

The action of
\pi_{0}(\Embop(Y_{g};X_{g}))
on
\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
induces an extension

1\to\smash{K_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\otimes S\pi_{n}(\SO(n))\to\pi_{0}(\Embop(Y_{g};X_{g}))\to\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})\to 1.

Moreover, the projection
\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})\to\GL(\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})
fits into an extension of the form

1\to M_{g}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi])\to\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})\to\GL(\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})\to 1

which admits a splitting.

Proof.

We begin with the following remarks. Firstly, the map
\Diff_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g})\to\Embo(Y_{g};X_{g})
hits all path components. In other words, every self-embedding
e
of
Y_{g}
is isotopic to a diffeomorphism: first isotope the embedding
e
so that the image of
\partial_{1}Y_{g}
lies in the interior of
Y_{g}
and consider its complement. Observe that this complement is an
h
-cobordism from
S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}\cong\partial_{1}Y_{g}\cup e(\partial_{1}Y_{g})
to the complement of the self-embedding
e|_{X_{g}}.
Since
\text{Wh}_{1}(\mathbf{Z})
is trivial, this
h
-cobordism is trivial and thus
e
may be isotoped to be surjective and thus a diffeomorphism. Therefore, the decoration
\cong
is redundant. Secondly, the map
\pi_{0}(\Embo(Y_{g};X_{g}))\to\pi_{0}(\bEmbo(Y_{g};X_{g}))
is an isomorphism: one considers the map of fibre sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Emb_{X_{g}\cup\partial_{0}}^{\cong}(Y_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Embo(Y_{g};X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Embop(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Emb}_{X_{g}\cup\partial_{0}}^{\cong}(Y_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bEmbo(Y_{g};X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\cong}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})}

where the decoration
\cong
on the leftmost spaces refers to the path components hit by
\Diffb(Y_{g}).
By applying Morlet’s lemma of disjunction
[
19
, Thm. 3.1]
(or
[
55
, Thm. 4.4]
), the outer vertical map are
(n-2)
-connected (see
[
22
, Prop. 4.3]
for more details). Hence, they are isomorphisms on path components when
n\geq 3
. In this case, the middle map induces an isomorphism on path components. Thirdly, as observed in
Section

2.1.3
, we have an equivalence
\smash{\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}_{\partial^{v}}}(Y_{g})\simeq\bEmbo(Y_{g};X_{g}).
The analogous discussion holds for
V_{g}
and
W_{g,1}
. Consider the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Embop(Y_{g};X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Aut_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g};X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Embop(V_{g};W_{g,1}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Aut_{\partial^{v}}(V_{g};W_{g,1}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z},\Lambda_{n}).}

(3.2.1)

By
3.2.1
, the rightmost square is a pullback square. To show the first extension, we start by showing that the outer square is a pullback square. Since the right square is cartesian, it suffices to prove that the horizontal kernels of the left square are isomorphic. To do so, it is equivalent to showing the same claim for the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\smash{\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}_{\partial^{v}}}(Y_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Aut_{\partial^{v}}^{\cong}(Y_{g};X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\smash{\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}_{\partial^{v}}}(V_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Aut_{\partial^{v}}^{\cong}(V_{g};W_{g,1}))}

by the discussion above. The fibre of the map
\smash{\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}_{\partial^{v}}}(Y_{g})\to\Aut_{\partial^{v}}^{\cong}(Y_{g};X_{g})
is equivalent to the loop space of the block surgery structure space of the triad
Y_{g}
fixed on
\partial^{v}Y_{g}
(see
[
57
, Section 2]
for more details). Since this triad satisfies the "
\pi
-
\pi
"-condition, it follows that the surgery structure space is equivalent to
\Map_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g},\mathrm{G}/\mathrm{O})
. This is, in turn, equivalent to
\Map_{\partial^{v}}(V_{g},\mathrm{G}/\mathrm{O})
and thus (after looping) to the fibre of
\smash{\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}_{\partial^{v}}}(V_{g})\to\Aut_{\partial^{v}}^{\cong}(V_{g};W_{g,1})
. So the square above is obtained by applying
\pi_{0}
to a homotopy cartesian square. Thus, it is a pullback square and hence, the horizontal kernels are isomorphic. We conclude that the kernel of the map
\pi_{0}(\Embop(Y_{g};X_{g}))\to\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})
is isomorphic to the kernel of the map
\pi_{0}(\Embop(V_{g};W_{g,1}))\to\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z},\Lambda_{n})
, which is isomorphic to
\smash{K_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\otimes S\pi_{n}(\SO(n))
by
[
57
, Thm. 3.3.(iii)]
. This finishes the proof of the first extension.

We now move to the proof of the second extension. We start with the following simple observations:

(i)

any
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
-linear map
\phi:\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
taking
\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
to itself is completely determined by the maps
A=\phi|_{\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}}:\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
,
A^{\prime}=p_{0}\circ\phi|_{\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}}:\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
and
B=p_{1}\circ\phi|_{\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}}:\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
, where
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
are the projections
\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
and
\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}};

(ii)

such
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
-linear map
\phi
preserves the hyperbolic form
\lambda_{X}
if and only if
A
is invertible,
A=(A^{-1})^{\vee}
and
A\circ B=(-1)^{n}(A\circ B)^{\vee}
. Here,
(-)^{\vee}
denotes the dual morphism in the category of
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
-modules and we use the identification
(\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})^{\vee}\cong\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}.

(iii)

\phi
preserves the quadratic refinement
q_{X}
if and only if
A
preserves
q_{X}
and
B+(A^{-1})^{\vee}:\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
preserves the quadratic form.

Under this notation, the map
p:\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})\to\GL(\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})
takes a map
\phi
to the map
A
and its clear that the map
\GL(\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})\to\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})
taking
A
to
A+(A^{-1})^{\vee}
is a splitting of this map. Moreover, the kernel of
p
is exactly those
\phi
such that
A=\id.
Let
\phi\in\ker(p)
, then it follows from the remarks above that
B=(-1)^{n}B^{\vee}
and
B+\id
preserves
q
. From the second condition, we conclude that for
x\in\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
,

q(x)=q(x+B(x))=q(x)+q(B(x))-\lambda(x,B(x))\mod\Lambda_{n}.

Moreover from the fact that
q(B(x))=0
since
q
vanishes on the isotropic subspace
\smash{K_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
, we conclude that
\lambda(x,B(x))
vanishes in
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]/\Lambda_{n}
and thus lives in
\Lambda_{n}
. Consider the map
r:\ker(p)\to M_{g}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi])
that takes a map
\phi
to the sesquilinear form
\mu_{\phi}
given by
\mu_{\phi}(x,y)=\lambda(x,B(y))
. It follows from the discussion above that
\mu_{\phi}
is
(-1)^{n+1}
-symmetric and
\mu_{\phi}(x,x)\in\Lambda_{n}
. In other words, this map associates
\phi
to the adjoint of
B:\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to(\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})^{\vee}
. Thus, the image of
\phi
under
r
totally determines
B
and thus determines
\phi
since
A=\id.
In other words, the map
r
is injective. Surjectivity follows from the fact that such a sesquilinear form
\mu
determines a map
B_{\mu}
since
\lambda
is non-degenerate. Thus,
r
is an isomorphism of groups, which finishes the proof of the second claim.
∎

3.2.3
Stably framed embeddings

Denote by
\pi_{0}(\Embop(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell})
the image of the map
\pi_{1}(\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(Y_{g};X_{g};\ell_{0}),\ell)\to\pi_{1}(\BEmbop(Y_{g};X_{g})).
This can be described as the isotopy classes of those self-embeddings
e
such that
e^{*}\ell
is homotopic to
\ell
. Denote by
\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext},\ell}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})
the image of the subgroup
\pi_{0}(\Embop(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell})
on
\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})
, and by
M_{g}^{\mathrm{sfr}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi])\subset M_{g}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi])
the submodule of sesquilinear forms
\mu
such that the image of
\mu(x,x)
along the augmentation
\Lambda_{n}\subset\mathbf{Z}[\pi]\to\mathbf{Z}
is even, for all
x\in\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
. Write
\Lambda_{n}^{\mathrm{sfr}}
for the pre-image of
2\mathbf{Z}
along this map.

Proposition 3.2.3
.

The action of
\pi_{0}(\Embop(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell})
on
\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
induces an extension

1\to\smash{K_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\otimes\ker(S\pi_{n}(\SO(n))\to\pi_{n}(\SO))\to\pi_{0}(\Embop(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell})\to\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext},\ell}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})\to 1.

Moreover, the projection
\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext},\ell}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})\to\GL(\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})
fits into an extension of the form

1\to M_{g}^{\mathrm{sfr}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi])\to\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext},\ell}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})\to\GL(\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})\to 1.

Proof.

We start by proving the first extension. Notice that
\pi_{0}(\Embop(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell})
identifies with the kernel of the map
\pi_{0}(\Embop(Y_{g};X_{g}))\to\pi_{0}(\Bun_{\partial_{0}}(TY_{g},\mathrm{sfr}^{*}\gamma_{2n+1})).
Using the stable framing
\ell
, the latter identifies with
\smash{K_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\otimes\pi_{n}(\SO),
as
\smash{K_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\cong(\smash{H_{V_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})^{\vee}
and the map above is a crossed homomorphism
6
6

6

Let
G
be a group and
H
an abelian group with a
G
-action. A function
h:G\to H
is a
crossed homomorphism
if
h(xy)=h(x)+x\cdot h(y)
.
. Under this identification, the map

f:\smash{K_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\otimes S\pi_{n}(\SO(n))\to\pi_{0}(\Embop(Y_{g};X_{g}))\to\smash{K_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\otimes\pi_{n}(\SO),

where the left map is the one appearing in
3.2.2
, is a homomorphism and identifies with the stabilisation
S\pi_{n}(\SO(n))\to\pi_{n}(\SO)
tensored with
\smash{K_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
(see
[
57
, Prop. 3.7]
for a proof of this fact, since this map factors through
\pi_{0}(\Embop(V_{g};W_{g,1})
). The kernel of the map
\pi_{0}(\Embop(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell})\to\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext},\ell}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})
identifies with the kernel of
f
, which proves the first extension.

We move on to the second extension. Let
P
be the pullback of the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext},\ell}(\mathbf{Z},\Lambda_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z},\Lambda_{n})}

where
\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext},\ell}(\mathbf{Z},\Lambda_{n})\subset\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z},\Lambda_{n})
is the analogous image of
\pi_{0}(\Embop(V_{g};W_{g,1})_{\ell}).
Since the bottom map is an inclusion, we can identify
P
as a subgroup of
\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n}).
The square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Embop(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Embop(Y_{g};X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Embop(V_{g};W_{g,1})_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Embop(V_{g};X_{g}))}

is a pullback square of groups, since the leftmost groups are the kernels of maps to a common group, namely
\smash{K_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\otimes\pi_{n}(\SO).
Moreover, this square maps to the previous square. We conclude that the map
\pi_{0}(\Embop(Y_{g};V_{g})_{\ell})\to\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})
factors through
P
. In other words,
\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext},\ell}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})\subset P
. Since the square (
3.2.1
) is a pullback, so is the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Embop(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\Embop(V_{g};W_{g,1})_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext},\ell}(\mathbf{Z},\Lambda_{n}).}

However, by definition, the bottom map is surjective and thus, so is the top map. In other words,
\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext},\ell}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})=P.
Thus, we can describe the kernel
K
of the map
\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext},\ell}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})\to\GL(\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})
using the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})\to\GL(\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}))\cong M_{g}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext},\ell}(\mathbf{Z},\Lambda_{n})\to\GL(\smash{H_{V_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z},\Lambda_{n})\to\GL(\smash{H_{V_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})).}

The bottom kernels were described by Krannich in
[
57
, Thm. 3.3/Prop 3.7]
. The bottom right kernel is isomorphic to the submodule of sesquilinear forms
\mu:\smash{H_{V_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\otimes\smash{H_{V_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\to\mathbf{Z}
which are
(-1)^{n+1}
-symmetric and
\mu(x,x)\in\Lambda_{n}
(see
[
57
, Rmk. 3.2]
). Under this identification, the right vertical map is induced by the augmentation map
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]\to\mathbf{Z}.
The bottom left kernel is isomorphic to the submodule of those sesquilinear forms where
\mu(x,x)
is even. Under this identification, it is clear that
K
identifies with
M_{g}^{\mathrm{sfr}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi]).
This finishes the proof of the second extension.
∎

3.3
Stable homology of mapping class groups

In this section, we relate the stable homology with rational coefficients of the mapping class groups of stably framed embeddings of
X_{g}
and
(Y_{g};X_{g})
to that of groups of arithmetic nature. For the case of
X_{g}
, we deduce this relation from the work of Bustamante and Randal-Williams
[
22
]
, and for the
(Y_{g};X_{g})
, we use the work of the previous subsection together with a result in functor homology due to Betley
[
8
]
.

3.3.1
The case of
X_{g}

In this section, we extract the homology of the group
\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}
from the work of Bustamante and Randal-Williams
[
22
]
, as
g
is taken to
\infty
. The space
(\smash{\text{{B}}\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{\infty}})^{+}
is equivalent to the identity component of the loop space
\Omega\text{{B}}(\amalg_{g}\text{{B}}\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g})
by the group-completion theorem, applied to the monoid
\amalg_{g}\text{{B}}\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}
defined via stacking of embeddings, which is possible since
n\geq 2
(see also the proof of
[
22
, Lemma 7.7]
). In particular, it is a simple space. So is
\text{{B}}\mathrm{U}_{\infty}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n}^{\min})^{+}
for the same reason.

Proposition 3.3.1
.

For
n\geq 3
, the action of
\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}
on
\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
induces a map of simple spaces

\left(\smash{\text{{B}}\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{\infty}}\right)^{+}\to\text{{B}}\mathrm{U}_{\infty}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n}^{\min})^{+}

which induces an isomorphism on rational homotopy groups in all degrees.

Proof.

This follows by combining
[
22
, Lemma 7.10]
(stated above as
3.1.1
with the fact that there is a fibre sequence

(\text{{B}}\Omega_{\infty}^{\min})^{+}\to\text{{B}}\mathrm{U}_{\infty}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n}^{\min})^{+}\to\text{{B}}Q

where
Q
is the quotient
\mathrm{U}_{g}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n}^{\min})/\Omega_{g}^{\min}
, which is finite abelian and independent of
g
(see
3.1.1
), and thus its classifying space is rationally contractible.
∎

Remark 3.3.2
.

The rational homotopy groups of
\text{{B}}\mathrm{U}_{\infty}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n}^{\min})^{+}
were completely computed in
[
22
, Lemma 7.3]
, by combining the results of
[
43
,
25
,
23
,
24
]
. More precisely, we have that
\pi_{k}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\text{{B}}\mathrm{U}_{\infty}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n}^{\min})^{+})
is isomorphic to
\mathbf{Q}
when
k=1
or
2n+k\equiv 0,1
mod
4
, and vanishes otherwise.

3.3.2
The case of
(Y_{g};X_{g})

Denote the group
\pi_{1}(\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(Y_{g};X_{g}),\ell)
by
\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}
and the colimit over
g
along the stabilisation maps by
\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{\infty}
. The basis
a_{i}
(recall
2.2.2
) induces an isomorphism
\GL(\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})\cong\GL_{g}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi]).
Just as before, the spaces
(\text{{B}}\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{\infty})^{+}
and
\BGL_{\infty}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi])^{+}
are equivalent to loop spaces and are thus simple spaces.

Proposition 3.3.3
.

For
n\geq 3
, the action of
\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}
on
\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
induces a map of simple spaces

\left(\text{{B}}\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{\infty}\right)^{+}\to\BGL_{\infty}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi])^{+}

which induces an isomorphism on rational homotopy groups in all degrees.

Proof.

Recall that a map of simple spaces induces an isomorphism on rational homotopy groups if and only if it induces one on rational homology groups. We shall show that the map
\text{{B}}\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{\infty}\to\BGL_{\infty}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi])
is a rational homology isomorphism. Consider the sequence of groups

\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}\to\pi_{0}(\Embop(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell})\to\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext},\ell}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})\to\GL(\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})

(3.3.1)

which factors the action of
\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}
on
\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}.
Recall that, by definition, the leftmost map is surjective. We shall show that the leftmost and middle map are rational homology equivalences after taking classifying spaces and the rightmost map induces a rational homology equivalence after taking the colimit over
g
. We start with the leftmost map. Consider the following long exact sequence

\cdots\to\pi_{1}(\Bun_{\partial_{0}}(TY_{g};\mathrm{sfr}^{*}\gamma_{2n+1}),\ell)\to\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}\to\pi_{0}(\Embop(Y_{g};X_{g}))\to\cdots

and let us show that the kernel of the map
\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}\to\pi_{0}(\Embop(Y_{g};X_{g}))
is a finite group. Consider the following map of fibre sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{\partial_{0}}(TY_{g};\mathrm{sfr}^{*}\gamma_{2n+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BEmbop(Y_{g};X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BAut_{\partial^{v}}^{\cong}(Y_{g};X_{g}).}

As mentioned in the proof of
3.2.2
, the middle map is an isomorphism on
\pi_{1}.
By
2.1.1
,
F
has finite homotopy groups and thus the map
\pi_{1}(\Bun_{\partial_{0}}(TY_{g};\mathrm{sfr}^{*}\gamma_{2n+1}),\ell)\to\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}
factors thorugh a finite group. We conclude that the kernel
K
of the leftmost map of (
3.3.1
) is a finite group. The Serre spectral sequence of the fibre sequence
1\to\text{{B}}K\to\text{{B}}\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}\to\text{{B}}\pi_{0}(\Embop(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell})\to 1
and the fact that
\H_{*}(K;\mathbf{Q})
vanishes in positive degrees and is isomorphic to
\mathbf{Q}
in degree
0
implies that
\smash{\text{{B}}\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}\to\text{{B}}\pi_{0}(\Embop(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell})}
induces an isomorphism on rational homology in all degrees. As for the middle map, by
3.2.3
and the finiteness of
\ker(S\pi_{n}(\SO(n))\to\pi_{n}(\SO))
(see
[
59
, Lemma 3.2]
), we deduce that it is a rational homology equivalence after taking classifying spaces, by the same argument as the leftmost map.

We consider now the rightmost map. The second extension of
3.2.3
yields a spectral sequence

E^{2}_{s,t}=\H_{s}(\GL(\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}});\H_{t}(M_{g}^{\mathrm{sfr}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi]);\mathbf{Q}))\Rightarrow\H_{s+t}(\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext},\ell}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n});\mathbf{Q})

which is compatible with respect to stabilisation over
g
. We shall prove that

\colim_{g}\H_{s}(\GL(\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}});\H_{t}(M_{g}^{\mathrm{sfr}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi]);\mathbf{Q}))

(3.3.2)

vanishes for
t\geq 1
. Together with the fact that
\H_{0}(M_{g}^{\mathrm{sfr}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi]);\mathbf{Q})\cong\mathbf{Q}
, this implies that the homomorphism
\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext},\ell}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})\to\GL(\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})
induces a rational homology equivalence after taking classifying spaces and colimits over
g.
This vanishing result is a consequence of a result of Betley
[
8
, Thm. 4.2]
(better stated for our purposes in
[
27
, Thm. F.3]
). We proceed to prove this claim. Let
V
be a finitely generated projective
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
-module and denote the abelian group
M^{\mathrm{sfr}}(V)
of sesquilinear forms
\mu:V\otimes V\to\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
which are
(-1)^{n+1}
-symmetric and
\mu(x,x)\in\Lambda^{\mathrm{sfr}}_{n}.
The basis
\{a_{i}\}_{i}
induces an isomorphism from the group (
3.3.2
) and
\colim_{g}\H_{s}(\GL_{g}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi]);\H_{t}(M^{\mathrm{sfr}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi]^{g}))).
Let
t\geq 0
and consider the functor
F:\Proj_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}^{\text{op}}\to\Mod_{\mathbf{Q}}
taking a finitely generated projective
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
-module
V
to
\H_{t}(M(V);\mathbf{Q})
. Our goal is to show that
F
is
polynomial
in the sense of
[
34
, Lecture 1
Defn. 3.1]
. A functor between additive categories
T:C\to D
is called
polynomial
if its
d
-th cross-effect functor vanishes for some
d\geq 0
. The class of such functors is closed under composition and subfunctors (see
[
34
, 25]
). In our setting, the category
C
will be the either
\Proj_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}
, the category of finitely generated projective left
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
-modules, or its opposite. The functor
F
is a composition of the functor
M^{\mathrm{sfr}}:\Proj^{\text{op}}_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}\to\text{Ab}
taking
V
to
M(V)
and the functor
\H_{t}(-;\mathbf{Q}):\text{Ab}\to\Mod_{\mathbf{Q}}
. The latter identifies with the
t
-th symmetric power
\smash{\Lambda^{t}_{\mathbf{Q}}(-\otimes\mathbf{Q})}
which is polynomial (see
[
34
, Rmk. 3.3.4]
). Thus, it suffices to prove that
M^{\mathrm{sfr}}
is polynomial. Observe that
M^{\mathrm{sfr}}
is a subfunctor of the functor
T:\Proj^{\text{op}}_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}\to\text{Ab}
taking
V
to
\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}(V\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}V,\mathbf{Z}[\pi])
, since
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
is commutative and hence any sesquilinear form factors through
V\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}V
. (This tensor product is defined by using the right module structure on the first variable and the left modules structure on the second variable, and seen as a left
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
-module by using the left module structure on the first variable.) The functor
T
is in turn the composition of the functors
(V\mapsto V\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}V)
and
(V\to\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\pi]}(V,\mathbf{Z}[\pi]))
, which are polynomial by Example 3.2 and Remark 3.3.4 of loc.cit. This implies that
T
and the subfunctor
M^{\mathrm{sfr}}
are polynomial. We may apply
[
27
, Thm. F.3]
for the ring
A=\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
and the functor
F
for
t\geq 1
, as
F(0)=0
. We conclude that (
3.3.2
) vanishes for
t\geq 1
, and hence that the string of morphisms (
3.3.1
) induces a rational homology isomorphism after taking classifying spaces and colimit over
g
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.3.4
.

The rational homotopy groups of
\BGL_{\infty}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi])^{+}
agree with the rationalised algebraic
K
-theory groups of
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
in positive degrees. In particular, the Bass–Heller–Swan splitting in algebraic
K
-theory implies that
\pi_{k}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\BGL_{\infty}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi])^{+})\cong(\K_{k}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathbf{Z})\oplus\K^{\mathbf{Q}}_{k-1}(\mathbf{Z}))
for
k\geq 1
, and thus, by the work of Borel, are isomorphic to
\mathbf{Q}
if
k\equiv 1,2
mod
4
and
k\geq 4
, and vanishes otherwise.

3.4
Odd modules over mapping class groups

In this section, we will introduce the notion of a
gr-odd module
in the context of
\mathbf{Q}[G]
-modules for certain groups
G
. We will establish closure properties of the class of gr-odd modules and prove a vanishing result for group (co)homology with odd coefficients.

Definition 3.4.1
.

Let
G
be a group and
\iota\in G
be an element such that there exists a finite normal subgroup
L\lhd G
such that
\iota
lies in the center of
G/L
. Let
M
be a module over
\mathbf{Q}[G].
We say that
M
is
odd
if
\iota\cdot m=-m
for every
m\in M.
We say that
M
is
gr-odd
if it admits a finite length filtration of
\mathbf{Q}[G]
-submodules

0=F_{-1}M\subseteq\cdots\subseteq F_{i}M\subseteq F_{i+1}M\subseteq\cdots\subseteq F_{k}M=M

where for every
i,
the module
F_{i+1}M/F_{i}M
is odd. These filtrations will be called
odd
. We say that
M
is
even
if
\iota\cdot m=m
for every
m\in M.
Analogously, we define
gr-even
modules as those that have a finite length filtration where the filtration quotients are even.

We start by recording favorable closure properties of the classes of gr-odd and gr-even modules.

Proposition 3.4.2
.

The class of gr-odd
\mathbf{Q}[G]
-modules is closed under taking subgroups, quotients, and extensions. The same properties hold for gr-even modules.

Proof.

We will prove the statement for the odd case, since the even case is completely analogous. Let
M
be an gr-odd
\mathbf{Q}[G]
-module and
F_{i}M
a finite filtration realizing this condition. For a quotient
M\twoheadrightarrow Q,
set
F_{i}Q
to be the image of
F_{i}M
under the quotient map. Then
F_{i}Q/F_{i-1}Q
is a quotient of
F_{i}M/F_{i-1}M
and thus, it is odd. For a submodule
N
, take
F_{i}N
to be the kernel of
F_{i}M\to F_{i}(M/N)
as just defined. We see that
F_{i}N/F_{i-1}N
is a submodule of
F_{i}M/F_{i-1}M
and the claim follows. Let now
0\to M^{\prime}\to M\to M^{\prime\prime}\to 0
be an extension of gr-odd modules
M^{\prime}
and
M^{\prime\prime}.
By first filtering
M^{\prime},
we may assume without loss of generality that
M^{\prime}
is itself is odd. Then setting
F_{i}M
to be the pullback of
M\to M^{\prime\prime}
over
F_{i}M^{\prime\prime}
for
i\geq 1
and
F_{0}M=M^{\prime},
we have an exact sequence
0\to M^{\prime}\to F_{i}M\to F_{i}M^{\prime\prime}\to 0
, so we see that the associated graded is odd.
∎

One can easily see that the classes of gr-odd and gr-even modules are not closed under tensor products over
\mathbf{Q}
and mapping spaces. However, the next proposition shows that their interplay behaves as expected.

Notation 3.4.3
.

Given two
\mathbf{Q}[G]
-modules
A
and
B,
we consider
A\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}B
as a
\mathbf{Q}[G]
-module with the diagonal action. Similarly, we consider
\Hom_{\mathbf{Q}}(A,B)
as a
\mathbf{Q}[G]
-module with the action by conjugation.

Proposition 3.4.4
.

Let
A
and
B
be
\mathbf{Q}[G]
-modules with
A
gr-odd. Then if
B
is gr-odd (resp. gr-even) then both
A\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}B
and
\Hom_{\mathbf{Q}}(A,B)
are gr-even (resp. gr-odd). The analogous statement for
A
gr-even also holds.

Proof.

We will assume that
A
and
B
are both gr-odd and will prove that
A\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}B
and
\Hom_{\mathbf{Q}}(A,B)
are gr-even. The remaining cases are analogous. We start with the tensor product. We will proceed by induction on the filtration length witnessing the gr-oddness of
B.
The base case is when
B
is odd. Let
F_{*}A
be an odd filtration of
A,
then by exactness of
(-)\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}B,
we can define a filtration of
\mathbf{Q}[G]
-modules
F_{i}(A\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}B)\coloneq F_{i}A\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}B
. Once again by the exactness of the tensor product, we have

F_{i}(A\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}B)/F_{i-1}(A\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}B)\cong(F_{i}A/F_{i-1}A)\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}B

as
\mathbf{Q}[G]
-modules. One can easily see by bilinearity that the tensor product of two odd modules is even. Assume now that the claim holds for all gr-odd modules
B^{\prime}
with odd filtration length
\leq n.
Let
B
be an gr-odd module along with an odd filtration
F_{i}B
of length
n+1.
Observe that we have a short exact sequence of
\mathbf{Q}[G]
-modules

0\to A\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}F_{n}B\to A\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}B\to A\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}(B/F_{n}B)\to 0.

Both leftmost and rightmost modules are gr-even by the induction hypothesis and the base case, respectively. The claim follows from
3.4.2
. The case of
\Hom_{\mathbf{Q}}(A,B)
follows the same strategy, since
\Hom_{\mathbf{Q}}(-,-)
is exact in both variables and that
\Hom_{\mathbf{Q}}(A,B)
is even when
A
and
B
are odd.
∎

3.4.1
Vanishing results and the centre kills trick

In this subsubsection, we establish the key property of gr-odd modules. This property may be viewed as a graded analogue of the classical
centre kills trick
from group homology. The idea of using this argument was inspired by
[
56
, Lem. A.3]
.

Proposition 3.4.5
.

If
M
is a gr-odd
\mathbf{Q}[G]
-module, then the group
\H_{i}(G;M)
vanishes for all
i\geq 0
. The same conclusion holds for cohomology.

Proof.

We will prove this statement by induction on the odd filtration length of
M
. The base case is, once again, when
M
is odd. Let
L
be a finite normal subgroup of
G
such that
\iota
is in the center of
Q\coloneq G/L
. Notice that the
L
-coinvariants of
M
form a
\mathbf{Q}[Q]
-module. By the
"centre kills trick"
(see, e.g.,
[
30
, Lemma 5.4]
for a proof), since the element
\iota\in Q
is central, the group
\H_{i}(Q;[M]_{L})
is
2
-torsion. On the other hand, it is a rational vector space so it vanishes. Consider the Lyndon–Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence

E^{2}_{s,t}=\H_{s}(Q;\H_{t}(L;M))\Rightarrow\H_{s+t}(G;M)

for the extension
1\to L\to G\to Q\to 1.
By finiteness of
L,
the only non-trivial entries are of the form
E^{2}_{s,0}=\H_{s}(Q;[M]_{L}).
But these vanish and therefore so do
\H_{i}(G;M).
Assume now that the claim holds for
M^{\prime}
with odd filtration length
\leq k
. Let
F_{i}M
be an odd filtration of
M
of length
k+1
. Then the long exact sequence on homology groups of the extension
0\to F_{k}M\to M\to M/F_{k}M\to 0
implies the claim.
∎

3.4.2
The element
-\id

In order to use the vanishing results from above, we choose a specific
\iota
for the mapping class groups considered in this section, satisfying the hypothesis of
3.4.1
.

Lemma 3.4.6
.

The subgroup
\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext},\ell}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})\subset\GL(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})
contains the element
-\id
for all stable framings
\ell
.

Proof.

We know that
-\id\in\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})
as it lies in the image of the splitting
\GL(\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})\to\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})
. Recall from
3.2.3
that we have the following pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext},\ell}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext},\ell}(\mathbf{Z},\Lambda_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathbf{Z},\Lambda_{n})}

so it suffices to show that
-\id\in\GL(\smash{H_{W_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})
is contained in
\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext},\ell}(\mathbf{Z},\Lambda_{n})
. This is exactly
[
57
, Lem. 3.9]
.
∎

Notation 3.4.7
.

We fix, once and for all, an element
\iota_{Y}\in\pi_{1}(\BDiff^{\mathrm{sfr}}_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g}),\ell)
lifting
-\id\in\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext},\ell}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})
, which is possible by
3.2.3
and the fact that
\smash{\pi_{1}(\BDiff^{\mathrm{sfr}}_{\partial^{v}}(Y_{g}),\ell)\to\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}
is surjective. Denote also the image of
\iota_{Y}
in
\smash{\pi_{1}(\BDiffsfr(X_{g}),\ell)}
by
\iota_{X}
. Since
-\id
is central in
\GL(\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}})
, it is so too in
\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext},\ell}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})
. The groups
\smash{\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}
and
\smash{\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}}
along with the elements
\iota_{Y}
and
\iota_{X}
satisfy the hypothesis of
3.4.1
by
3.2.3
and
3.1.1
. When the context is clear, we supress the subscripts of
\iota_{X}
and
\iota_{Y}
.

4
Self-embedding spaces of
X_{g}

A major ingredient in the approach of Bustamante–Randal-Williams is the computation of the rational homotopy type of
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}
in the range
*\leq n-2
. In this section, we extend this computation to degrees up to approximately
*\leq 2n-2
, the main result being
4.5.2
. In
Section

4.6
, we use this last result to analyse the homotopy type of the plus construction
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{\infty})^{+}_{\ell}
appearing in the Weiss fibre sequence (
6.1.1
); the conclusion is
4.6.4
. We now give a brief overview this section.

In order to study the rational homotopy type of the space
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}
, we consider the fibre sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})_{\ell},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

(4.0.1)

where the left hand term denotes the homotopy fibre of
\BEmbop(X_{g})\to\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\cong}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})
, and hence that of
\iota
too. By Proposition
2.1.1
, the base space is rationally equivalent to
\BAutbp(X_{g})
, whose higher homotopy groups are described in
[
22
, Thm. 3.1]
. By Morlet’s lemma, the fibre is
(n-2)
-connected
[
22
, Prop. 4.3]
. The main input that allows us to extend the understanding of this homotopy type up to degrees
*\leq 2n-2
is the work of the second-named author in
[
66
]
, which we recall in
Section

4.1
for convenience. The computation of this left-hand term—outlined in
Section

4.1.6
—will be carried out in Sections
4.2
and
4.3
.

We also need to deal with the connecting homomorphisms in the long exact sequence of the fibration (
4.0.1
). For this, note that for each positive integer
d\in\mathbf{N}
, the circle
S^{1}
is its own
d
-fold cover and, similarly,
X_{dg}
is the corresponding
d
-fold cover of
X_{g}
for every
g\geq 0
. This observation gives rise to
Frobenius maps

\begin{array}[]{lc}\varphi_{d}:\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}\longrightarrow\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{dg})_{\ell},&\\[6.0pt]
\varphi_{d}:\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}\longrightarrow\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{dg})_{\ell},&\quad g\geq 0,\quad d\in\mathbf{N},\\[4.0pt]
\varphi_{d}:\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\longrightarrow\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{dg}),&\end{array}

(4.0.2)

given by “lifting” a self-embedding of
X_{g}
to
X_{dg}
. These maps are natural in
g\geq 0
, satisfy that the composite
\varphi_{d}\circ\varphi_{d^{\prime}}
is homotopic to
\varphi_{d\cdot d^{\prime}}
for every
d,d^{\prime}\in\mathbf{N}
, and are compatible with the fibre sequences (
4.0.1
) for varying
g\geq 0
. In particular, the connecting homomorphisms

\delta_{*}:\pi_{*+1}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})_{\ell})\longrightarrow\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\right)

(4.0.3)

are compatible with the homomorphisms induced by the Frobenius maps (
4.0.2
)—this additional compatibility will allow us to determine these connecting homomorphisms in
Section

4.5
(it will turn out that, for our purposes, we will only need to understand
\delta_{n}
). The analysis of the Frobenius homomorphisms on the domain and codomain of (
4.0.3
) will take place in
Section

4.4
.

Finally, in order to understand the homotopy type of the plus construction
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{\infty})_{\ell}^{+}
, we will need to compute its homology and, consequently, that of
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}
. In
Section

4.6
, we convert the knowledge we
have of its homotopy groups into information about its homology. We will need to understand the action of

\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}=\pi_{1}(\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell})
on the higher homotopy groups of the base and the fibre in (
4.0.1
) to do this. The analysis of this mapping class group action on the fibre takes place in
Section

4.3
.

4.1
Recollections on pseudoisotopy theory

The purpose of this section is to recall some results and constructions from
[
39
,
76
,
72
,
66
]
that we will need later. We aim to keep the exposition concise, but readers already familiar with the contents of these references may safely skip this section and consult it only when referenced in the text.

4.1.1
The Weiss–Williams theorems

The original Weiss–Williams theorem
[
76
]
aims to describe the homotopy type of the pseudoisotopy space
\big(\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}/\Diff\big)_{\partial}(M)\coloneq\hofib(\BDiff_{\partial}(M)\to\BbDiffb(M))
of a compact, smooth
d
-manifold
M
. To do so, the authors introduced certain (Borel)
C_{2}
-spectrum
\mathbf{H}(M)
—the
h-cobordism spectrum
of
M
—which is non-connective and natural in codimension-zero embeddings. Its
1
-connective cover
\mathbf{H}^{s}(M)
is known as the
simple

h
-cobordism spectrum. Then, they showed that there is a map

\Phi^{\Diff}_{M}:\big(\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}/\Diff\big)_{\partial}(M)\longrightarrow\Omega^{\infty}\big(\mathbf{H}^{s}(M)_{hC_{2}}\big)

(4.1.1)

that is
(\phi(M)+1)
-connected, where
\phi(M)
denotes the concordance stable range of
M
.

We now recall an analogue of this theorem for embedding spaces that was established in
[
66
, Thm. A]
. Fix a compact, codimension-zero (for simplicity), neat submanifold
\iota:P\hookrightarrow M
. The goal now is to describe the homotopy type of the
pseudoisotopy embedding space

\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(P,M)\coloneq\hofib_{\iota}\left(\Embo(P,M)\to\bEmbo(P,M)\right).

Write
\nu P
for an open tubular neighbourhood of
P
in
M
, and consider the induced embedding of compact manifolds
M-\nu P\hookrightarrow M
. The
concordance embedding spectrum
of
\iota:P\hookrightarrow M
is the
C_{2}
-spectrum

\mathbf{CE}(P,M)\coloneq\hofib(\mathbf{H}(M-\nu P)\to\mathbf{H}(M)).

(4.1.2)

Then, there exists a map

\Phi^{\Emb}_{(P,M)}:\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(P,M)\longrightarrow\Omega^{\infty}\big(\mathbf{CE}(P,M)_{hC_{2}}\big)

(4.1.3)

which is
\phi_{\CEmb}(P,M)
-connected if
d\geq 4
and the handle dimension
p
of
P
relative to
\partial_{0}P\coloneq\partial P\cap\partial M
satisfies
p\leq d-3
. Here,
\phi_{\CEmb}(P,M)
is the concordance embedding stable range of
\iota:P\hookrightarrow M
. By
[
38
]
,

\phi_{\CEmb}(P,M)\geq 2d-p-5\quad\text{if}\quad p\leq d-3.

(4.1.4)

Moreover, the maps
\Phi^{\Diff}_{(-)}
and
\Phi^{\Emb}_{(-)}
of (
4.1.1
) and (
4.1.3
) give rise to a map of fibre sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}/\Diff\big)_{\partial}(M-\nu P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}/\Diff\big)_{\partial}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\big(\mathbf{H}^{s}(M-\nu P)_{hC_{2}}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\big(\mathbf{H}^{s}(M)_{hC_{2}}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(\mathbf{CE}(P,M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\big(\mathbf{H}(M-\nu P)_{hC_{2}}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\big(\mathbf{H}(M)_{hC_{2}}\big),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\Emb}_{(P,M)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\Diff}_{M-\nu P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\Diff}_{M}}

where the top fibre sequence arises as the homotopy fibre of the map between the ordinary and block isotopy extension fibre sequences, and the bottom right subsquare is cartesian as long as
M-\nu P\to M
is
2
-connected (cf.
[
76
, Cor. 5.6]
)—this is the case under the assumption
p\leq d-3
.

4.1.2
The map
\Phi^{\Emb}
and orthogonal calculus

We will need to understand the particular construction of the map
\Phi^{\Emb}_{(P,M)}
, which we now recall. For this, we will use Weiss’
orthogonal calculus

[
77
]
, which studies functors
\mathcal{J}\to\mathrm{Spc}_{*}
from the (
\infty
-)category
\mathcal{J}
of inner product vector spaces and linear isometric embeddings between them, to based spaces
\mathrm{Spc}_{*}
. The only thing we will need to recall from this theory is that, to each such orthogonal functor
F:\mathcal{J}\to\mathrm{Spc}_{*}
, there is associated a
C_{2}=\mathrm{O}(1)
-spectrum
\Theta F^{(1)}
—the
first derivative of

F
—and a diagram of orthogonal functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{1}F(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\left((S^{\sigma\cdot(-)}\otimes\Theta F^{(1)})_{h\mathrm{O}(1)}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{0}F(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip-70.0ptF(\mathbf{R}^{\infty}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{1}}

(4.1.5)

where the indicated blue arrows form a fibre sequence of orthogonal functors. Here,

•

\sigma
is the one-dimensional
\mathrm{O}(1)
-sign representation,
S^{\sigma\cdot V}
denotes the one point compactification of
\sigma\cdot V\coloneq\sigma\otimes V
, and
\mathrm{O}(1)
acts diagonally on the smash
S^{\sigma\cdot V}\otimes\Theta F^{(1)}
;

•

the zeroth stage
T_{0}F(-)
is given by

T_{0}F(V)\coloneq\operatorname{hocolim}_{k}F(V\oplus\mathbf{R}^{k})
, and thus admits a canonical equivalence from the constant orthogonal functor with
value at infinity

F(\mathbf{R}^{\infty})\coloneq\operatorname{hocolim}_{k}F(\mathbf{R}^{k})
. The map
\eta_{0}:F(V)\to T_{0}F(V)
is simply the inclusion map;

•

a model of the
\mathrm{O}(1)
-spectrum
\Theta F^{(1)}
is as follows: the space
F^{(1)}(V)\coloneq\hofib(F(V)\to F(V\oplus\mathbf{R}))
inherits an
\mathrm{O}(1)
-action by declaring
-1\in\mathrm{O}(1)
to act on
V
and
V\oplus\mathbf{R}
by
-1
on all coordinates. There are
\mathrm{O}(1)
-equivariant maps
s_{V}:S^{1}\wedge F^{(1)}(V)\longrightarrow F^{(1)}(V\oplus\mathbf{R})
given, roughly, by performing a 180º rotation of
V\oplus\mathbf{R}^{2}
about the
2
-plane
0\oplus\mathbf{R}^{2}
(cf.
[
66
, §A.1]
for an explicit description). The
\mathrm{O}(1)
-(pre-)spectrum
\Theta F^{(1)}
has
F^{(1)}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
as its
n
-th space, and
s_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}
as the structure map.

Given such an orthogonal functor
F:\mathcal{J}\to\mathrm{Spc}_{*}
, there is a map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{F}_{\infty}:F^{(1)}(\mathbf{R}^{\infty})\coloneq\hofib(F(0)\to F(\mathbf{R}^{\infty}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hofib(T_{1}F(0)\to T_{1}F(\mathbf{R}^{\infty}))\simeq\Omega^{\infty}\big(\Theta F^{(1)}_{h\mathrm{O}(1)}\big),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{1}}

(4.1.6)

where the second equivalence follows from the observation that
T_{1}F(\mathbf{R}^{\infty})\to T_{0}F(\mathbf{R}^{\infty})=F(\mathbf{R}^{\infty})
is an equivalence (for the infinite-dimensional sphere
S^{\sigma\cdot\mathbf{R}^{\infty}}
is contractible). The map
\Phi^{\Emb}_{(P,M)}
of (
4.1.3
) is
\Phi^{F}_{\infty}
for some appropriate orthogonal functor
F
that we now introduce.

Definition 4.1.1
.

(i)

For
Q
a compact smooth manifold, consider the orthogonal functor

\mathsf{Bd}_{Q}:\mathcal{J}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Spc}_{*},\quad\mathsf{Bd}_{Q}(V)\coloneq\BDiff^{b}_{\partial}(Q\times V),

where
\Diff^{b}_{\partial}(Q\times V)
is the group-like topological monoid of diffeomorphisms of
Q\times V
(rel boundary) which are bounded in the
V
-direction (see e.g.
[
66
, Defn. 2.4]
). Weiss and Williams define the
h
-cobordism spectrum
\mathbf{H}(Q)
as the first orthogonal derivative of
\mathsf{Bd}_{Q}
:

\mathbf{H}(Q)\coloneq\Theta\mathsf{Bd}_{Q}^{(1)}.

(4.1.7)

The involution
\iota_{\H}
on
\mathbf{H}(Q)
inhereted from the
\mathrm{O}(1)
-action on
\Theta\mathsf{Bd}_{Q}^{(1)}
is the
h-cobordism involution
.

(ii)

A codimension-zero embedding
Q\hookrightarrow Q^{\prime}
induces a natural transformation
\mathsf{Bd}_{Q}\to\mathsf{Bd}_{Q^{\prime}}
. We set

\mathsf{E}_{(P,M)}\coloneq\hofib(\mathsf{Bd}_{M-\nu P}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Bd}_{M}),\quad\text{so that}\quad\Theta\mathsf{E}_{(P,M)}^{(1)}=\mathbf{CE}(P,M).

(4.1.8)

We will also write
\iota_{\H}
for the involution on
\mathbf{CE}(P,M)
inhereted from the
\mathrm{O}(1)
-action on
\Theta\mathsf{E}_{(P,M)}^{(1)}
.

Consider now the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BbDiffb(M-\nu P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BbDiff^{b}_{\partial}((M-\nu P)\times\mathbf{R}^{\infty})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BDiff^{b}_{\partial}((M-\nu P)\times\mathbf{R}^{\infty})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bd}_{M-\nu P}(\mathbf{R}^{\infty})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BbDiffb(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BbDiff^{b}_{\partial}(M\times\mathbf{R}^{\infty})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BDiff^{b}_{\partial}(M\times\mathbf{R}^{\infty})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bd}_{M}(\mathbf{R}^{\infty}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

(4.1.9)

where
\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}_{\partial}^{b}(Q\times V)
is the topological monoid of bounded (in the
V
-direction) block diffeomorphisms of
Q\times V
(cf.
[
66
, Defn. 2.8]
). That the middle horizontal maps are equivalences is
[
76
, Thm. B]
, and the left subsquare is homotopy cartesian if
M-\nu P\to M
is
2
-connected by
[
76
, Cor. 5.5]
. Thus, taking vertical homotopy fibres, we see that

\smash{\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\cong}_{\partial_{0}}}(P,M)\simeq\mathsf{E}_{(P,M)}(\mathbf{R}^{\infty})\quad\text{if $p\leq d-3$.}

(4.1.10)

Definition 4.1.2
.

The map
\Phi^{\Emb}_{(P,M)}
of (
4.1.3
) is the map
\Phi^{F}_{\infty}
of (
4.1.6
) for
F=\mathsf{E}_{(P,M)}
, which takes the form

\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(P,M)\overset{\eqref{bEmbAsT0}}{\simeq}\hofib(\mathsf{E}_{(P,M)}(0)\to\mathsf{E}_{(P,M)}(\mathbf{R}^{\infty}))\xrightarrow{\Phi^{\mathsf{E}_{(P,M)}}_{\infty}}\Omega^{\infty}\big(\Theta(\mathsf{E}_{(P,M)})^{(1)}_{h\mathrm{O}(1)}\big)\overset{\eqref{CEspDefn}}{=}\Omega^{\infty}\big(\mathbf{CE}(P,M)_{C_{2}}\big).

Remark 4.1.3
.

The original Weiss–Williams map
\Phi^{\Diff}_{M}
of (
4.1.1
) is not quite
\Phi^{\mathsf{Bd}_{M}}_{\infty}
, but they are related: using obstruction theory
[
76
, §4]
, they showed that the dashed arrow in the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\frac{\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}}{\Diff}\right)_{\partial}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}^{b}_{\partial}(M\times\mathbf{R}^{\infty})}{\Diffb(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{\Diff^{b}_{\partial}(M\times\mathbf{R}^{\infty})}{\Diffb(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\left(\mathbf{H}^{s}(M)_{hC_{2}}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\left(\mathbf{H}(M)_{hC_{2}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\Diff}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\mathsf{Bd}_{M}}_{\infty}}

exists—this map is, by definition,
\Phi^{\Diff}_{M}
.

4.1.3
On the homotopy type of
\mathbf{H}(M)
and
\mathbf{CE}(P,M)

Ignoring the
C_{2}
-actions on
\mathbf{H}(M)
and
\mathbf{CE}(P,M)
, the homotopy types of these spectra are closely related to Waldhausen’s algebraic
K
-theory—in this section, we recall this connection.

For
Q
a compact smooth manifold, let
\H(Q)
denote the
space of (smooth)
h
-cobordisms starting at
Q
(cf.
[
71
, p. 296]
). For instance, by the
s
-cobordism theorem, if
\dim Q\geq 5
, then the set of path components
\pi_{0}(\H(Q))
is in bijection with the Whitehead group
\mathrm{Wh}(\pi_{1}Q)
of the fundamental group(oid) of
Q
. The codimension-zero embedding
M-\nu P\hookrightarrow M
induces an “extension-by-the-identity” map
\H(M-\nu P)\to\H(M)
, which, as long as
d\geq 5
and
d-p\geq 3
7
7

7

In general, (
4.1.11
) is a fibre sequence upon looping the total space and the base.
, features in an isotopy extension fibre sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CEmb(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H(M-\nu P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H(M).}

(4.1.11)

Here
\CEmb(P,M)
denotes the space of

—that is, the space of all embeddings

for

, and

(ii)

P\times\{0\}\cup\partial_{0}P\times[0,1]
.

Under the usual handle codimension assumption
p\leq d-3
, the space
\CEmb(P,M)
is connected by Hudson’s Theorem
[
45
]
. Let us assume this condition from now on.

There are stabilisation maps

\Sigma_{\H}:\H(Q)\longrightarrow\H(Q\times I),\qquad\Sigma_{\CEmb}:\CEmb(P,M)\longrightarrow\CEmb(P\times I,M\times I)

(4.1.12)

roughly given by crossing an

\mathcal{H}(Q)\coloneq\hocolim_{k}\H(Q\times I^{k}),\qquad\mathcal{CE}(P,M)\coloneq\hocolim_{k}\CEmb(P\times I^{k},M\times I^{k}),

(4.1.13)

where the bonding maps in the filtered systems are
\Sigma_{H}
and
\Sigma_{\CEmb}
, respectively. Since the fibre sequence (
4.1.11
) is compatible with these stabilisation maps, we also obtain a stable isotopy extension fibre sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{CE}(P,M)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{CE}(P,M)}

\simeq

\simeq

One of Waldhausen’s most celebrated results, proved together with Jahren and Rognes in
[
72
]
, is the
parametrised stable
s
-cobordism theorem
, which establishes an equivalence

\mathcal{H}(M)\simeq\Omega\Wh(M)\coloneq\hofib\big(Q_{+}M=\Omega^{\infty}\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}M\xrightarrow{\nu}A(M)\big)

(4.1.17)

which is natural in codimension-zero embeddings. Here
A(-)\simeq\Omega^{\infty}\mathrm{K}(\mathrm{Sp}_{(-)})
denotes Waldhausen’s algebraic
K
-theory of spaces—for
M
based connected, we have a natural equivalence
A(M)\simeq\Omega^{\infty}\mathrm{K}(\mathbf{S}[\Omega M])
—and
\nu
is induced by the composition of the unit map
M_{+}\otimes\mathbf{S}\to M_{+}\otimes\mathrm{K}(\mathbf{S})
and the assembly
M_{+}\otimes\mathrm{K}(\mathbf{S})\to\mathrm{K}(\mathrm{Sp}_{M})
; this map is well-known to be split injective. As a consequence of (
4.1.17
) and the fibre sequence (

), we see that the homotopy type of the infinite loop space of the concordance embedding spectrum is given by

\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{CE}(P,M)\simeq\mathcal{CE}(P,M)\simeq\tohofib\Bigg(\hbox to142.58pt{\vbox to46.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 71.28946pt\lower-23.31946pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
