(4.1.18)

In the next section, we recall how to describe the right-hand term in terms of simpler trace invariants.

4.1.4
Trace invariants and Goodwillie’s isomorphism

Trace methods
are concerned with the study of
topological cyclic homology

[
10
,
67
]
, denoted
\mathrm{TC}(-)
, and related invariants as an approximation to algebraic
K
-theory. The invariants that will be relevant to us feature in the following commutative diagram of functors from
\mathbf{E}_{1}
-ring spectra, say, to spectra:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{K}(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{TC}(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{HC}^{-}(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{HH}(-).}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma\mathrm{HC}(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Trc}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Tr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Trc}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}

(4.1.19)

Here
\mathrm{HH}(-)
denotes
Hochschild homology
,
\mathrm{HC}(-)
is
cyclic homology
and
\mathrm{HC}^{-}(-)
is
negative cyclic homology
. The map
\mathrm{Trc}
is the cyclotomic trace map of Bökstedt–Hsiang–Madsen,
\mathrm{Tr}
is the Dennis trace map, and
N
is the
S^{1}
-norm map under the well-known equivalences
\mathrm{HC}(-)\simeq\mathrm{HH}(-)_{hS^{1}}
and
\mathrm{HC}^{-}(-)\simeq\mathrm{HH}(-)^{hS^{1}}

[
58
]
. These invariants will be relevant to us because of the following remarkable property: given a ring map
R\to S
such that
\pi_{0}(R)\to\pi_{0}(S)
is surjective with kernel a nilpotent ideal, then the squares in the following diagram are cartesian as indicated:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{K}(R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{TC}(R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{HC}^{-}(R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma\mathrm{HC}(R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{K}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{TC}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{HC}^{-}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma\mathrm{HC}(S).}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner_{\mathbf{Q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathbf{Q}}\llcorner}

(4.1.20)

That the leftmost square is cartesian is the celebrated theorem of Dundas–Goodwillie–McCarthy
[
28
]
. Earlier, Goodwillie
[
39
]
had shown that the right-hand subsquare, as well as the combined square obtained from the left and middle subsquares, are cartesian after rationalisation.

We are only concerned with ring spectra of the form
R=\mathbf{S}[\Omega X]
, i.e. the spherical group ring of
\Omega X
, for a connected based space
X
; in this case, the Hochschild homology
\mathrm{HH}(\mathbf{S}[\Omega X])
is equivalent to
\mathrm{H}\mathbf{Z}\otimes LX_{+}
, the (integral) homology of the free loop space of
X
. In particular, it also follows that

\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathbf{S}[\Omega X])\cong\H_{*}(LX)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{HC}_{*}(\mathbf{S}[\Omega X])\cong\H_{*}^{S^{1}}(LX).

(4.1.21)

To conclude, let
\iota:P\hookrightarrow M
with
M
connected and such that
p\leq d-3
, so that the map of ring spectra
(R\to S)=(\mathbf{S}[\Omega(M-P)]\to\mathbf{S}[\Omega M])
is
1
-connected. Then, by (
4.1.18
), (
4.1.20
) and (
4.1.21
), we obtain

\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathbf{CE}(P,M))\cong\frac{\H^{S^{1}}_{*+1}(LM,L(M-P);\mathbf{Q})}{\H_{*+2}(M,M-P;\mathbf{Q})}.

(4.1.22)

Here, the right-hand side really stands for the cokernel of the homomorphism induced on
\H_{*+2}(-;\mathbf{Q})
by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{\infty}(M/M-P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\Sigma^{\infty}(LM/L(M-P))\right)^{hS^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\Sigma^{\infty+1}(LM/L(M-P))\right)_{hS^{1}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq_{\mathbf{Q}}}

(4.1.23)

where
c
is induced by the (
S^{1}
-equivariant) inclusion
(M,M-P)\to(LM,L(M-P))
as constant loops, and hence is split injective (with left inverse induced by the evaluation map
\mathrm{ev}_{1}:LX\to X
).

Our computation of the rational homotopy groups of the pseudoisotopy embedding space
\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(P,M)
will crucially use the isomorphism (
4.1.22
). However, since the codomain of the Weiss–Williams map
\Phi^{\Emb}_{(P,M)}
in (
4.1.3
) is the infinite loop space of
\mathbf{CE}(P,M)_{hC_{2}}
rather than that of
\mathbf{CE}(P,M)
itself, we will need to trace the effect of the involution
\iota_{\H}
on
\mathbf{CE}(P,M)
through the isomorphism (
4.1.22
)—we do this in the next section.

4.1.5
The involution on
\mathbf{CE}(P,M)

By the homotopy orbits spectral sequence, given a finite group
G
and
G
-spectrum
X
, the rational homotopy groups
\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{hG})
of the homotopy orbit space of
X
are isomorphic to the
G
-coinvariants
[\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X)]_{G}
of the rational homotopy groups of
X
. Thus, in order to compute the rational homotopy groups of
\Omega^{\infty}(\mathbf{CE}(P,M)_{hC_{2}})
, which is our goal, it suffices to undertand the effect of the
h
-cobordism involution on (the rational homotopy groups of)
\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{CE}(P,M)\simeq\mathcal{CE}(P,M)
. We now briefly recall how this was done in
[
71
]
and
[
66
, §5]
.

The first step is to introduce an involution on the stable spaces
\mathcal{H}(Q)
and
\mathcal{CE}(P,M)
that can be compared to the concordance involution
\iota_{\H}
on
\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{H}(Q)
and
\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{CE}(P,M)
, respectively. This was done in
[
71
, §2]
, who constructed geometric involutions on the
h
-cobordism spaces
\H(Q\times I^{k})
which assembled to a (homotopy) involution on
\mathcal{H}(Q)
—we shall also write
\iota_{\H}
for these involutions on
\H(Q\times I^{n})
and
\mathcal{H}(Q)
, as in
[
66
, Prop. 5.8]
they were shown to be compatible (up to homotopy) with the one on
\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{H}(Q)
. Equipping
\CEmb(P,M)\simeq\hofib(\H(M-\nu P)\to\H(M))
and
\mathcal{CE}(P,M)\simeq\hofib(\mathcal{H}(M-\nu P)\to\mathcal{H}(M))
with the induced involutions
\iota_{\H}
on the homotopy fibre, it also follows that the equivalence
\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{CE}(P,M)\simeq\mathcal{CE}(P,M)
is (homotopy)
C_{2}
-equivariant. We record some constructions and facts related to
\iota_{\H}
that we will need:

(1)

Here is an explicit model for the involution
\iota_{\H}
on the
h
-cobordism space
\H(Q)
: Vogell introduced in
[
71
, §2]
a model of
\H(Q)
in terms of
partitions
—a zero-simplex in
\H(Q)
is a triple
(W,F,V)
giving rise to a decomposition
Q\times[-1,1]=W\cup_{F}V
, where
W
and
V
are compact codimension-zero submanifolds of
Q\times[-1,1]
which are
h
-cobordisms
W:M\times\{-1\}\leadsto F
and
V:F\leadsto M\times\{1\}
, and
F
is a codimension-one sumanifold of
Q\times[-1,1]
meeting
\partial Q\times\{0\}
transversely. A
p
-simplex in
\H(Q)
is a bundle of such triples over
\Delta^{p}
. Then,
\iota_{\H}:\H(Q)\to\H(Q)
is induced by the reflection
r
on
[-1,1]
, i.e., it sends a partition
(W,F,Q)
to
(r(V),r(F),r(W))
, its image under
\Id_{Q}\times r:Q\times[-1,1]\cong Q\times[-1,1]
. With this model, it is evident that
\iota_{\H}
is natural in codimension-zero embeddings, and thus induces an involution
\iota_{\H}
on the homotopy fibre
\CEmb(P,M)\simeq\hofib(\H(M-\nu P)\to\H(M))
—we will explicitly model the
h
-cobordism involution
\iota_{\H}
on
\CEmb(P,M)
in
Section

A.1
.

(2)

The
h
-cobordism involution
\iota_{\H}
on the space
\H(Q)
admits an alternative simpler description on its (based) loop space. Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{C}(Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H(Q),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

(4.1.24)

where
\operatorname{C}(Q)\cong\Diff_{Q\times\{-1\}\cup\partial Q\times[-1,0]}(Q\times[-1,0])
is the group of concordance diffeomorphisms and
E(Q)
is the space of embeddings of
Q\times[-1,0]
into
Q\times[-1,1]
that are fixed on a neighbourhood of
Q\times\{-1\}\cup\partial Q\times[-1,0]
. The map
p
is given by sending such an embedding
\varphi
to the partition
(\varphi(Q\times[-1,0]),\varphi(Q\times 0),Q\times[-1,1]-\varphi(Q\times[-1,0)))
. The map
a
is the action map by precomposition on the identity embedding. The space
E(Q)
is contractible for it is a space of collars, and, by
[
71
, Prop. 2.1]
, this diagram is in fact a fibre sequence. Thus, it follows that
\Omega\H(Q)
is equivalent to
\operatorname{C}(Q)
. On
\operatorname{C}(Q)
, there is a natural involution
\iota_{\operatorname{C}}
—the
concordance involution
—which sends a concordance diffeomorphism
F=(f,g):Q\times I\to Q\times I
to
\overline{F}=(\overline{f},\overline{g})
, where

\overline{f}(x,t)=f_{1}^{-1}\circ f(x,1-t),\qquad\overline{g}(x,t)=1-g(x,1-t),\qquad(x,t)\in Q\times I,

(4.1.25)

and
f_{1}=f\mid_{M\times 1}:Q\cong Q
. By
[
71
, Prop. 2.2]
(see also
[
66
, Warn. 5.11]
), the involutions
\iota_{\operatorname{C}}
and
\iota_{\H}
agree on
\operatorname{C}(Q)\simeq\Omega\H(Q)
up to a sign—more precisely, writing
\sigma
for the
C_{2}
-sign representation and
\Omega^{\sigma}(-)\coloneq\Map_{*}(S^{\sigma},-)
for the
\sigma
-twisted loop space, there is a
C_{2}
-equivariant equivalence

\Omega^{\sigma}(\H(Q),\iota_{\H})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}(\operatorname{C}(Q),\iota_{\operatorname{C}}),\quad\text{so $\iota_{\operatorname{C}}=-\iota_{\H}$ on $\pi_{*}(\H(Q))$.}

(4.1.26)

In particular, the restriction map
(\operatorname{C}(M),\iota_{\operatorname{C}})\to(\CEmb(P,M),\iota_{\H})
is anti-equivariant.

(3)

The stabilisation maps
\Sigma:\operatorname{C}(Q)\to\operatorname{C}(Q\times I)
and
\Sigma:\H(Q)\to\H(Q\times I)
are
anti
-equivariant with respect to
\iota_{\operatorname{C}}
and
\iota_{\H}
, respectively (cf.
[
42
, App. I, Lem.]
and
[
66
, Lem. C.1]
). In particular, the maps
\H(Q\times I^{n})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}(Q)\simeq\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{H}(Q)
and
\CEmb(P\times I^{n},M\times I^{n})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{CE}(P,M)\simeq\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{CE}(P,M)
are
(-1)^{n}
-equivariant on homotopy groups when equipping all of the spaces with
\iota_{\H}
.

The second step in understanding
\iota_{\H}
on
\mathbf{CE}(P,M)
is to compare the homotopy involution on
\mathcal{CE}(P,M)
with an involution on the relative algebraic
K
-theory space on the right-hand side of (
4.1.18
). This comparison was carried out by Vogell
[
71
]
, who introduced
the canonical involution

\tau_{\epsilon}
on
\smash{\Omega^{\infty}\mathrm{K}(\mathrm{Sp}_{X})\simeq A(X)}
, natural in
X
, which moreover descends to the smooth Whitehead space
\smash{\Wh(X)}=\Omega^{\infty}\hocofib\big(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}X\xrightarrow{\nu}\mathrm{K}(\mathrm{Sp}_{X})\big)
. One might then hope to compare this canonical involution with involutions on the trace invariants featuring in (
4.1.19
), so to make the isomorphism (
4.1.22
)
C_{2}
-equivariant with respect to
\iota_{\H}
on the left-hand side, and an appropriate involution on the free loop space homology. This is indeed what happens.

Proposition 4.1.5
.

Let
\iota:P\hookrightarrow M
be such that
p\leq d-3
, so that the isomorphism

\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathbf{CE}(P,M))\cong\frac{\H^{S^{1}}_{*+1}(LM,L(M-P);\mathbf{Q})}{\H_{*+2}(M,M-P;\mathbf{Q})}

in (
4.1.22
) holds. Complex conjugation on
S^{1}\subset\mathbf{C}
induces a natural involution
\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}
on the free loop space
LX
, and hence on the homology groups
\H_{*}(LX)
and
\H_{*}^{S^{1}}(LX)
. This involution descends to the quotient on the right-hand side of the above isomorphism. If
M
is connected
9
9

9

Otherwise, deal with each component separately.
, then this isomorphism is
C_{2}
-equivariant for
\iota_{H}
on the left and
(-1)^{d+1}\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}
on the right. Hence, it follows that

\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(P,M))\cong\left[\frac{\H^{S^{1}}_{*+1}(LM,L(M-P);\mathbf{Q})}{\H_{*+2}(M,M-P;\mathbf{Q})}\right]_{(-1)^{d+1}\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}}\quad\text{for $*\leq 2d-p-6$.}

Proof.

First note the free loop space
LX
is naturally an
\mathrm{O}(2)
-space, and that
\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}
on
\H_{*}^{S^{1}}(LX)
is induced by the residual
\mathrm{O}(2)/S^{1}
-action on the Borel construction
(LX)_{hS^{1}}
. Now, for a
2
-connected map
X\to Y
of connected, based spaces, Goodwillie’s isomorphism (
4.1.20
) for the ring map
\mathbf{S}[\Omega X]\to\mathbf{S}[\Omega Y]
becomes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}A(X,Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{HC}^{-}_{*}(\mathbf{S}[\Omega X],\mathbf{S}[\Omega Y];\mathbf{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{HC}_{*-1}(\mathbf{S}[\Omega X],\mathbf{S}[\Omega Y];\mathbf{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H^{S^{1}}_{*-1}(LX,LY;\mathbf{Q}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Trc}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

(4.1.27)

This isomorphism can be made
C_{2}
-equivariant as follows: given a (simplicial) ring
R
with anti-involution (e.g.
R=\mathbf{Q}[\Omega X]\simeq_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbf{S}[\Omega X]
with the flip
C_{2}
-action), the ordinary and negative cyclic homologies of
R
,
\mathrm{HC}_{*}(R)
and
\mathrm{HC}^{-}_{*}(R)
, inherit canonical involutions (cf.
[
64
, §5.2]
and
[
65
, p. 177]
). By
[
65
, Lem. 1.8]
, the trace map
\mathrm{Trc}^{-}
of (
4.1.27
) is equivariant with respect to the canonical involutions on both sides, while the map
N:\mathrm{HC}_{*-1}(R)\to\mathrm{HC}_{*}^{-}(R)
is anti-equivariant
[
65
, p. 195]
(i.e. equivariant up to a minus sign)—alternatively, this is explained by the fact that
\Sigma\mathrm{HC}(\mathbf{S}[\Omega X])
on the domain of the
S^{1}
-norm map should really be
\mathrm{H}\mathbf{Z}\otimes(S^{\operatorname{ad}S^{1}}\otimes\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}LX)_{hS^{1}}
, and the residual
C_{2}=\mathrm{O}(2)/S^{1}
-action on the adjoint representation is that of the sign representation. Finally, the isomorphism
\mathrm{HC}_{*}(\Omega X)\cong\H^{S^{1}}_{*}(LX;\mathbf{Q})
is equivariant with respect to the canonical involution on
\mathrm{HC}
and
\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}
on the right; this follows by a generalisation of
[
64
, Cor. 7.3.14]
and the results in Section 7 loc.cit., using the work of Dunn
[
29
]
. Thus, the canonical involution on
\mathrm{HC}^{-}_{*}(\mathbf{S}[\Omega X],\mathbf{S}[\Omega Y];\mathbf{Q})
agrees with the FLS involution
\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}
on the rational homotopy groups of
\left(\Sigma^{\infty}(LM/L(M-P))\right)^{hS^{1}}
. The map
c
of (
4.1.23
) is equivariant for the trivial
C_{2}
-action on the domain and
\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}
on the codomain, so it follows that
\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}
descends to the quotient in the isomorphism (
4.1.22
).

To conclude, we recall that for each spherical fibration
\xi
over
X
, Vogell defined a natural involution
\tau_{\xi}
on the
A
-theory space
A(X)
(which gives
\tau_{\epsilon}
when
\xi=\epsilon
is the trivial
0
-dimensional fibration), with the property that when
X=M
is a smooth
d
-manifold and
\xi
is the spherical fibration associated to the
1
-stabilised tangent bundle
\tau_{M}\oplus\epsilon^{1}
, then the connecting map
(\Omega A(M),\tau_{\xi})\twoheadrightarrow(\mathcal{H}(M),\iota_{H})
of the Waldhausen–Jahren–Rognes fibre sequence is
C_{2}
-equivariant on homotopy groups. But by
[
20
, Lem. 3.2]
,
\tau_{\xi}=(-1)^{d}\tau_{\epsilon}
on rational homotopy groups
\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(A(M))
. It follows that the connecting homomorphism

(\pi_{*+2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(A(M,M-P)),(-1)^{d}\tau_{\epsilon})\twoheadrightarrow(\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathcal{CE}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M)),\iota_{H})\cong(\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathbf{CE}(P,M)),\iota_{H})

is
C_{2}
-equivariant. The anti-equivariance of the norm map
N
in (
4.1.27
) turns
(-1)^{d}\tau_{\epsilon}
into
(-1)^{d+1}\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}
, establishing the first claim. The addendum now follows from the fact that the map
\Phi^{\Emb}_{(P,M)}
in (
4.1.3
) is
(2d-p-5)
-connected by (
4.1.4
), so

\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(P,M))\cong\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\mathbf{CE}(P,M)_{hC_{2}}\right)\cong\left[\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathbf{CE}(P,M))\right]_{\iota_{\H}}\quad\text{for $*\leq 2d-p-6$.}

∎

4.1.6
Pseudoisotopy self-embeddings of
X_{g}

Let us specialise to the case where
\iota:P\hookrightarrow M
is a
fixed self-embedding
\iota:X_{g}\hookrightarrow X_{g}
isotopic to the identity, fixed on
\partial_{0}(X_{g})
and with a fixed identification
S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}\cong X_{g}-\nu(\iota(X_{g}))
rel
\partial_{1}(X_{g})=S^{1}\times D^{2n-2}_{-}
. In this case, the handle dimension of
X_{g}
relative to
\partial_{0}(X_{g})
is
p=n
, which satisfies the handle codimension condition
n=p\leq d-3=2n-3
as
n\geq 3
. In the last sections, we have explained how to understand the (rational) homotopy type of
\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\coloneq\hofib_{\iota}(\Embo(X_{g})\to\bEmbo(X_{g}))
, but we are really after that of
\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})
. However, by Hudson’s theorem
[
45
]
, the map
\Embo(X_{g})\to\bEmbo(X_{g})
is an isomorphism on
\pi_{0}
, and hence

\Omega\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\simeq\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}).

(4.1.28)

Thus, by the conclusion of
4.1.5
, we obtain

\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\right)\cong\left[\frac{\H^{S^{1}}_{*}(LX_{g},LS^{1});\mathbf{Q})}{\H_{*+1}(X_{g},S^{1};\mathbf{Q})}\right]_{-\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}}\quad\text{for $*\leq 3n-5$.}

(4.1.29)

We will need to carry out the following tasks related to the isomorphism above:

§
4.2
.

Compute the right-hand side of (
4.1.29
) (cf.
4.2.3
(iii)).

§
4.3
.

Trace the action of the mapping class group
\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}
and the reflection involution
\rho
of
Section

2.4
on the homotopy groups of
\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})
through the isomorphism (
4.1.29
) (cf.
4.3.14
).

§
4.4
.

Understand the Frobenius homomorphisms of (
4.0.2
) on the left-hand side of (
4.1.29
).

4.2
The
S^{1}
-equivariant homology of
LX_{g}

This section is devoted to computing the right-hand side of (
4.1.29
) in degrees
*\leq 2n-2
. For use in the next section, it is convenient to carry out this computation as a representation of
\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}\coloneq\pi_{0}(\Aut_{*}(X_{g}))
. Since
n\geq 3
, we have an identification
\pi_{0}(\Aut_{*}(X_{g}))\cong\pi_{0}(\Aut_{*}(X_{g},S^{1}))
, and hence
\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}
acts on
\smash{\H^{S^{1}}_{*}(LX_{g},LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})}
and
\H_{*+1}(X_{g},S^{1};\mathbf{Q})
by naturality on the pair
(X_{g},S^{1})
. We begin by introducing some notation.

Notation 4.2.1
.

(i)

All throughout this section and the following,
\otimes
and
\otimes_{\pi}
stand for
\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}
and
\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}[\pi]}
, respectively, and
[A]^{\pi}
(resp.
[A]_{\pi}
) denotes the
\pi
-(co)invariants of a
\mathbf{Q}[\pi]
-module
A
.

(ii)

The main
\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}
-representation we will need to consider is

H_{X_{g}}\coloneq\smash{H_{X_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\otimes\mathbf{Q}\cong\H_{n}(\widetilde{X}_{g};\mathbf{Q}),

as introduced in
2.2.2
. The fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X_{g})=\pi\cong\mathbf{Z}\langle t\rangle
is a normal subgroup of
\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}
. The generator
t
is represented by the homotopy automorphism of
X_{g}\simeq S^{1}\vee\bigvee^{2g}S^{n}
that fixes the
S^{1}
–summand and wraps each of the
S^{n}
–summands once around
S^{1}
. Equivalently, it corresponds to the subgroup
\{+1\}\ltimes\pi\trianglelefteq\{\pm 1\}\ltimes\GL_{2g}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi])\cong\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}
, consisting of diagonal matrices whose diagonal entries are all equal to
t^{i}
for some
i\in\mathbf{Z}
. The restricted
\mathbf{Q}[\pi]
-module structure on
H_{X_{g}}
is visibly induced by the usual action of
\pi_{1}(X_{g})
on its higher homotopy groups, and as
\mathbf{Q}[\pi]
-modules,

H_{X_{g}}\cong\mathbf{Q}[\pi]\otimes H_{g},\quad\text{where $H_{g}\coloneq\H_{n}(W_{g,1};\mathbf{Q})$.}

We will also regard
H_{g}
as a
\mathbf{Q}[\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}]
-module via the evident isomorphism
H_{g}\cong[H_{X_{g}}]_{\pi}
.

(iii)

Given a
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space
V
and
i,k\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we will write
S^{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle(i)}(V)
for the
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space given by the
ki
-graded piece of the free graded commutative
\mathbf{Q}
-algebra on
V
, when
V
is seen as a graded
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space concentrated in degree
i
. For instance, we have

S^{*}_{\scriptscriptstyle(i)}(V)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathrm{Sym}^{*}(V)&\text{if $i$ is even,}\\
\Lambda^{*}(V)&\text{if $i$ is odd,}\end{array}\right.

where
\mathrm{Sym}(W)
and
\Lambda(W)
denote the symmetric and exterior algebras of a
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space
W
.

We begin by computing the ordinary rational homology of the free loop space
LX_{g}
, which decomposes as
LX_{g}=\coprod_{r\in\mathbf{Z}}L_{r}X_{g}
, where
L_{r}X_{g}
is the component of unbased loops representing
t^{r}\in\pi_{1}X_{g}\cong\mathbf{Z}\langle t\rangle
.

Proposition 4.2.2
.

Let
H_{X_{g}}
denote the
\mathbf{Q}[\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}]
-module introduced in
4.2.1
(ii)
.

(i)

For
r\neq 0
, consider the
\mathbf{Q}[\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}]
-modules

A_{r}\coloneq\frac{H_{X_{g}}}{\langle 1-t^{r}\rangle},\qquad B_{r}\coloneq\frac{S^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle(n-1)}(H_{X_{g}})}{S^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle(n-1)}(1-t^{r})}.

The rational homology of
L_{r}X_{g}
in degrees
*\leq 2n-2
is given, as a
\mathbf{Q}[\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}]
-module, by

\H_{*}(L_{r}X_{g};\mathbf{Q})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbf{Q}&*=0,1,\\[3.0pt]
[A_{r}]_{\pi}\cong H_{g}&*=n-1,\\[3.0pt]
[A_{r}]^{\pi}\cong H_{g}\langle 1+t+\dots+t^{r-1}\rangle&*=n,\\[3.0pt]
{\left[S^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle(n-1)}(A_{r})\right]}_{\pi}\oplus[B_{r}]_{\pi}&*=2n-2,\\[3.0pt]
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

(ii)

The rational homology of
L_{0}X_{g}
in degrees
*\leq 2n-2
is given, as a
\mathbf{Q}[\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}]
-module, by

\H_{*}(L_{0}X_{g};\mathbf{Q})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbf{Q}&*=0,1,\\
{[H_{X_{g}}]}_{\pi}\cong H_{g}&*=n-1,n,\\
C_{0}&*=2n-2,\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{array}\right.

where
C_{0}
is some quotient of the
\mathbf{Q}[\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}]
-module
[H_{X_{g}}^{\otimes 2}]_{\pi}
.

Proof.

Let us first deal with the case
r\neq 0
. Consider the fibre sequence

\Omega_{r}X_{g}\longrightarrow L_{r}X_{g}\longrightarrow X_{g},

where
\Omega_{r}X_{g}
is the path-component of the based loop space
\Omega X_{g}
corresponding to
t^{r}
under
\pi_{0}(\Omega X_{g})\cong\mathbf{Z}\langle t\rangle
. Let us also write
t
for a based loop in
X_{g}
representing
t
in
\pi
. One verifies that the connecting map of this fibration is, under the equivalence
t^{r}_{\#}:\Omega_{r}X_{g}\simeq\Omega_{0}X_{g}
and up to a sign, the Whitehead bracket

[t^{r},-]:\pi_{*}X_{g}\longrightarrow\pi_{*}X_{g},\quad x\longmapsto(1-t^{r})\cdot x=x-t^{r}\cdot x.

Moreover, since the universal cover of
X_{g}
is a wedge of spheres
\bigvee_{\mathbf{Z}}\bigvee_{i=1}^{2g}S^{n}
, the rational homotopy groups of
X_{g}
in degrees
*\geq 2
are given, as a graded
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space, by the suspension of the free graded Lie
\mathbf{Q}
-algebra
\mathrm{Lie}^{*}(H_{X_{g}})
of
H_{X_{g}}
, seen as concentrated in degree
n-1
. It follows that the fundamental group of
L_{r}X_{g}
is
\pi
, and that, as graded
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space,

\pi_{*\geq 2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(L_{r}X_{g})\cong A_{r}[n-1]\oplus B_{r}[2n-2]\oplus\ \text{($*\geq 3n-3$)-terms.}

Here we are using the fact that
1-t^{r}\in\mathbf{Q}[\pi]
is a not a zero divisor if
r\neq 0
, that

\mathrm{Lie}^{2}(1-t^{r}):\pi^{\mathbf{Q}}_{2n-1}(X_{g})\longrightarrow\pi^{\mathbf{Q}}_{2n-1}(X_{g}),\quad[x,y]\mapsto[(1-t^{r})x,(1-t^{r})y]

is injective by the same reason, and that
\operatorname{Lie}^{2}(H_{X_{g}}[n-1])\cong S^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle(n-1)}(H_{X_{g}})
.

From this, and observing that the
(2n-2)
-th (rational) Postnikov invariant of the universal cover
\widetilde{L_{r}X_{g}}
lies in a zero group, we deduce that the
(3n-4)
-th (rational) Postnikov trunction of
\smash{\widetilde{L_{r}X_{g}}}
is

\tau_{\leq 3n-4}\widetilde{L_{r}X_{g}}\simeq_{\mathbf{Q}}K(A_{r},n-1)\times K(B_{r},2n-2).

By the fibration sequence
\widetilde{L_{r}X_{g}}\to L_{r}X_{g}\to B\pi\simeq S^{1}
, the homology group
\H_{p+q}(L_{r}X_{g};\mathbf{Q})
is given by

\H_{p}(S^{1};\underline{\H_{q}(\widetilde{L_{r}X_{g}};\mathbf{Q})})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathrm{coker}(1-t:\H_{q}(\widetilde{L_{r}X_{g}};\mathbf{Q})\to\H_{q}(\widetilde{L_{r}X_{g}};\mathbf{Q}))&p=0,\\
\ker(1-t:\H_{q}(\widetilde{L_{r}X_{g}};\mathbf{Q})\to\H_{q}(\widetilde{L_{r}X_{g}};\mathbf{Q}))&p=1\\
0,&\textit{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

The claim in part (i) readily follows.

For
r=0
, consider the spectral sequence associated to the fibration
\Omega_{0}X_{g}\to L_{0}X_{g}\to X_{g}
, with
E^{2}
-page

\H_{p}(X_{g};\underline{\H_{q}(\Omega_{0}X_{g};\mathbf{Q})})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}[\H_{q}(\Omega_{0}X_{g};\mathbf{Q})]_{\pi}&p=0,\\
{[\H_{q}(\Omega_{0}X_{g};\mathbf{Q})]}^{\pi}&p=1,\\
{[H_{X_{g}}\otimes\H_{q}(\Omega_{0}X_{g};\mathbf{Q})]}_{\pi}&p=n,\\
0,&\textit{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Since the map
\Omega\widetilde{X}_{g}\to\Omega_{0}X_{g}
is an equivalence and the homology of the domain is the free graded tensor coalgebra
T^{c}(H_{X_{g}})
, we need only consider the following two differentials in the previous spectral sequence:

\displaystyle\alpha=d^{n}:[H_{X_{g}}]_{\pi}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow[\H_{n-1}(\Omega_{0}X_{g};\mathbf{Q})]_{\pi}\cong[H_{X_{g}}]_{\pi},

\displaystyle\beta=d^{n}:[H_{X_{g}}^{\otimes 2}]_{\pi}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow[\H_{2n-2}(\Omega_{0}X_{g};\mathbf{Q})]_{\pi}\cong[H_{X_{g}}^{\otimes 2}]_{\pi}.

Claim 1
.

The map
\alpha
is the zero map.

Proof of Claim.

To see this, consider the map of fibre sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\widetilde{X}_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\widetilde{X}_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{0}X_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0}X_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{g}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

(4.2.1)

which gives rise to a map of corresponding spectral sequences. We obtain a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{X_{g}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{X_{g}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[H_{X_{g}}]}_{\pi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[H_{X_{g}}]}_{\pi},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

where the top horizontal map is the corresponding differential in the spectral sequence associated to the top fibration of (
4.2.1
). But this fibration is obtained as the (homotopy) colimit of the fibrations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\widetilde{X}_{g}^{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\widetilde{X}_{g}^{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}_{g}^{N},}

(4.2.2)

where
\widetilde{X}_{g}^{N}\simeq\vee_{|i|\leq N}\vee^{2g}S^{n}
. Thus, the differential
d^{n}:H_{X_{g}}\to H_{X_{g}}
above is obtained as the colimit over
N\in\mathbf{N}
of the analogous differentials
d^{n}_{N}:\bigoplus_{|i|\leq N}H_{g}\to\bigoplus_{|i|\leq N}H_{g}
for the fibre sequences (
4.2.2
). It suffices to show that each of the
d^{n}_{N}
’s are the zero map. This in turn follows from a dimension-counting argument involving the well-known cohomology of the free loop space of a finite wedge of
n
-spheres (a.k.a. the Hochschild homology of
\operatorname{C}^{*}(\widetilde{X}_{g}^{N};\mathbf{Q})\simeq\H^{*}(\widetilde{X}_{g}^{N};\mathbf{Q})
).
∎

To conclude, let us point out that all of the computations in this argument were of
\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}
-representations, and that the
\mathbf{Q}[\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}]
-module
C_{0}
in the statement is the cokernel of the map
\beta
.
∎

We now deal with the
S^{1}
-equivariant homology of
LX_{g}
and the right-hand side of (
4.1.29
).

Proposition 4.2.3
.

Let
A_{r}
and
B_{r}
and
C_{0}
be the grade
\mathbf{Q}[\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}]
-modules of
4.2.2
.

(i)

Let
r\neq 0
. As a
\mathbf{Q}[\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}]
-module, the rational
S^{1}
-equivariant homology of
L_{r}X_{g}
in degrees
*\leq 2n-2
is

\H_{*}^{S^{1}}(L_{r}X_{g};\mathbf{Q})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbf{Q}&*=0,\\[3.0pt]
{[H_{X_{g}}]}_{\pi}\cong H_{g}&*=n-1,\\[3.0pt]
{[S^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle(n-1)}(A_{r})]}_{\pi}\oplus[B_{r}]_{\pi}&*=2n-2,\\[3.0pt]
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

(ii)

As a
\mathbf{Q}[\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}]
-module, the rational
S^{1}
-equivariant homology of
L_{0}X_{g}
in degrees
*\leq 2n-2
is given by

\H_{*}^{S^{1}}(L_{0}X_{g};\mathbf{Q})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbf{Q}&*=0,\\
{[H_{X_{g}}]}_{\pi}\cong H_{g}&*=n-1,\\
C_{0}&*=2n-2,\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

(iii)

Therefore, for
*\leq 2n-2
, the
\mathbf{Q}[\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}]
-module appearing on the right-hand side of (
4.1.29
) is given by

\left[\frac{\H^{S^{1}}_{*}(LX_{g},LS^{1});\mathbf{Q})}{\H_{*+1}(X_{g},S^{1};\mathbf{Q})}\right]_{-\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\bigoplus_{r>0}{[A_{r}]_{\pi}}\cong\bigoplus_{r>0}H_{g}&*=n-1,\\[3.0pt]
C\oplus\bigoplus_{r>0}\left({\left[S^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle(n-1)}(A_{r})\right]}_{\pi}\oplus[B_{r}]_{\pi}\right)&*=2n-2,\\[3.0pt]
0&\text{otherwise},\end{array}\right.

where
C
denotes the coinvariants of
C_{0}
under some involution.

Proof.

For part (i), consider the homotopy orbit spectral sequence

\H_{*}(\text{{B}}S^{1};\H_{*}(L_{r}X_{g};\mathbf{Q}))\implies\H_{*}^{S^{1}}(L_{r}X_{g};\mathbf{Q}).

(4.2.3)

The
d^{2}
-differentials
\mathbf{Q}\to\mathbf{Q}
going from bidegree
(2k,0)
to
(2k-2,1)
for
k>0
are all isomorphisms: this is the case for the spectral sequence for the
S^{1}
-equivariant homology of
L_{r}S^{1}\simeq S^{1}
, which is a retract of (
4.2.3
).

By considering the cohomology version of (
4.2.3
) and exploiting its multiplicative structure, we also see that the
d^{2}
-differentials
H_{g}\to H_{g}
from bidegree
(2k,n-1)
to
(2k-2,n)
, for
k>0
, are isomorphisms too. Thus, (
4.2.3
) collapses on the
E^{3}
-page in degrees
*\leq 2n-2
, and the claim (i) follows immediately.

A similar argument as for part (i) also holds for (ii). Notice that both computations are as
\mathbf{Q}[\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}]
-modules.

For part (iii), we first show that for
*\leq 2n-2
, we have an isomorphism of
\mathbf{Q}[\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}]
-modules

\frac{\H^{S^{1}}_{*}(LX_{g},LS^{1});\mathbf{Q})}{\H_{*+1}(X_{g},S^{1};\mathbf{Q})}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\bigoplus_{r\in\mathbf{Z}}{[A_{r}]_{\pi}}\cong\bigoplus_{r\in\mathbf{Z}}H_{g}&*=n-1,\\[3.0pt]
C_{0}\oplus\bigoplus_{r\neq 0}\left({[S^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle(n-1)}(A_{r})]}_{\pi}\oplus[B_{r}]_{\pi}\right)&*=2n-2,\\[3.0pt]
0&\text{otherwise},\end{array}\right.

(4.2.4)

where we recall that the left-hand side above stands for the cokernel of the homomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H_{*+1}(X_{g},S^{1};\mathbf{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{HC}^{-}_{*+1}(\mathbf{S}[\Omega X_{g}],\mathbf{S}[\Omega S^{1}];\mathbf{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{HC}_{*}(\mathbf{S}[\Omega X_{g}],\mathbf{S}[\Omega S^{1}];\mathbf{Q})\cong\H^{S^{1}}_{*}(LX_{g},LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

(4.2.5)

induced by (
4.1.23
) on
\H_{*+1}(-;\mathbf{Q})
for
\iota:P\hookrightarrow M
our fixed self-embedding
\iota:X_{g}\hookrightarrow X_{g}
. It follows that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H_{*}(X_{g},S^{1};\mathbf{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H_{*}(LX_{g},LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H_{*}(X_{g},S^{1};\mathbf{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H^{S^{1}}_{*-1}(LX_{g},LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
(
4.2.5
)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}

commutes, where
B
is the composition of the norm map
N
with the natural transformation
\mathrm{HC}^{-}(-)\to\mathrm{HH}(-)
. This map
B
fits in Connes’ Periodicity exact sequence (see e.g.
[
64
, Thm. 2.2.1]
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H_{*+1}^{S^{1}}(LX_{g},LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H_{*-1}^{S^{1}}(LX_{g},LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H_{*}(LX_{g},LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H_{*}^{S^{1}}(LX_{g},LS^{1};\mathbf{Q}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

(4.2.6)

a.k.a. the Gysin exact sequence for the
S^{1}
-fibration
(LX_{g},LS^{1})\to(LX_{g},LS^{1})_{hS^{1}}
(cf.
[
64
, Thm. 7.2.3(b)]
). Thus, by the computations in
4.2.2
and parts (i) and (ii) of this proof, we see that
B
is an isomorphism in degrees
*\leq n
. The isomorphism (
4.2.4
) now follows from these observations and the fact that
c:(X_{g},S^{1})\to(LX_{g},LS^{1})
only hits the degree-zero component
(L_{0}X_{g},L_{0}S^{1})
, so, by dimension-counting, it must induce an isomorphism in
\H_{n}
onto this component. Observe also that
\smash{\H_{*}^{S^{1}}(LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})}
is supported in degree
0
and, in this degree, there is an isomorphism
\smash{\H_{0}^{S^{1}}(LX_{g};\mathbf{Q})\cong\H_{0}^{S^{1}}(LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})}
, so
\smash{\H_{0}^{S^{1}}(LX_{g},LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})=0}
.

Also note that a consequence of our previously mentioned computations and the exact sequence (
4.2.6
) is that the map
j:\H_{*}(LX_{g},LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})\to\H_{*}^{S^{1}}(LX_{g},LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})
is surjective for
*\leq 2n-1
and moreover, if
*\leq 2n-2
, then it is an isomorphism precisely when the codomain is non-zero. Thus, since
j
is clearly equivariant for
\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}
, it suffices to understand the effect of this involution on
\H_{*}(LX_{g},LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})
. On
LX_{g}
,
\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}
restricts to isomorphisms
L_{r}X_{g}\cong L_{-r}X_{g}
for all
r
, and thus it identifies the
r
and
-r
summands, for
r\neq 0
, and restricts to some involution on
C_{0}
. This establishes (iii).
∎

4.3
The mapping class group action on pseudoisotopy self-embeddings of
X_{g}

From the fibre sequence (
4.0.1
), there is an action of
\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}=\pi_{1}(\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g}),\ell)
on the homotopy groups of
\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})
. That fibre sequence is
\langle\rho\rangle
-equivariant, where
\rho
is the reflection involution of
Section

2.4
, so it follows that this action upgrades to one of the semidirect product
\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
by
(\gamma,\rho^{i})\cdot\sigma\coloneq\gamma\cdot(\rho^{i}\cdot\sigma)
. In this section, we trace this group action through the isomorphism (
4.1.29
), the right-hand side of which we have just computed in
4.2.3
(iii). Every embedding or automorphism space appearing in this section naturally carries a pointed
\langle\rho\rangle
-action induced by conjugation by
\rho
. We will often leave this implicit.

We first get rid of the stable framings by considering the map of fibre sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{{\cong}}}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\cong}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}),}

(4.3.1)

where the right square is cartesian. Hence, the action of
\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}
on the homotopy groups of
\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})
factors through
\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}\to\check{\Xi}_{g}\coloneq\pi_{1}(\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{{\cong}}}(X_{g}))
, and thus we only need to understand the action of
\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
arising from the bottom
\langle\rho\rangle
-equivariant fibre sequence.

However, note that the space we understand the homotopy type of is not
\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})
, but rather its loop space
\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})
(cf. (
4.1.28
)). The homotopy groups of the latter space admit a natural
\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-action.

Construction 4.3.1
.

Consider the fibre sequence
\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\xrightarrow{i}\smash{\Emb_{\partial_{0}}^{{\cong}}}(X_{g})\xrightarrow{q}\smash{\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\cong}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})
. Given a mapping class
[\phi]\in\check{\Xi}_{g}
and a homotopy class
[\sigma]\in\pi_{k}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}))
, these can be represented, respectively, by a
diffeomorphism

\phi
of
X_{g}
and a map of pairs
\sigma:(D^{k},\partial D^{k})\to(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}),*)
. Write
\psi
for the diffeomorphism
\phi\rho^{i}
. It is clear that
q
and pointwise conjugation by
\psi
, denoted
\psi_{\#}
, commute, and thus
q(\psi_{\#}(i\sigma))=\psi_{\#}(qi\sigma)
. But since
qi
is null-homotopic (rel basepoints), it follows that
q(\psi_{\#}(i\sigma))
is null-homotopic (rel
\partial D^{k}
). Using this null-homotopy and the fact that
i
and
q
give a fibre sequence, it follows that
\psi_{\#}(i\sigma)
lifts, uniquely up to homotopy, to some map
\psi_{\#}\sigma:(D^{k},\partial D^{k})\to(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}),*)
. Set

([\phi],\rho^{i})\cdot[\sigma]\coloneq[\psi_{\#}\sigma]=[(\phi\rho^{i})_{\#}\sigma].

Remark 4.3.2
.

One verifies that the rule defined in
4.3.1
does not depend on the representatives
\phi
and
\sigma
, and so indeed gives a
\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-action on
\pi_{k}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}))
. We assumed
\phi
to be a diffeomorphism for simplicity, but in general the homotopy class
[(\phi\rho^{i})_{\#}\sigma]
is well-defined if
\phi
is just an embedding, provided a choice of homotopy right inverse—that is, an embedding
\phi^{-1}
together with an isotopy
\gamma
from
\phi\circ\phi^{-1}
to the identity
\Id_{X_{g}}
. Such inverses exist by assumption, and the space of such pairs
(\phi^{-1},\gamma)
is contractible
10
10

10

Indeed, given a unital group-like topological monoid
M
and an element
m\in M
, the map
m\cdot-:M\to M
is a homotopy equivalence. The space of homotopy right inverses to
m
is the homotopy fibre of this map at the identity element, which is contractible since
m\cdot-
is an equivalence.
.

Lemma 4.3.3
.

For every
*\geq 0
, the isomorphism
\pi_{*}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}))\cong\pi_{*+1}\big(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\big)
induced by the equivalence
\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\simeq\Omega\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})
of (
4.1.28
) is
\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-equivariant for the action on the left-hand side described in
4.3.1
, and the usual action on the right-hand side arising from the bottom
\langle\rho\rangle
-equivariant fibre sequence in (
4.3.1
).

Proof.

The equivalence mentioned in the statement arises from the map of fibre sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\Emb_{\partial_{0}}^{{\cong}}}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\cong}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{{\cong}}}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\cong}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}),}
(
4.1.28
)

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

(4.3.2)

where
\eta
stands for the canonical
\mathbf{E}_{1}
-equivalence
\eta:M\xrightarrow{\sim}\Omega\text{{B}}M
associated to any unital group-like topological monoid
M
. We need an alternative description of the
\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-action on the homotopy groups of the left bottom term that resembles that of
4.3.1
.

For
F\xrightarrow{i}E\xrightarrow{q}B
a fibre sequence of based spaces, the usual action of
\pi_{1}(E)
on
\pi_{*\geq 1}(F)
can be recovered from the looped fibre sequence
\Omega F\to\Omega E\to\Omega B
as follows: given homotopy classes
[\gamma]\in\pi_{1}(E)
and
[\sigma]\in\pi_{k}(\Omega F)
, these can be represented by a loop
\gamma\in\Omega E
and a map
\sigma:(D^{k},\partial D^{k})\to(\Omega F,*)
. Applying pointwise concatenation of loops, we obtain a map
\gamma_{\#}(i\sigma)\coloneq\gamma\cdot i\sigma\cdot\overline{\gamma}:D^{k}\to\Omega E
. Note that on the boundary
\partial D^{k}
,
\gamma_{\#}(i\sigma)
takes the value
\gamma\cdot*\cdot\overline{\gamma}
, which is canonically null-homotopic. Thus, we obtain a map
\widetilde{\gamma}_{\#}(i\sigma):(D^{k},\partial D^{k})\to(\Omega E,*)
. But
q(\widetilde{\gamma}_{\#}(i\sigma))=\widetilde{q(\gamma)}_{\#}(qi\sigma)
, where the latter is obtained from
q(\gamma)_{\#}(qi\sigma)
as before, and since
qi
is null-homotopic, then
q(\widetilde{\gamma}_{\#}(i\sigma))
is null-homotopic (rel
\partial D^{k}
). It follows that
\widetilde{\gamma}_{\#}(i\sigma)
lifts, up to homotopy, to some map
\widetilde{\gamma}_{\#}\sigma:(D^{k},\partial D^{k})\to(\Omega F,*)
, and thus we set
[\gamma]\cdot[\sigma]\coloneq[\widetilde{\gamma}_{\#}\sigma]\in\pi_{k}(\Omega F)
. One readily verifies that this action of
\pi_{1}(E)
on
\pi_{k}(\Omega F)
recovers the standard action of
\pi_{1}(E)
on
\pi_{k+1}(F)
under the natural isomorphism
\pi_{k}(\Omega F)\cong\pi_{k+1}(F)
, and is therefore well-defined. Moreover, if
G
is a discrete group and the fibre sequence
F\to E\to B
is
G
-equivariant, then setting
([\gamma],g)\cdot[\sigma]\coloneq[\gamma]\cdot[g\cdot\sigma]
defines an action of
\pi_{1}(E)\rtimes G
on
\pi_{k}(\Omega F)
which recovers the one just described for the fibre sequence
F\times_{G}EG\to E\times_{G}EG\to B\times_{G}EG
obtained from
F\to E\to B
by applying the Borel construction.

Taking
F\to E\to B
to be the bottom fibre sequence in (
4.3.1
), it is clear that the description just given of the
\check{\Xi}_{g}
-action on the homotopy groups of
\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})
differs from that of
4.3.1
only in that conjugation by an embedding
\phi
is replaced with conjugation by the loop
\eta(\phi)
, and hence both agree under the equivalence (
4.1.28
) (for
\eta:M\xrightarrow{\sim}\Omega\text{{B}}M
identifies the multiplication on
M
with loop concatenation on
\Omega\text{{B}}M
). The
\langle\rho\rangle
-equivariance follows since all the maps involved in (
4.3.2
) are
\langle\rho\rangle
-equivariant.
∎

So we only need to trace the
\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
–action on
\pi_{*}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}))
from
4.3.1
through the isomorphism (
4.1.29
). Recall that this isomorphism arises from the
(3n-5)
-connected map (
4.1.3
)

\Phi^{\Emb}_{X_{g}}\colon\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\longrightarrow\Omega^{\infty}(\mathbf{CE}_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})_{hC_{2}}),

(4.3.3)

where
\mathbf{CE}_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})
denotes the concordance embedding spectrum
\mathbf{CE}(P,M)
associated to our fixed embedding
\iota\colon X_{g}\hookrightarrow X_{g}
(we retain
\partial_{0}
in the notation
\mathbf{CE}_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})
to avoid confusion with the concordance diffeomorphism spectrum
\mathbf{C}(X_{g})=\Sigma^{-1}\mathbf{H}(X_{g})
). Because of the somewhat opaque definition of this spectrum (cf. (
4.1.2
)), it is not immediate whether the homotopy groups of the target in (
4.3.3
) carry a natural
\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
–action. We will resolve this after rationalisation (indeed, inverting
2
would suffice, but we rationalise for consistency).

Construction 4.3.4
.

Given
([\phi],\rho^{i})\in\check{\Xi}_{g}
and
[\sigma]\in\pi_{k}(\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}))
, represent them, respectively, by a diffeomorphism
\phi
of
X_{g}
and a map of pairs
\sigma:(D^{k},\partial D^{k})\to(\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}),*)
, and let
\psi\coloneq\phi\circ\rho^{i}
. For a concordance embedding
\varphi\in\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})
, let us write
\psi_{\#}\varphi\coloneq(\psi\times\mathrm{Id}_{I})\circ\varphi\circ(\psi^{-1}\times\mathrm{Id}_{I})\in\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})
. Then, if
\psi_{\#}\sigma:(D^{k},\partial D^{k})\to(\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}),*)
is the map given by
(\psi_{\#}\sigma)(v)=\psi_{\#}(\sigma(v))
for
v\in D^{k}
, we set

([\phi],\rho^{i})\cdot[\sigma]\coloneq[\psi_{\#}\sigma]=[\phi_{\#}\rho^{i}_{\#}\sigma]\in\pi_{k}(\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})).

This action is clearly compatible with the stabilisation maps
\Sigma:\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})\to\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}\times I)
of (
4.1.12
), and hence there is an induced
\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-action on the homotopy groups of the stable space
\mathcal{CE}_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})\coloneq\hocolim_{k}\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}\times I^{k})
. Moreover, this action is also visibly compatible with the
h
-cobordism involution
\iota_{\H}
modelled in
A.1.1
(ii), and hence there is an induced
\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-action on the groups

\left[\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathcal{CE}_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}))\right]_{\iota_{\H}}\overset{\eqref{StableCEmbIETSeq}}{\cong}\left[\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathbf{CE}_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}))\right]_{\iota_{\H}}\cong\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathbf{CE}_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})_{hC_{2}}).

(4.3.4)

The following is one of the main results in this section, whose proof will be our next immediate goal.

Theorem 4.3.5
.

The Weiss–Williams map (
4.3.3
)

\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\Phi^{\Emb}_{X_{g}}\big):\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\big)\longrightarrow\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\Omega^{\infty}(\mathbf{CE}_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})_{hC_{2}})\right)

is
\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-equivariant on rational (in fact,
\mathbf{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2}]
-local) homotopy groups in degrees
*\leq 3n-6
, for the action from
4.3.1
on the domain and the action from
4.3.4
on the target.

Remark 4.3.6
.

As mentioned in the statement of
4.3.5
, the conclusion also holds for
\mathbf{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2}]
-local homotopy groups. The only properties of the rationalisation hypothesis that we will use are:

•

the natural map
\nu:(\Omega^{\infty}X)_{hC_{2}}\xrightarrow{\simeq_{\mathbf{Q}}}\Omega^{\infty}(X_{hC_{2}})
is a rational equivalence for any
C_{2}
-spectrum
X
;

•

the group
\pi_{0}\left(\operatorname{C}(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1})\right)
is
2
-torsion for
2n\geq 6
, and so vanishes after rationalisation (cf. (
4.3.10
)).

The
\mathbf{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2}]
-local analogues of both of these facts clearly hold. In particular, a
\mathbf{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2}]
-local version of
4.3.9
also holds. For clarity of notation, and because this is the only case required later, we choose to work rationally throughout.

We first relate the
\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
–actions on
\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})
and on
\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})
from
4.3.1
and
4.3.4
, respectively, via a map that is (at least initially) unrelated to the Weiss–Williams map (
4.3.3
). Consider the
\langle\rho\rangle
-equivariant fibre sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Embo(X_{g}\times I)}

where
\partial_{0}(X_{g}\times I)=\partial_{0}(X_{g}\times I)\coloneq X_{g}\times\{0,1\}\cup\partial_{0}(X_{g})\times I
. Looping the base and total space, we obtain

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})}

where
\Gamma
is, by inspection, the map that sends a loop
\{\varphi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
(based at the identity) of self-embeddings of

to the embedding
11
11

11

For
\varphi

\Omega
in the domain of

to denote based

[

for a proper model of the map
\Gamma
.

\varphi:X_{g}\times I\xhookrightarrow{}X_{g}\times I
given by
\varphi(x,t)=(\varphi_{t}(x),t)
. Note that
\Gamma\circ\mathrm{res}_{1}
is indeed null-homotopic: replace the interval

in the codomain of
\Gamma
by

, and model
\Gamma
by the map sending

to

if

and

if

. Now, given a loop of concordance embeddings

with
\{\varphi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}=\mathrm{res}_{1}(\{\Phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]})
, and writing

for the translation map

, the nullhomotopy
\Gamma\circ\mathrm{res}_{1}\simeq*
is

[0,1]\ni s\longmapsto\left((x,t)\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}(x,t),&-1\leq t\leq s-1,\\
\mathrm{tr}_{s-1}(\Phi_{s}(x,t-s+1)),&s-1\leq t\leq s,\\
(\varphi_{t}(x),t),&s\leq t\leq 1.\end{array}\right.\right).

Lemma 4.3.7
.

The map
\pi_{*}(\alpha):\pi_{*}(\Omega\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}))\to\pi_{*}(\Omega\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}))
is
\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-equivariant for the actions of Constructions
4.3.4
and
4.3.1
, respectively.

Proof.

This follows by a direct verification. Given
([\phi],\rho^{i})\in\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
and
[\sigma]\in\pi_{k-1}(\Omega\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}))
, choose representatives
\phi
and
\sigma\colon(D^{k},\partial D^{k})\to(\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}),*)
as in
4.3.4
, and set
\psi=\phi\circ\rho^{i}
. Pointwise conjugation by
\psi
, written
\psi_{\#}
, is well-defined in each space of the top fibre sequence in (
4.3.6
): on the base it is given by conjugation by
\psi\times\Id_{I}
, and on the total space and the fibre by conjugation by
\psi
and by
\psi\times\Id_{I}
, respectively. Both
\mathrm{res}_{1}
and
\Gamma
commute with
\psi_{\#}
, hence
\Gamma(\psi_{\#}\mathrm{res}_{1}(\sigma))=\psi_{\#}\Gamma(\mathrm{res}_{1}(\sigma))
. Since
\Gamma\circ\mathrm{res}_{1}
is null-homotopic, the map
\psi_{\#}\mathrm{res}_{1}(\sigma)
lifts along this null-homotopy to a map
(D^{k},\partial D^{k})\to(\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}),*)
, unique up to homotopy; this lift must be
\psi_{\#}(\sigma)
.

Replacing the base space of the bottom fibre sequence in (
4.3.6
) by
\bEmbo(X_{g}\times I)
and replacing
b
by
\widetilde{\Gamma}\circ\beta
, it is clear that the action described in
4.3.4
is compatible with that of
4.3.1
, since the null-homotopy of
\widetilde{\Gamma}\circ i\circ\beta\circ\alpha=\beta\circ\Gamma\circ\mathrm{res}_{1}
is compatible with the null-homotopy of
\Gamma\circ\mathrm{res}_{1}
.
∎

Remark 4.3.8
.

Note that
Lemma

4.3.7
does not deal with the map
\pi_{0}(\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}))\to\pi_{0}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}))
, but, luckyly, both of these groups are zero by Hudson’s theorem
[
45
]
.

As foreshadowed, the map
\alpha
of (
4.3.6
) is indeed closely related to the Weiss–Williams map (
4.3.3
).

Remark 4.3.11
.

It is very likely that one can get rid of one of the loopings in the square of

Proof of
4.3.9
.

We first argue that the homotopy fibre of the map between the natural commutative diagrams of
4.3.10
induced by the embedding
(Q\hookrightarrow Q^{\prime})=(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}\hookrightarrow X_{g})
yields the one appearing in the statement of
4.3.9
looped once more.

The bottom horizontal map follows by (
4.1.14
) and the definition of
\mathbf{CE}_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})\coloneq\hofib(\mathbf{H}(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1})\to\mathbf{H}(X_{g}))
. The left vertical map follows by the usual isotopy extension fibre sequence (
4.1.11
) and its stable version (see
4.1.4
).
For the top horizontal map, there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\operatorname{C}(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\operatorname{C}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}\left(\frac{\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}}{\Diff}\right)_{\partial}(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}\left(\frac{\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}}{\Diff}\right)_{\partial}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}\left(\frac{\Diff^{b}_{\partial}(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}\times\mathbf{R}^{\infty})}{\Diffb(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1})}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}\left(\frac{\Diff^{b}_{\partial}(X_{g}\times\mathbf{R}^{\infty})}{\Diffb(X_{g})}\right),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\circ i}

where the top map is one of fibre sequences by the isotopy extension theorem, and the bottom right subsquare is cartesian as explained in (
4.1.9
). Finally for the right vertical map, note from
4.1.2
that the map
\smash{\Phi^{\Emb}_{X_{g}}}
is defined to be
\smash{\Phi_{\infty}^{\mathsf{E}_{X_{g}}}}
up to the equivalence (
4.1.10
) in the domain (which is a consequence of the bottom right subsquare in the above diagram being cartesian). Since the maps
\smash{\Phi_{\infty}^{F}}
of (
4.1.6
) preserve fibre sequences of orthogonal functors, the claim follows.

Thus, we have established that the diagram in the statement of
4.3.9
is homotopy commutative upon looping once, so the original diagram must be commutative on homotopy groups in degrees
*\geq 1
. It remains to argue the commutativity on
\pi_{0}^{\mathbf{Q}}(-)
, i.e. that the diagram of rational vector spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\mathbf{CE}_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\mathbf{CE}_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})\right)\right]_{\iota_{\H}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\Emb}_{*}\coloneq(\Phi^{\Emb}_{X_{g}})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{*}}

(4.3.9)

commutes. (Observe that since
\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})
is an

-space,

is abelian and thus can indeed be rationalised.) If
2n>6
, this follows immediately from the fact that
\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})
is
(n-2)
-connected, so we only need to deal with the case

. To this end, consider the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\operatorname{C}(X_{g})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\left(\frac{\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}}{\Diff}\right)_{\partial}(X_{g})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\frac{\Diff^{b}_{\partial}(X_{g}\times\mathbf{R}^{\infty})}{\Diff_{\partial}(X_{g})}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\mathbf{H}(X_{g})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\pi_{2}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\mathbf{H}(X_{g})\right)\right]_{\iota_{\H}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\Diff}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{*}}

obtained from the one in

for

when taking

and rationalising appropriately. This square maps to (
4.3.9
) and we further claim that the group homomorphism

is surjective. If so, then (
4.3.9
) is commutative by an elementary diagram chase. To see this, note that
r_{*}
fits in a long exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\operatorname{C}(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\operatorname{C}(X_{g})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\operatorname{C}(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1})\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{*}}

and hence it suffices to show that
\pi_{0}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\operatorname{C}(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1})\right)=0
. This in turn follows from the work of Hatcher–Wagoner
[
41
]
(with corrections by Igusa
[
48
, Thm. 8.a.1]
) who, for

a compact smooth manifold of dimension

, established an exact sequence of abelian groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Wh}_{1}^{+}(\pi_{1}M;\pi_{2}M)\oplus\mathrm{Wh}_{1}^{+}(\pi_{1}M;\mathbf{F}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\operatorname{C}(M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Wh}_{2}(\pi_{1}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

(4.3.10)

For
M=S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}
(so
\pi_{1}M=\mathbf{Z}
and
\pi_{2}M=0
), the abelian groups on the left and right terms in (
4.3.10
) are either zero or
2
-torsion. Thus,
\pi_{0}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\operatorname{C}(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}))=0
(in fact,
\pi_{0}(\operatorname{C}(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}))[\tfrac{1}{2}]=0
), as desired.
∎

Now that
4.3.5
has been established, we need to understand the
\mathbf{Q}[\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle]
-action of
4.3.4
on
\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathcal{CE}_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}))
(or rather, on its
\iota_{\H}
-coinvariants). For the remainder of the section, fix
([\phi],\rho^{i})\in\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
and choose a representative diffeomorphism
\phi
of
X_{g}
that fixes a neighbourhood of
\partial X_{g}\cup(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1})
. Looping the fibre sequence

4.1.14
and noting (

) yields a fibre sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{CE}_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}

(4.3.11)

which is split since
\mathcal{C}(-)\simeq\Omega\mathcal{H}(-)
is a homotopy functor and the embedding

lies in the homotopy class of the standard inclusion

(which is a section of the obvious collapse map). Thus the restriction map

is surjective on homotopy groups. Moreover, conjugation by

is also defined on the space

, and

clearly commutes with
\psi_{\#}
. We claim that

on

is induced by functoriality of the homotopy functor
\Omega^{2}\Wh(-)\simeq\Omega\mathcal{H}(-)
.

Lemma 4.3.12
.

Let
\psi
be a diffeomorphism of a compact manifold
M
. Then, there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(M)}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
(
4.1.17
)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
(
4.3.8
)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
(
4.1.17
)

Here
\psi_{*}
are the maps induced by
\psi
from the functoriality of the homotopy functors
\mathcal{H}(-)
and
\Wh(-)
. Moreover, if
N
is a codimension-zero submanifold of
M
on which
\psi
restricts to a diffeomorphism
\psi\mid_{N}=\xi
, then the above diagram is compatible with

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\mathcal{H}(N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}\Wh(N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\mathcal{H}(N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}\Wh(N)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

via the obvious maps induced by the inclusion
N\subset M
.

Proof.

The Waldhausen–Jahren–Rognes equivalence
\mathcal{H}(-)\simeq\Omega\Wh(-)
of (
4.1.17
) is functorial in codimension-zero embeddings (in particular diffeomorphisms), so the right hand square in the diagram above commutes.

Regarding the commutativity of the left-hand subsquare, consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{C}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{C}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H(M),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

(4.3.12)

where the rows are the fibre sequence in (
4.1.24
) inducing
\Omega\H(M)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{C}(M)
. This diagram is visibly commutative and compatible with stabilisation. Thus, we obtain the desired commutativity of the left subsquare in the statement. The addendum easily follows too: that the right subsquares are compatible follows from the fact that the Waldhausen–Jahren–Rognes equivalence is functorial with respect to codimension-zero embeddings. The left subsquares are compatible because (
4.3.12
) is also functorial in such embeddings.
∎

Therefore, given
([\phi],\rho^{i})\in\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
as before, the diffeomorphism
\psi=\phi\rho^{i}
restricts to a diffeomorphism on
S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}\subset X_{g}
by the assumption that
\phi
is fixed on this submanifold and the reflection
\rho
preserves it. Thus, by the previous result we obtain a homotopy commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\mathcal{CE}_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{3}\Wh(X_{g},S^{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\mathcal{CE}_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{3}\Wh(X_{g},S^{1}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Finally, Goodwillie’s isomorphism (
4.1.27
) is functorial in the pair
(X,Y)
as it is induced by the natural diagram (
4.1.20
)—this isomorphism is the one that allows us to access the rational homotopy of the relative Whitehead space
\Wh(X,Y)
in
4.1.5
. All in all, we have proved:

Theorem 4.3.13
.

The action of
\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
on the homotopy groups of the fibre in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}}

factors through the homomorphism
\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle\to\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
. Under the isomorphism

\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\big)\cong\left[\frac{\H^{S^{1}}_{*}(LX_{g},LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})}{\H_{*+1}(X_{g},S^{1};\mathbf{Q})}\right]_{-\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}},\quad*\leq 3n-5,

of (
4.1.29
), the action of
([\phi],\rho^{i})\in\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
(where
[\phi]
is represented by a diffeomorphism
\phi
of
X_{g}
) on the left-hand side corresponds, on the right-hand side, to the map

(\phi\rho^{i})_{*}:\left[\frac{\H^{S^{1}}_{*}(LX_{g},LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})}{\H_{*+1}(X_{g},S^{1};\mathbf{Q})}\right]_{-\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\left[\frac{\H^{S^{1}}_{*}(LX_{g},LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})}{\H_{*+1}(X_{g},S^{1};\mathbf{Q})}\right]_{-\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}}

induced by functoriality on
(X_{g},S^{1})
.

Proof.

Note that when
*=0
or
1
there is nothing to prove as
\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})
is
(n-2)
-connected. The rest of the cases follow from
4.3.5
and the work in this section.
∎

Recall that in
4.2.3
(iii) we computed the group
\smash{\big[\frac{\H^{S^{1}}_{*}(LX_{g},LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})}{\H_{*+1}(X_{g},S^{1};\mathbf{Q})}\big]_{-\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}}}
as a representation of
\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}=\pi_{0}(\Aut_{*}(X_{g}))
. Moreover, there are natural homomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}

(4.3.13)

where
\nu([\phi],\rho^{i})\coloneq[\phi\rho^{i}]
. Thus, all the
\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}
-representations involved in that statement are naturally
\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-representations by restriction along (
4.3.13
). Together with
4.3.13
, we conclude:

Corollary 4.3.14
.

Let
H_{X_{g}}
,
A_{r}
,
B_{r}
and
C
be the
\mathbf{Q}[\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}]
-modules of
4.2.2
and
4.2.3
, regarded now as
\mathbf{Q}[\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle]
-modules by restriction along (
4.3.13
). Then, for all
n\geq 3
and
*\leq 2n-2
, there is an isomorphism of
\mathbf{Q}[\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle]
-modules

\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\big)\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\bigoplus_{r>0}{[A_{r}]_{\pi}}\cong\bigoplus_{r>0}H_{g}&*=n-1,\\[3.0pt]
C\oplus\bigoplus_{r>0}\left({[S^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle(n-1)}(A_{r})]}_{\pi}\oplus[B_{r}]_{\pi}\right)&*=2n-2,\\[3.0pt]
0&\text{otherwise},\end{array}\right.

where
C
is some quotient of
[H_{X_{g}}^{\otimes 2}]_{\pi}

.

4.4
The
\mathbf{Z}[\mathbf{N}^{\times}]
-module structures

For each positive integer
d\in\mathbf{N}
, the circle
S^{1}
is its own
d
-fold cover and, similarly,
X_{dg}
is the corresponding
d
-fold cover of
X_{g}
for every
g\geq 0
. This observation gives rise to maps

\varphi_{d}:\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}\longrightarrow\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{dg})_{\ell},\quad d\in\mathbf{N},

given by “lifting” a self-embedding of
X_{g}
to
X_{dg}
. These maps are natural in
g\geq 0
and satisfy that the composite
\varphi_{d}\circ\varphi_{d^{\prime}}
is homotopic to
\varphi_{d\cdot d^{\prime}}
for every
d,d^{\prime}\in\mathbf{N}
. Letting
g\to\infty
, these (homotopy classes of) maps induce self-maps of
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{\infty})_{\ell}
that equip its homotopy groups with the structure of a
\mathbf{Z}[\mathbf{N}^{\times}]
-module—also known as a
cyclotomic module
.

Similarly, the homotopy groups of
\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{\infty})_{\ell}
and
\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{\infty})
inherit compatible
\mathbf{Z}[\mathbf{N}^{\times}]
-module structures that make the long exact sequence associated to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{\infty})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{\infty})_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{\infty})_{\ell},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

one of
\mathbf{Z}[\mathbf{N}^{\times}]
-modules—in particular, the connecting maps

\delta_{*}:\pi_{*+1}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{\infty})_{\ell})\longrightarrow\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{\infty})\right)

are
\mathbf{Z}[\mathbf{N}^{\times}]
-module maps. We will use this additional structure in later sections to understand
\delta
and, consequently, the homotopy groups of
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{\infty})_{\ell}
.

In this section, we focus on the
\mathbf{Z}[\mathbf{N}^{\times}]
-module structures of the vector spaces featuring in the connecting maps
\delta_{*}
above. To do so, we will study the Frobenius maps
\varphi_{d}
unstably, i.e., in the homotopy groups of the spaces
\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}
and
\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})
, for all
g\geq 0
and
d\in\mathbf{N}
.

4.4.1
The
\mathbf{Z}[\mathbf{N}^{\times}]
-module structure of pseudoisotopy embeddings

In this section, we study the Frobenius map

\varphi_{d}\colon\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}))\;\longrightarrow\;\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{dg})),

(4.4.1)

which, by
4.3.14
, is of the form

\varphi_{d}\colon\bigoplus_{r>0}H_{g}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{r>0}H_{dg}
. One possible approach is to trace the effect of
\varphi_{d}
through our computation of

\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}))
in the previous sections, which, as explained in
Section

4.1
, proceeds via the Weiss–Williams map, the Waldhausen–Jahren–Rognes equivalence, and finally the cyclotomic trace.
This method is complicated by the intricacy of the Waldhausen–Jahren–Rognes equivalence, which makes it difficult to relate
\varphi_{d}
to modern
K
-theoretic transfer maps. Instead, we adopt a more direct approach to understanding (
4.4.1
), using the work of Goodwillie
[
40
]
. (We note, however, that this direct method does not work for the Frobenius map (
4.4.1
) in degree
2n-3
—the next non-vanishing homotopy group—for which the earlier, more involved approach seems better suited. As we will not need that case, we restrict ourselves to the direct method.) It occurred to us to use this more direct approach after an illuminating conversation about Goodwillie’s work with Manuel Krannich, to whom we are grateful.

Let us explain the strategy first. Recall the map
\alpha:\Omega\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})\to\Omega\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})
of (
4.3.6
). By the construction of this map, it is readily seen that it is compatible with the Frobenii, i.e.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{dg})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{dg})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

commutes. It follows from
4.3.9
that the map
\alpha
induces the right isomorphism in

\left[\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}\times I^{2m})\big)\right]_{\iota_{\H}}\cong\left[\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})\big)\right]_{\iota_{\H}}\cong\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\big),\qquad*\leq 3n-6,

(4.4.2)

which is compatible with the Frobenii. The left isomorphism is induced by the concordance stabilisation map
\Sigma^{2m}:\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})\to\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}\times I^{2m})
, which is
(3n-5)
-connected, clearly compatible with the Frobenii, and
\iota_{\H}
-equivariant by
(3)
. Thus, it is enough for us to compute
\smash{\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}\times I^{2m}))}
, for sufficiently large
m\geq 0
, in a way that we can identify the action of
\iota_{\H}
and understand the Frobenius maps
\varphi_{d}
.

For this, we import the work of Goodwillie in
[
40
, §3]
, which we shall now recall. Writing
\mathrm{Spc}^{[1]}
for the arrow category
\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{Spc})
, there is defined a (homotopy) functor

L:\mathrm{Spc}^{[1]}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Spc},\quad(Y\to X)\longmapsto Y\times_{X}LX\simeq\operatorname{holim}(Y\to X\xleftarrow{\mathrm{ev}_{1}}LX).

The inclusion
c:X\to LX
as the constant loops (a section of
\mathrm{ev}_{1}
) gives rise to a map
Y\to L(Y\to X)
, and we denote by
L(Y\to X)/Y
its (homotopy) cofibre. There is another functor

\Psi:\mathrm{Spc}^{[1]}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Spc}_{*},\quad(Y\to X)\longmapsto\Omega^{2}Q(L(Y\to X)/Y)=\Omega^{\infty+2}\Sigma^{\infty}(L(Y\to X)/Y).

Write
\Psi(X)
for
\Psi(\id_{X})
and
\overline{\Psi}(Y\to X)
for the homotopy fibre of the natural map
\Psi(Y\to X)\to\Psi(X)
. Note that
\Psi(X)\simeq\Omega^{2}Q(LX/X)
and that for a map
Y\to X
, the factorisation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

gives rise to natural maps
\Psi(Y)\to\Psi(Y\to X)\to\Psi(X)
.

For
(Y\to X)=(N\xhookrightarrow{}M)
an embedding of compact manifolds, Goodwillie constructs a commutative diagram (cf. page 24 loc.cit.)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{C}(N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi(N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi(N\hookrightarrow M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{C}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi(M),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}

whose vertical homotopy fibre is denoted by
\overline{\tau}
. When
P\subset M
is a compact submanifold of
M
, and
N\xhookrightarrow{}M
is the evident inclusion
M-\nu P\hookrightarrow M
, this map
\overline{\tau}
is of the form

\overline{\tau}:\Omega\CEmb(P,M)\longrightarrow\overline{\Psi}(M-P\to M).

(4.4.3)

Lemma 4.4.1

(Lemma 3.19,
[
40
]
)
.

If
3\leq k<\dim M
and
M
is obtained from
M-\nu P
by attaching handles of index
k
, then
(
4.4.3
)
is
(2k-5)
-connected.

The case of interest for us is when
N\xhookrightarrow{}M
is the inclusion
S^{1}\times D^{2n-1+2m}\xhookrightarrow{}X_{g}\times I^{2m}
. In this case,
Lemma

4.4.1
tells us that, for
n\geq 3
, the map
\overline{\tau}:\Omega\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}\times I^{2m})\to\overline{\Psi}(S^{1}\to X_{g})
is
(2n-5)
-connected. Since
n-3\leq 2n-6
for
n\geq 3
, we obtain an isomorphism

\overline{\tau}_{*}:\pi_{n-2}(\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}\times I^{2m}))\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi_{n-3}(\overline{\Psi}(S^{1}\to X_{g})).

(4.4.4)

We will use this isomorphism, described in
[
40
, pp. 23–24]
, to study the Frobenii on
\pi_{n-2}\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}\times I^{2m})
.

Remark 4.4.2
.

A quick word on why
X_{g}\times I^{2m}
: as explained in
[
40
, p. 26]
, the map
\tau\colon\operatorname{C}(M)\to\Psi(M)
factors through the stable concordance space
\mathcal{C}(M)
, and hence
\overline{\tau}
factors through
\Omega\mathcal{CE}(P,M)
. Since the descriptions of
\tau
and
\overline{\tau}
are simpler in a fixed finite dimension, we work in such a dimension. For
Lemma

4.4.4
, we require this dimension
N\coloneq d+2n
large enough that Igusa’s lower bound for the concordance stable range of
X_{g}\times I^{2m}
(approximately
N/3
) exceeds
n-2
. Thus, we replace
X_{g}
by
X_{g}\times I^{2m}
for
m
sufficiently large; however, we continue to simply write
X_{g}
for the homotopy type
of
X_{g}\times I^{2m}
.

To this end, we first need to compute
\pi_{n-3}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\overline{\Psi}(S^{1}\to X_{g}))
; of course, we already know by (
4.1.22
) and (
4.2.4
) that it is isomorphic to
\bigoplus_{r\neq 0}H_{g}
, but we need to fix an identification that we can trace the Frobenii through. Consider the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\operatorname{C}(X_{g}\times I^{2m}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}\times I^{2m}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{r\neq 0}H_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\Psi(X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n-3}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\overline{\Psi}(S^{1}\to X_{g})),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\tau}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

(4.4.5)

where the bottom left isomorphism is
\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\Psi(X_{g}))=\pi_{n}^{s}(LX_{g}/X_{g})\otimes\mathbf{Q}\cong\H_{n}(LX_{g},X_{g};\mathbf{Q})
together with
4.2.2
.

Lemma 4.4.3
.

The map
\delta
in (
4.4.5
) is an isomorphism.

Proof.

By definition,
\delta
is the connecting map in a long exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\Psi(S^{1}\to X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\Psi(X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n-3}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\overline{\Psi}(S^{1}\to X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n-3}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\Psi(S^{1}\to X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n-3}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\Psi(X_{g})).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

We will show that i) the leftmost term
\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}\Psi(S^{1}\to X_{g})=0
and that ii) the rightmost map
a
is an isomorphism. Recall also that
\Psi(X_{g})=\Omega^{2}Q(LX_{g}/X_{g})
and
\Psi(S^{1}\to X_{g})=\Omega^{2}Q(L(S^{1}\to X_{g})/S^{1})
, so we need to understand the map
L(S^{1}\to X_{g})/S^{1}\to LX_{g}
in rational homology. Note that, just like
LX_{g}
, the space
L(S^{1}\to X_{g})
decomposes as a disjoint union of connected components
\coprod_{r\in\mathbf{Z}}L_{r}(S^{1}\to X_{g})
, and by definition we have maps of fibrations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{r}X_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{r}(S^{1}\to X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{r}X_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{r}X_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{g}.}

Their respective Serre spectral sequences are

\displaystyle\H_{p}(S^{1};\underline{\H_{q}(\Omega_{r}X_{g};\mathbf{Q})})

\displaystyle\implies\H_{p+q}(L_{r}(S^{1}\to X_{g});\mathbf{Q}),

\displaystyle\H_{p}(X_{g};\underline{\H_{q}(\Omega_{r}X_{g};\mathbf{Q})})

\displaystyle\implies\H_{p+q}(L_{r}X_{g};\mathbf{Q}),

(4.4.6)

and the map of
E^{2}
-pages
\H_{p}(S^{1};\underline{\H_{q}(\Omega_{r}X_{g};\mathbf{Q})})\to\H_{p}(X_{g};\underline{\H_{q}(\Omega_{r}X_{g};\mathbf{Q})})
is an isomorphism if
p\leq n-1
. We only need to consider the case when
q=n-1
, as the homology of
\Omega_{r}X_{g}\simeq\Omega_{0}X_{g}
is concentrated in degrees that are multiples of
n-1
. In this degrees, we have the Hurewicz isomorphism
\H_{n-1}(\Omega_{r}X_{g};\mathbf{Q})\cong\pi_{n-1}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\Omega_{r}X_{g})\cong H_{X_{g}}
, and thus the action of
\pi=\pi_{1}S^{1}=\pi_{1}X_{g}
on
\underline{\H_{n-1}(\Omega_{r}X_{g};\mathbf{Q})}
is the one arising from the usual action of
\pi_{1}
of the total space of a fibration on its fibre. Thus,
\underline{\H_{n-1}(\Omega_{r}X_{g};\mathbf{Q})}\cong H_{X_{g}}
as a
\mathbf{Q}[\pi]
-module. Noting that
[H_{X_{g}}]^{\pi}=0
, both i) and ii) follow.
∎

Lemma 4.4.4
.

For all
n\geq 3
and
m\geq 0
such that
\min(\tfrac{2(n+m)-4}{3},\tfrac{2(n+m)-7}{2})\geq n-1
, the map
r
in (
4.4.5
) is surjective. (By
Lemma

4.4.3
, then so is
\tau_{*}
).

Proof.

By Igusa’s stability theorem
[
49
]
, the left vertical map in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{C}(X_{g}\times I^{2m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}\times I^{2m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{CE}_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}

is
\min(\tfrac{2(n+m)-4}{3},\tfrac{2(n+m)-7}{2})
-connected, whereas the right vertical map is
3(n+m)-5\geq n-1
. As explained in (
4.3.11
), the bottom horizontal map
r
is surjective on homotopy groups, so the result follows.
∎

From now on, let
m\geq 0
satisfy the assumption in
Lemma

4.4.4
. We now define Frobenius homomorphisms

\varphi_{d}:\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\Psi(X_{g}))\longrightarrow\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\Psi(X_{dg}))

(4.4.7)

which will be compatible with the map
\tau_{*}
in (
4.4.5
). Since
r
is surjective by
Lemma

4.4.4
and
\delta
is an isomorphism by
Lemma

4.4.3
, the map
\delta^{-1}\circ\overline{\tau}_{*}
will be Frobenius-preserving as long as we equip the right-bottom term in (
4.4.5
) with the Frobenii

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi}_{d}:\pi_{n-3}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\overline{\Psi}(S^{1}\to X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\Psi(X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\Psi(X_{dg}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n-3}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\overline{\Psi}(S^{1}\to X_{dg})).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

To define
\varphi_{d}
, let
p:X_{dg}\times I^{2m}\to X_{g}\times I^{2m}
be a fixed
d
-fold cover. Then
Lp:LX_{dg}\to LX_{g}
is a fibration with compact fibres (either empty or
d
-many points), so we can consider the diagram
