{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}X_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}X_{dg}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}LX_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}X_{dg},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Lp)^{!}}

where
c:(-)\to L(-)
stands for the inclusion as the constant loops, and
p^{!}
and
Lp^{!}
are the Becker–Gottlieb transfers of the fibrations
p
and
Lp
, respectively. By formal properties of the Becker–Gottlieb transfer, this square of spectra is homotopy commutative and hence, upon taking vertical cofibres and
\Omega^{\infty+2}(-)
, induces a map
(Lp)^{!}:\Omega^{2}Q(LX_{g}/X_{g})\to\Omega^{2}Q(LX_{dg}/X_{dg})
which, on homotopy groups gives

\varphi_{d}\coloneq\pi_{k}((Lp)^{!}):\pi_{k+2}^{s}(LX_{g},X_{g})\longrightarrow\pi_{k+2}^{s}(LX_{dg},X_{dg}).

(4.4.8)

Rationally, this gives the desired (
4.4.7
). We must now check that
\tau_{*}
is compatible with these Frobenii.

Construction 4.4.5
.

Let us first recall the description of the map

\tau_{*}:\pi_{k}(\operatorname{C}(M))\longrightarrow\pi_{k}(\Psi(M))\cong\pi_{k+2}^{s}(LM,M)\cong\Omega^{\mathrm{fr}}_{k+2}(LM,M)

given in
[
40
, pp. 23–24]
. Here
\Omega^{\mathrm{fr}}_{*}
stands for framed bordism theory, and the last isomorphism in the above equation is the one by Pontryagin–Thom. For simplicity, assume that
M
is stably framed
12
12

12

The homomorphism
\tau_{*}
factors through
\pi_{k}(\mathcal{C}(M))
as shown in
[
40
, pp. 26–27]
. If
M
is not stably framed, we can replace it with the disc bundle
D(\nu_{M})
of a normal bundle for
M
since Burghelea–Lashof construct in
[
16
, p. 10]
a factorisation
\operatorname{C}(M)\to\operatorname{C}(D(\xi))\to\mathcal{C}(M)
for any vector bundle
\xi
over
M
.
.

Given a concordance diffeomorphism
F=(f,g):M\times I\xrightarrow{\cong}M\times I
, a point
(x,s,t)\in M\times I\times I
is a
crossing
for
F
if it is either an

•

ordinary crossing
:
s<t
,
f(x,s)=f(x,t)
and
g(x,s)>g(x,t)
;

•

infinitesimal crossing
:
s=t
,
(Df)_{(x,s)}(0,1)=0
and
(Dg)_{(x,s)}(0,1)<0
.

A homotopy class in
\pi_{k}(\operatorname{C}(M))
can be represented by a smooth map
F:D^{k}\times M\times I\to M\times I
, seen as a family of maps
F_{z}:M\times I\to M\times I
for each
z\in D^{k}
, such that
F_{z}\in C(X_{g})
for every
z\in D^{k}
and
F_{z}=\Id_{M\times I}
if
z\in\partial D^{k}
. Under the generic transversality assumptions on
F
of Hypothesis 3.18 loc.cit., the set

\mathcal{M}_{F}\coloneq\{(z,x,s,t)\in D^{k}\times M\times I\times I:\text{$(x,s,t)$ is a crossing for $F_{z}$}\}

is a smooth
(k+2)
-dimensional manifold with boundary
\partial\mathcal{M}_{F}
, and the boundary points correspond precisely to the infinitesimal crossings of
F
. It is also stably framed, for the normal bundle of
\mathcal{M}_{F}-\partial\mathcal{M}_{F}
in
D^{k}\times M\times I\times I
can be identified with the pullback of the tangent bundle of
M
along

\mathcal{M}_{F}\longrightarrow M,\quad(z,x,s,t)\longmapsto f_{z}(x,s)=f_{z}(x,t),

map which is homotopic to the evident projection
\mathcal{M}_{F}\to M
via
\{f_{z}(x,rs)\}_{r\in[0,1]}
. (Here we use that
M
is stably framed by assumption.) Moreover, there is a natural map

q_{F}:(\mathcal{M}_{F},\partial{M}_{F})\longrightarrow(LM,M),\quad(z,x,s,t)\mapsto\big([0,1]\ni r\mapsto f_{z}(x,s+r(t-s))\big),

where we regard
M\subset LM
as the constant loops. Then:

\tau_{*}:\pi_{k}(\operatorname{C}(M))\longmapsto\pi_{k}(\Psi(M))\cong\Omega_{k+2}^{\mathrm{fr}}(LM,M),\quad[F]\longmapsto[q_{F}:(\mathcal{M}_{F},\partial\mathcal{M}_{F})\to(LM,M)].

This concludes the description of
\tau_{*}
.

We now show that
\tau_{*}
is Frobenius-preserving and explicitly describe the Frobenii on the target.

Proposition 4.4.6
.

The map
\tau_{*}:\pi_{k}(\operatorname{C}(X_{g}\times I^{2m}))\to\pi_{k}(\Psi(X_{g}))
of
4.4.5
is Frobenius-preserving if we equip the target with the Frobenii of (
4.4.8
), i.e., for every
d\in\mathbf{N}
, the following square commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k}(\operatorname{C}(X_{g}\times I^{2m}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k}(\operatorname{C}(X_{dg}\times I^{2m}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k+2}^{s}(LX_{g},X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k+2}^{s}(LX_{dg},X_{dg}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{d}=(Lp)^{!}}

(4.4.9)

Under the isomorphism
\pi_{n}^{s}(LX_{g},X_{g})\otimes\mathbf{Q}\cong H_{n}(LX_{g},X_{g};\mathbf{Q})\cong\bigoplus_{r\neq 0}H_{g}
, the Frobenius
\varphi_{d}=(Lp)^{!}
is:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{r\neq 0}H_{g}^{(r)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{s\neq 0}H_{dg}^{(s)},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{r\neq 0}\alpha_{r}}

where the subscripts
(-)^{(r)}
and
(-)^{(s)}
only serve bookkeeping purposes, and
\alpha_{r}
:

(i)

is zero if
d
does not divide
r
;

(ii)

is injective if
r=di
, and factors as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{g}^{(di)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{j=0}^{d-1}H_{g}\cong H_{dg}^{(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{s\neq 0}H_{dg}^{(s)}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

For the first part of
4.4.6
, fix a finite-sheeted cover
p:\widetilde{X}\to X
(we only need the special case
p:X_{dg}\to X_{g}
). Consider the homomorphism

\displaystyle\ell_{p}:\Omega_{k+2}^{\mathrm{fr}}(LX,X)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Omega_{k+2}^{\mathrm{fr}}(L\widetilde{X},\widetilde{X}),

\displaystyle[q:(W,\partial W)\to(LX,X)]

\displaystyle\longmapsto[q\times_{\id}\id:(W,\partial W)\times_{(LX,X)}(L\widetilde{X},\widetilde{X})\to(L\widetilde{X},\widetilde{X})],

which is well-defined as
L\widetilde{X}\to\widetilde{X}
is also finite-sheeted, and consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k}(\operatorname{C}(X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k}(\operatorname{C}(\widetilde{X}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{k+2}^{\mathrm{fr}}(LX,X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{k+2}^{\mathrm{fr}}(L\widetilde{X},\widetilde{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k+2}^{s}(LX,X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k+2}^{s}(L\widetilde{X},\widetilde{X}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PT}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PT}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Lp)^{!}}

(4.4.10)

where
\varphi_{p}
stands for the lifting map. When
p
is our preferred cover
X_{dg}\to X_{g}
, the outer square in (
4.4.10
) is (
4.4.9
). We shall show that both
1
and
2
commute.

Lemma 4.4.7
.

The square
1
in (
4.4.10
) commutes.

Proof.

This is nearly immediate. Represent a homotopy class
[F]\in\pi_{k}(\operatorname{C}(X))
by a smooth map
F:D^{k}\times X\times I\to X\times I
as in
4.4.5
, and let
\widetilde{F}:D^{k}\times\widetilde{X}\times I\to\widetilde{X}\times I
be its lift (thus representing
\varphi_{p}([F])
). Then, the map

\displaystyle(\mathcal{M}_{\widetilde{F}},\partial\mathcal{M}_{\widetilde{F}})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\mathcal{M}_{F},\partial\mathcal{M}_{F})\times_{(LX,X)}(L\widetilde{X},\widetilde{X})

\displaystyle(z,\widetilde{x},s,t)

\displaystyle\longmapsto((x,p(\widetilde{x}),s,t),q_{\widetilde{F}}(z,\widetilde{x},s,t))

is a diffeomorphism over
(L\widetilde{X},\widetilde{X})
, as desired.
∎

Lemma 4.4.8
.

The square
2
in (
4.4.10
) commutes. More generally, given a finite-sheeted cover
p:E\to B
, we can define the homomorphism

\ell_{p}:\Omega^{\mathrm{fr}}_{k}(B)\longrightarrow\Omega^{\mathrm{fr}}_{k}(E),\quad[q:W\to B]\longmapsto[q\times_{\id}\id:W\times_{B}E\to E].

Then, if there exists a fibrewise smooth embedding
\psi:E\subset B\times\mathbf{R}^{n}
, the following square commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{k}^{\mathrm{fr}}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{k}^{\mathrm{fr}}(E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k}^{s}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k}^{s}(E).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PT}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PT}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{!}}

Remark 4.4.9
.

When
p\colon E\to B
is a finite-sheeted cover, the homomorphism
\ell_{p}
is well defined since
W\times_{B}E
is a closed, stably framed
k
-manifold. If
p\colon E\to B
were a smooth fibre bundle with fibres closed, stably framed
d
-manifolds, then
W\times_{B}E
would be a
(k+d)
-manifold, and
\ell_{p}\colon\Omega_{k}^{\mathrm{fr}}(B)\to\Omega_{k+d}^{\mathrm{fr}}(E)
would necessarily raise degree. Thus, the assumption in
Lemma

4.4.8
that
p\colon E\to B
is a finite cover seems to be necessary.

Remark 4.4.10
.

For a finite-sheeted cover
p:\widetilde{X}\to X
of compact (smooth) manifolds, the cover
Lp:L\widetilde{X}\to LX
admits such a fibrewise smooth embedding as in the statement of
Lemma

4.4.8
: indeed, fixing an embedding
\phi:\widetilde{X}\subset\mathbf{R}^{n}
, the map

\psi:L\widetilde{X}\longrightarrow LX\times\mathbf{R}^{n},\quad\gamma\longmapsto(p\circ\gamma,\phi(\gamma(1))),

is such a fibrewise smooth embedding (so long as we replace
L(-)
with the homotopy equivalent space of smooth maps
C^{\infty}(S^{1},-)
).

Proof of
Lemma

4.4.8
.

We will need to recall the constructions of the Becker–Gottlieb transfer and the Pontryagin–Thom isomorphism. For the former, let
p:E\to B
be a smooth fibre bundle with compact manifold fibres (we will later specialise to the case of a finite cover), and let
\tau
and
\nu
denote the vertical tangent and normal bundles of a fibrewise smooth embedding
\psi:E\subset B\times\mathbf{R}^{n}
. We obtain

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{n}\wedge B_{+}=\mathrm{Th}(B\times\mathbf{R}^{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Th}(\nu)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Th}(\tau\oplus\nu)=S^{n}\wedge E_{+},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{!}}

where
\mathrm{Th}(-)
stands for the Thom space and
\psi^{!}
is the Pontryagin–Thom collapse map along
\psi
. This construction stabilises as
n\to\infty
to the Becker–Gottlieb transfer for
p
:

p^{!}:\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}B\longrightarrow\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}E.

A property of the Becker–Gottlieb transfer that we will need
[
5
, Eq. 3.2]
is that if
p^{\prime}:E^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
is another smooth fibre bundle and
h:E\to E^{\prime}
is a map of smooth fibre bundles, then the following square commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}E^{\prime}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p^{\prime})^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

(4.4.11)

As for the Pontryagin–Thom isomorphism

PT:\Omega_{k}^{\mathrm{fr}}(X)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\pi_{k}^{s}(X),

given a map
q:W\to X
representing a framed bordism class (so
W
is a stably framed closed
k
-manifold), we can find an embedding
\varphi:W\subset\mathbf{R}^{m}
with trivial normal bundle. Then, the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{m}=\Th(\mathbf{R}^{m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Th(\nu_{\varphi})\simeq W_{+}\wedge S^{m-k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{+}\wedge S^{m-k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\wedge\id}

stabilises, as
m\to\infty
, to a map
\mathbf{S}^{k}\to\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}W
representing
PT([q:W\to X])\in\pi_{k}^{s}(X)
.

With this in mind, let
p:E\to B
be a finite-sheeted cover as in the statement, let
q:W\to B
represent a framed bordism class in
\Omega^{\mathrm{fr}}_{k}(B)
, and choose an embedding
\varphi:W\subset\mathbf{R}^{m}
. This yields

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi:W\times_{B}E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\times_{B}(B\times\mathbf{R}^{n})=W\times\mathbf{R}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{R}^{m}\times\mathbf{R}^{n}=\mathbf{R}^{m+n}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id\times_{B}\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\times\id}

whose normal bundle is again trivial. We need to show that the outer square in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{m+n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{m-k+n}\wedge W_{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{m-k+n}\wedge B_{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Th}(\nu_{\Phi})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{m-k+n}\wedge(W\times_{B}E)_{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{m-k+n}\wedge E_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi\times\id_{\mathbf{R}^{n}})^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{W}^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id\wedge q_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id\wedge(\mathrm{pr}_{E})_{+}}

(4.4.12)

is homotopy commutative. The dashed arrow
\mathrm{pr}_{W}^{!}
is the (unstable) Becker–Gottlieb transfer for the finite cover
\mathrm{pr}_{W}:W\times_{B}E\to W
. Then, the right subsquare commutes since it is an unstable instance of (
4.4.11
) for the map of smooth fibre bundles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\times_{B}E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

Regarding the left subsquare in (
4.4.12
), note that the (unstable) Becker–Gottlieb transfer for
\mathrm{pr}_{W}
arises from the fibrewise smooth embedding
\id\times_{B}\psi:W\times_{B}E\hookrightarrow W\times\mathbf{R}^{n}
. Thus, because the Pontryagin–Thom collapse map is functorial on compositions of embeddings, it follows that the left subsquare is commutative by definition of
\Phi
. Of course, one needs to check that the commutativity of the diagram (
4.4.12
) stabilises appropriately as
m\to\infty
, but this is routine. This concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of
4.4.6
.

Lemmas
4.4.7
and
4.4.8
establish the commutativity of (
4.4.9
). It remains to show that the Becker–Gottlieb transfer
(Lp)^{!}:\pi_{n}^{s}(LX_{g},X_{g})\otimes\mathbf{Q}\to\pi_{n}^{s}(LX_{dg},X_{dg})\otimes\mathbf{Q}
yields the claimed formula under the isomorphisms
\pi_{n}^{s}(LX_{g},X_{g})\otimes\mathbf{Q}\cong\bigoplus_{r\neq 0}H_{g}
and
\pi_{n}^{s}(LX_{dg},X_{dg})\otimes\mathbf{Q}\cong\bigoplus_{r\neq 0}H_{dg}
.

Observe that
p:X_{dg}\to X_{g}
sends the generator of
\pi_{1}(X_{dg})
to
t^{d}\in\pi
, and hence
Lp
factors as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lp:LX_{dg}=\coprod_{s\in\mathbf{Z}}L_{s}X_{dg}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod_{s\in\mathbf{Z}}L_{ds}X_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod_{r\in\mathbf{Z}}L_{r}X_{g}=LX_{g}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod_{s\in\mathbf{Z}}L_{s}p}

Thus, the connected component
L_{r}X_{g}
is hit by
Lp
if and only if
d
divides
r
. This proves claim (i).

It remains to understand the Becker–Gottlieb transfer of the cover
L_{i}p:L_{i}X_{dg}\to L_{di}X_{g}
on
H_{n}(-,\mathbf{Q})
. Note that
(L_{i}p)^{!}:H_{n}(L_{di}X_{g};\mathbf{Q})\to H_{n}(L_{i}X_{dg};\mathbf{Q})
is readily seen to be injective since, by formal properties of the Becker–Gottlieb transfer
[
5
, Eq. 3.4]
, the composition
(L_{i}p)_{*}\circ(L_{i}p)^{!}
is given by multiplication by
d
, which is invertible over
\mathbf{Q}
. Recall that from our computation of
H_{n}(LX_{g};\mathbf{Q})
in
Lemma

4.4.3
using the spectral sequence (
4.4.6
), it follows that the map
(\mathrm{ev}_{1})_{*}:H_{n}(L_{r}X_{g};\mathbf{Q})\to H_{n}(X_{g};\mathbf{Q})
is an isomorphism for all
r\in\mathbf{Z}
. Moreover, by (
4.4.11
), the left subsquare in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n}(L_{di}X_{g};\mathbf{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n}(X_{g};\mathbf{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{j=0}^{d-1}H_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n}(L_{i}X_{dg};\mathbf{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n}(X_{dg};\mathbf{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{dg}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{ev}_{1})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(L_{i}p)^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{ev}_{1})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

is commutative. The right subsquare is easily seen to commute, yielding claim (ii).
∎

To finish our computation of the cyclotomic structure on
\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}))
, by (
4.4.2
), it remains to understand the action of the
h
-cobordism involution
\iota_{\H}
on
\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}\times I^{2m}))\cong\bigoplus_{r\neq 0}H_{g}
. Since the map
r:(\Omega\operatorname{C}(X_{g}),\iota_{\operatorname{C}})\to(\Omega\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}),\iota_{\H})
is anti-equivariant by
A.1.5
and surjective by
Lemma

4.4.4
, it suffices to equip
\bigoplus_{r\neq 0}H_{g}
with an involution for which the map
\tau_{*}
is anti-equivariant. For this, recall the free loop space involution
\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}
on
LX_{g}
induced by complex conjugation on
S^{1}\subset\mathbf{C}
.

Lemma 4.4.11
.

Let
M
be a compact manifold. The map
\tau_{*}:\pi_{*}(\operatorname{C}(M))\longrightarrow\pi_{*+2}^{s}(LM,M)
is
C_{2}
-equivariant for
\iota_{\operatorname{C}}
on the domain and
\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}
on the codomain.

Proof.

As explained in
4.4.5
, we may assume that
M
is stably framed. The concordance involution
\iota_{\operatorname{C}}
sends a concordance diffeomorphism
F=(f,g):M\times I\to M\times I
to
\overline{F}=(\overline{f},\overline{g})
, where

\overline{f}(x,t)=f_{1}^{-1}\circ f(x,1-t),\qquad\overline{g}(x,t)=1-g(x,1-t),\qquad(x,t)\in M\times I,

and
f_{1}=f\mid_{M\times 1}:M\cong M
. It is straightforward to check that if
(x,s,t)\in M\times I\times I
is a crossing for
F
(in the sense of
4.4.5
), then
(x,1-t,1-s)
is a crossing for
\overline{F}
. It follows that if
F:D^{k}\times M\times I\to M\times I
is a family of concordance embeddings representing a homotopy class in
\pi_{k}C(M)
(and which satisfies the generic transversality assumtion), then there is a diffeomorphism

\xi:(\mathcal{M}_{F},\partial\mathcal{M}_{F})\longrightarrow(\mathcal{M}_{\overline{F}},\partial\mathcal{M}_{\overline{F}}),\quad(z,x,s,t)\longmapsto(z,x,1-t,1-s).

Moreover, if
(z,x,s,t)\in\mathcal{M}_{F}
, then the loop
q_{\overline{F}}(z,x,1-t,1-s)
is
f^{-1}_{1}(\overline{q_{F}(z,x,s,t)})
. But the diffeomorphism
f_{1}
is homotopic to the identity (via
\{f\mid_{M\times r}\}_{0\leq r\leq 1}
), and hence
q_{F}
and
\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}\circ q_{\overline{F}}\circ\xi
are homotopic.
∎

The upshot of this section is the following.

Proposition 4.4.12
.

For each
d\in\mathbf{N}
, the Frobenius map

\varphi_{d}:\pi_{n-1}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\big)\longrightarrow\pi_{n-1}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{dg})\big)

is isomorphic to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{r>0}H_{g}^{(r)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{s>0}H_{dg}^{(s)},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{r>0}\alpha_{r}}

where the subscripts
(-)^{(r)}
and
(-)^{(s)}
only serve bookkeeping purposes, and
\alpha_{r}
:

(i)

is zero if
d
does not divide
r
;

(ii)

is injective if
r=di
, and factors as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{g}^{(di)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{j=0}^{d-1}H_{g}\cong H_{dg}^{(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{s>0}H_{dg}^{(s)}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

Proof.

The isomorphism
\pi_{*+1}(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}))\cong\pi_{*}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}))
is Frobenius-preserving, so it suffices to prove the claim for the Frobenius of the latter. Under the isomorphism
H_{n}(LX_{g},X_{g};\mathbf{Q})\cong\bigoplus_{r\neq 0}H_{g}
,
-\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}
on the left becomes the involution on the right-hand side that identifies
H_{g}^{(r)}
with
H_{g}^{(-r)}
via the
(-1)
-map. Thus, by
Lemma

4.4.11
and (
4.4.2
) (noting
\iota_{\operatorname{C}}=-\iota_{\H}
), we recover our isomorphism
\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}))\cong\bigoplus_{r>0}H_{g}
. The claim now follows from the second part of
4.4.6
and because all the maps in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\operatorname{C}(X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

are Frobenius-preserving.
∎

There are two additional pieces of data that, together with the Frobenii, will be useful to keep track of:

(a)

There is an embedding
\iota_{0}:X_{g}\hookrightarrow X_{dg}
which, under the evident homotopy equivalences
X_{g}\simeq S^{1}\vee(\vee^{2g}S^{n})
and
X_{dg}\simeq S^{1}\vee(\vee_{i=0}^{d-1}(\vee^{2g}S^{n}))
, lies in the homotopy class of the map
S^{1}\vee(\vee^{2g}S^{n})\to S^{1}\vee(\vee_{i=0}^{d-1}(\vee^{2g}S^{n}))
that is the identity on the
S^{1}
summand, and on the other summand is the canonical inclusion
\vee^{2g}S^{n}\to\vee_{i=0}^{d-1}(\vee^{2g}S^{n})
as the
i=0
summand. See
Figure

1
. This embedding gives rise to a map of fibre sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\Emb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{dg})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\Emb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{dg})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{dg}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota_{0})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota_{0})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota_{0})_{*}}

(4.4.13)

where, the middle and right vertical maps are induced by extension by the identity on the complement of the embedding
\iota_{0}
. We would like to understand the map

(\iota_{0})_{*}:\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}))\longrightarrow\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{dg})).

Figure 1:
Depiction of the image of
\iota_{0}:X_{g}\hookrightarrow X_{dg}
for
d=2
and
2n=2
.

(b)

Conjugation by the rotation
C_{d}
-action on the manifold
X_{dg}
gives rise to another
C_{d}
-action on the bottom fibre sequence in (
4.4.13
). We should keep track of the
C_{d}
-action on
\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{dg}))
.

We finish this section by recording this data.

Lemma 4.4.13
.

Under the isomorphism
\pi_{n-1}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{dg})\big)\cong\bigoplus_{r>0}H_{dg}
of
4.4.12
,

(a)

the map
(\iota_{0})_{*}:\pi_{n-1}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\big)\to\pi_{n-1}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{dg})\big)
becomes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{r>0}H_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{r>0}\bigoplus_{j=0}^{d-1}H_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{r>0}H_{dg};}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j=0}

(b)

the
C_{d}
-action on the group
\pi_{n-1}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{dg})\big)
is the diagonal
C_{d}
-action on
\bigoplus_{r>0}H_{dg}
, where
C_{d}
acts on
H_{dg}\cong\bigoplus_{j=0}^{d-1}H_{g}
by permuting the summands.

Proof.

Both (a) and (b) are almost immediate from the work we have already done: for (a), note that there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}(\operatorname{C}(X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*+2}^{s}(LX_{g},X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}(\operatorname{C}(X_{dg}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*+2}^{s}(LX_{dg},X_{dg}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota_{0})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota_{0})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{*}}

where the right vertical map
(\iota_{0})_{*}
is the one induced by the map of pairs
(L\iota_{0},\iota_{0}):(LX_{g},X_{g})\to(LX_{dg},X_{dg})
. Now in our identification
H_{n}(LX_{g},X_{g};\mathbf{Q})\cong\bigoplus_{r\in\mathbf{Z}}H_{g}
, the
r
-th summand corresponds to the
n
-th rational homology of the pair
(L_{r}X_{g},X_{g})
, and the map
(\iota_{0})_{*}
on it behaves as the inclusion
H_{g}\xhookrightarrow{}\bigoplus_{j=0}^{d-1}H_{g}\cong H_{dg}
of the
j=0
-summand. Then, the claim follows by the same argument as in the previous proof.

For (b), again note that the map
\tau_{*}:\pi_{*}(\operatorname{C}(X_{dg}))\to\pi_{*+2}^{s}(LX_{dg},X_{dg})
is
C_{d}
-equivariant and, under the isomorphism
H_{n}(LX_{dg},X_{dg};\mathbf{Q})\cong\bigoplus_{r\in\mathbf{Z}}H_{dg}
, the
C_{d}
-action becomes the diagonal action described in the statement. The same line of reasoning as before applies.
∎

4.4.2
The
\mathbf{Z}[\mathbf{N}^{\times}]
-module structure on block embeddings of
X_{g}

Recall from
[
22
, Cor. 6.2]
that there is a map

\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}\xrightarrow{\simeq_{\mathbf{Q}}}\BAut_{\partial}^{\cong}(X_{g})

which is a rational equivalence; this map is also Frobenius-preserving, so it will be enough for us to study these in the codomain. Recall from
[
22
, Eq. (2)]
that there is a fibration sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{\partial}(X_{g},X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{S^{1}}(X_{g},X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{S^{1}}(S^{1}\times S^{2n-2},X_{g}),}

(4.4.14)

where the decoration on the middle and right mapping spaces demands maps to be fixed on
S^{1}\times*\subset S^{1}\times S^{2n-2}=\partial X_{g}
. This fibration sequence is compatible with the lifting Frobenius maps, and since the fibre inclusion is injective in rational homotopy groups, we will only need to understand the Frobenii on the rational homotopy groups of the middle term.

Lemma 4.4.14
.

Fix a
d
-th root of unity
\zeta\in S^{1}
. Then, there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{S^{1}}(X_{g},X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{S^{1}}(X_{dg},X_{dg})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{*}(X_{dg},X_{dg})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i=0}^{d-1}\Map_{S^{1}}(X_{dg},X_{dg}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota_{0})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,\zeta,\dots,\zeta^{d-1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}

where
(\iota_{0})_{*}
denotes the "extension-by-the-identity" map induced by the embedding
\iota_{0}:X_{g}\hookrightarrow X_{dg}
of
Figure

1
, the horizontal bottom maps are conjugation by the rotation action of
\zeta^{i}
, and the right vertical map stands for composition of self-maps. Thus, on homotopy groups, the
d
-th Frobenius is given by

\varphi_{d}(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}t^{i}\cdot(\iota_{0})_{*}(x),\quad x\in\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\Map_{S^{1}}(X_{dg},X_{dg})\cong H_{g}\otimes\pi_{*+n}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g}).

Moreover, it is injective.

Proof.

There is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{S^{1}}(X_{g},X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{S^{1}}(X_{dg},X_{dg})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{*}(\vee^{2g}S^{n},X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{*}(\vee^{2g}S^{n},X_{dg})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i=0}^{d-1}\Map_{\zeta^{i}}(\vee^{2g}S^{n},X_{dg}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{d}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{res}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota_{0})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,\zeta,\dots,\zeta^{d-1})}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}

(4.4.15)

where
(\iota_{0})_{*}
denotes postcomposition by
\iota_{0}:X_{g}\to X_{dg}
,
\Map_{\zeta^{i}}
stands for the mapping space that sends the wedge point
*\in\vee^{2g}S^{n}
to
\zeta^{i}\in S^{1}\subset X_{g}
, the left vertical map “res” is given by restriction to the core of the
n
-handles of
X_{g}
, the vertical map
e
sends
(f_{0},\dots,f_{d-1})
to the map
f:X_{dg}\to X_{dg}
which is the identity on
S^{1}
and is
f_{i}
when restricted to the
i
-th wedge of cores
\vee_{\zeta^{i}}^{2g}S^{n}\subset X_{dg}
, and finally the lower horizontal maps
\zeta^{i}
are given by post-composition by the rotation
C_{d}
-action on
X_{dg}
. One immediately verifies that the bottom composition in (
4.4.15
) coincides with the one in the diagram of the statement. The last claim in the statement holds since the bottom composition in (
4.4.15
) is visibly injective on homotopy groups.
∎

Corollary 4.4.15
.

The
d
-th Frobenius on
\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})_{\ell})
is injective and is given by

\varphi_{d}(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}t^{i}\cdot(\iota_{0})_{*}(x),\quad x\in\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}),

where
(\iota_{0})_{*}
is as in
Equation

4.4.13
and
t^{i}
is conjugation by the rotation action of
C_{d}=\langle t\rangle/\langle t^{d}\rangle
on
X_{dg}
.

Proof.

This follows from
Lemma

4.4.14
and since
\Map_{\partial}(X_{g},X_{g})\to\Map_{S^{1}}(X_{g},X_{g})
is injective on
\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}
.
∎

4.5
Homotopy of embeddings spaces of
X_{g}

As mentioned at the beginning of this section, we consider the fibre sequence (
4.0.1
) and use it to compute the rational homotopy groups of
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}
in the range of degrees
\leq 2n-2
. In this range, we have

\pi^{\mathbf{Q}}_{*\geq 2}\left(\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}\right)\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\ker\left([-,-]_{\pi}:\pi_{n}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g})\otimes_{\pi}\pi_{2n-1}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g})\to[\pi_{3n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g})]_{\pi}\right)&*=n,\\[4.0pt]
\ker\left([-,-]_{\pi}:\pi_{n}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g})\otimes_{\pi}\pi_{3n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g})\to[\pi_{4n-3}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g})]_{\pi}\right)&*=2n-1,\\[4.0pt]
0&\text{otherwise,}\end{array}\right.

where
[-,-]_{\pi}
is the map obtained by taking
\pi
-coinvariants of the Whitehead bracket map
[-,-]:\pi_{p-1}(X_{g})\otimes\pi_{q-1}(X_{g})\to\pi_{p+q-1}(X_{g})
, which is
\pi
-equivariant using the diagonal action on the source, by
[
22
, Thm. 3.1]
(see
2.3.1
) and
2.1.1
.

We observe that this isomorphism is equivariant with respect to the action of
\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}\cong\pi_{1}(\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})_{\ell})
on its higher homotopy groups (i.e. the lefthand side) and the diagonal action on the tensor product
\pi_{n}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g})\otimes_{\pi}\pi_{2n-1}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g})
by the following argument: since this isomorphism is the composition of the induced map of
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}\to\BAut_{\partial}(X_{g})
with the isomorphism in
2.3.1
, it suffices to prove that the action of
\pi_{1}(\BAut_{\partial}(X_{g}))\cong\pi_{0}(\Aut_{\partial}(X_{g}))
on its higher rational homotopy groups agrees with the diagonal action in
\smash{\pi_{n}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g})\otimes_{\pi}\pi_{n+*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g})}
under the isomorphism of
2.3.1
. By loc.cit, it suffices to show that the latter action agrees with the conjugation action in loc.cit(i) (and the fact that, for any group-like topological monoid, the action of
\pi_{0}(G)
on
\pi_{k}(\text{{B}}G)
, under the isomorphism
\pi_{k}(\text{{B}}G)\cong\pi_{k-1}(G)
, is the conjugation action), on
\Hom_{\mathbf{Q}[\pi]}(\pi_{n}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g}),\pi_{n+*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g}))
under the isomorphism

\pi_{n}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g})\otimes_{\pi}\pi_{n+*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g})\to\Hom_{\mathbf{Q}[\pi]}(\pi_{n}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g}),\pi_{n+*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g}))

taking
x\otimes y
to
\lambda_{X}(-,x)\cdot y
. This follows from the fact that any
\phi\in\Aut_{\partial}(X_{g})
preserves the form
\lambda_{X}
.

To compute the homotopy groups of the total space of (
4.0.1
), we must understand the connecting map between the homotopy groups of the base and the fibre in degree
n
. The next lemma considers this problem.

Lemma 4.5.1
.

The connecting map

\delta_{n}:\pi_{n}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}\right)\to\pi_{n-1}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\big)

is zero.

Proof.

First observe that, by
4.4.12
(i), the Frobenii on the target of
\delta_{n}
are such that for any
y\in\pi_{n-1}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\big)
there exists some
d\in\mathbf{N}
such that
\varphi_{d}(y)=0
. Thus, given
x\in\pi_{n}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}\right)
, let
d\in\mathbf{N}
be such that
\varphi_{d}(\delta_{n}(x))=0
. Since the Frobenii fit in maps of fibre sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{dg})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{dg})_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{dg})_{\ell},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{d}}

(4.5.1)

it follows that
\delta_{n}
commutes with them, so
0=\varphi_{d}(\delta_{n}(x))=\delta_{n}(\varphi_{d}(x))=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\delta_{n}\left(t^{i}\cdot(\iota_{0})_{*}(x)\right)
, where the last equality is
4.4.15
. Note that
\delta_{n}
is
C_{d}
-equivariant, for the bottom fibre sequence in (
4.5.1
) is so, and by (
4.4.13
), we also get that
(\iota_{0})_{*}
and
\delta_{n}
commute. All in all, we get

0=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}t^{i}\cdot(\iota_{0})_{*}(\varphi_{d}(x)).

By
Lemma

4.4.13
, this can only happen if
\varphi_{d}(x)=0
. But since
\varphi_{d}
on
\pi_{n}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}\right)
is injective by
4.4.15
, we must have that
x=0
, establishing the claim.
∎

From the long exact sequence of (
4.0.1
), we obtain the following computation of the homotopy groups of the embedding space in question.

Proposition 4.5.2
.

The rational homotopy groups of
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}
in degrees
2\leq*\leq 2n-2
are given as
\mathbf{Q}[\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle]
-modules by

\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}\right)\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\bigoplus_{r>0}H_{g},&*=n-1\\[4.0pt]
\ker\left([-,-]_{\pi}:\pi_{n}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g})\otimes_{\pi}\pi_{2n-1}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g})\to[\pi_{3n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g})]_{\pi}\right),&*=n\\[4.0pt]
\coker(\delta_{2n-1}),&*=2n-2\\[4.0pt]
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

4.6
Homology of embedding spaces of
X_{g}

In this section, we wish to calculate the homotopy groups of the space
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}^{+}
in a range. To do so, we start by computing the homology of the universal cover of the latter space before taking the plus-construction and colimit over
g
, which we denote by
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}^{\sim}
. We start by setting notation to simplify our exposition.

Notation 4.6.1
.

Recall
4.5.2
and let
L
,
M
and
N
denote the following
\mathbf{Q}[\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle]
-modules:

L\coloneq\pi_{n-1}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}\right),\qquad M\coloneq\pi_{n}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}\right),\qquad N\coloneq\pi_{2n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}\right).

We start by noticing that the Postnikov truncation
\tau_{\leq 2n-3}\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}^{\sim}
is given by
K(L,n-1)\times K(M,n)
since there are no possible
k
-invariants for degree reasons. Thus, by looking at the Serre spectral sequence of the map from
(2n-2)
-nd Postnikov truncation to the
(2n-3)
-rd of
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}^{\sim}
, we obtain:

Proposition 4.6.2
.

The rational homology groups of
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}^{\sim}
as
\mathbf{Q}[\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle]
-modules in degrees
*\leq 2n-2
are given by

\widetilde{\H}_{*}\left(\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}^{\sim};\mathbf{Q}\right)\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}L&*=n-1,\\[4.0pt]
M&*=n,\\[4.0pt]
S^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle(n-1)}(L)\oplus\coker(d:L\otimes M\to N),&*=2n-2,\\[4.0pt]
0&\text{otherwise,}\end{array}\right.

where
d
is a certain map.

Lemma 4.6.3
.

Let
G
be a group,
k\geq 1
and let
f:X\to Y
be a
G
-equivariant map of simple
G
-spaces
13
13

13

That is, simple spaces equipped with a
G
-action.
which induces an isomorphism on
\H_{*}(-;\mathbf{Q})
for
*\leq k
and a surjection for
*=k+1,k+2
. Moreover, assume that there is an isomorphism of
\mathbf{Q}[G]
-modules
\H_{k+1}(X;\mathbf{Q})\cong\H_{k+1}(Y;\mathbf{Q})\oplus A
, where the map to the first factor is
\H_{k+1}(f;\mathbf{Q})
and
A
is a
\mathbf{Q}[G]
-module. Then,
f
induces an isomorphism on
\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(-)
for
*\leq k
and
\pi_{k+1}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X)\cong\pi_{k+1}^{\mathbf{Q}}(Y)\oplus A
as
\mathbf{Q}[G]
-modules.

Proof.

We start by proving the following subclaim: for any integer
i
and a map
\phi:\H_{n}(X;\mathbf{Q})\to V
of
\mathbf{Q}
-vector spaces, there exists a map
\Phi:X\to K(V,n)
such that
\phi=\text{hur}^{-1}\circ\H_{n}(\Phi;\mathbf{Q})
where
\text{hur}^{-1}:\H_{n}(K(V,n);\mathbf{Q})\to\pi_{n}^{\mathbf{Q}}(K(V,n))\cong V
is the inverse of the rational Hurewicz map. To prove this, recall that the Kronecker pairing map
\kappa:\H^{n}(X;V)\to\Hom_{\mathbf{Q}}(\H_{n}(X;\mathbf{Q}),V)
is an isomorphism, by the universal coefficient theorem. Moreover, a class in
\H^{n}(X;V)
determines a unique homotopy class of maps
X\to K(V,n)
. Choose
\Phi
to correspond to the class that maps to
\phi
under
\kappa
. One sees that the induced map
\H_{n}(\Phi;\mathbf{Q})
is precisely
\text{hur}\circ\phi
.

Using this subclaim, we can construct the map
F=(f,\Phi):X\to Y\times K(A,k+1)
whose induced map on
\H_{k+1}(-;\mathbf{Q})
witnesses the isomorphism
\H_{k+1}(X;\mathbf{Q})\cong\H_{k+1}(Y;\mathbf{Q})\oplus A
in the statement. Thus,
F
induces an isomorphism on
\H_{*}(-;\mathbf{Q})
for
*\leq k+1
. Moreover, as
\H_{k+2}(K(A,k+1);\mathbf{Q})
vanishes (since
k\geq 1
), the map
F
induces a surjection on
\H_{k+2}(-;\mathbf{Q})
. On the other hand, as both source and target of
F
are simple spaces, we deduce from
[
44
, Cor. 3.4]
for
C
the class of torsion groups that
F
is rationally
(k+2)
-connected.

To deal with the
G
-equivariance, note that by construction of
F=(f,\Phi)
, and since
f
itself is
G
-equivariant, it suffices to show that the map
\pi_{k+1}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\Phi)
is
G
-equivariant: this map fits in a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k+1}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k+1}^{\mathbf{Q}}(K(A,k+1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H_{k+1}(X;\mathbf{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H_{k+1}(K(A,k+1);\mathbf{Q}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{hur}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k+1}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\Phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{hur}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H_{k+1}(\Phi;\mathbf{Q})}

By construction,
a\circ\mathrm{hur}^{-1}\circ H_{k+1}(\Phi;\mathbf{Q})
agrees with the projection
H_{k+1}(X;\mathbf{Q})\to A
, which is
G
-equivariant by assumption; since both
a
and the Hurewicz homomorphism are
G
-equivariant, the claim follows.
∎

Let
\mathcal{L}_{X}
denote the
\mathbf{Q}[\langle\rho\rangle]
-module given by

\mathcal{L}_{X}\coloneq\colim_{g}\,\bigl[S^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle(n-1)}(L)\oplus\coker(d:L\otimes M\to N)\bigr]_{\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}}

where
d
is the map in
4.6.2
. Observe also that conjugation by
\rho=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{\oplus g}
(with respect to the
\mathbf{Z}[\pi]
-basis
\{a_{1},b_{1},\dots,a_{g},b_{g}\}
) on
\mathrm{U}_{g}^{\mathrm{ext},\ell}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n})
induces a
\langle\rho\rangle
-action on the colimit
\text{{B}}\mathrm{U}_{\infty}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n}^{\min})
. Equipped with this action, the standard map
\smash{\text{{B}}\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{\infty}}\to\text{{B}}\mathrm{U}_{\infty}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n}^{\min})
is easily seen to be
\langle\rho\rangle
-equivariant.

Theorem 4.6.4
.

In the range
*\leq 2n-2
, we have an isomorphism of
\mathbf{Q}[\langle\rho\rangle]
-modules

\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{\infty})_{\ell}^{+}\right)\cong\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\text{{B}}\mathrm{U}_{\infty}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n}^{\min})^{+}\right)\oplus\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathcal{L}_{X}&*=2n-2,\\[4.0pt]
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Proof.

We want to use
Lemma

4.6.3
for the map of simple spaces
r:\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{\infty})_{\ell}^{+}\to\text{{B}}\mathrm{U}_{\infty}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n}^{\min})^{+}
, with
A=\mathcal{L}_{X}
. To do so, first show that
r
induces an isomorphism on
\H_{*}(-;\mathbf{Q})
for
*\leq 2n-3
and a surjection for
*=2n-2,2n-1.
By definition,
r
factors as
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{\infty})_{\ell}^{+}\to(\smash{\text{{B}}\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{\infty}})^{+}\to\text{{B}}\mathrm{U}_{\infty}(\mathbf{Z}[\pi],\Lambda_{n}^{\min})^{+}
, and the second map is a rational equivalence by
3.3.1
. This reduces to showing that the first map induces an isomorphism on
\H_{*}(-;\mathbf{Q})
for
*\leq 2n-3
and a surjection for
*=2n-2,2n-1.
Consider the Serre spectral sequence with rational coefficients for the universal cover fibre sequence

\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{\infty})_{\ell}^{\sim}\to\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{\infty})_{\ell}\to\smash{\text{{B}}\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{\infty}}.

(4.6.1)

Observe that the
\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}
-modules
L
and
M
from
4.6.1
are gr-odd in the sense of
3.4.1
: recall that
L
is a direct sum of
H_{g}
, which is odd, and
M
is a submodule of
\smash{H_{X_{g}}}\otimes_{\pi}\pi_{2n-1}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X_{g})
, which is a tensor product of an odd module with an even module, hence odd by
3.4.4
. We conclude from
3.4.5
that the homology groups of
\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}
with coefficients in
L
or
M
vanish in all degrees. Using
4.6.2
, we can then compute the groups
E^{2}_{s,t}
of the spectral sequence of (
4.6.1
) for
t\leq 2n-3
in the following way: for
t=0
, then
E^{2}_{s,0}\cong\H_{s}(\smash{\text{{B}}\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{\infty}};\mathbf{Q})
. For
t=n-1
, we have
E^{2}_{s,n-1}
is the homology of
\smash{\text{{B}}\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{\infty}}
in degree
s
with coefficients in the colimit of the
\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}
-modules
L
over
g
, which vanishes as it is the colimit of the groups
\H_{s}(\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}};L)
, which vanishes. Similarly, we see that
E_{s,n}^{2}
vanishes for all
s\geq 0
. Otherwise, we see that
E^{2}_{s,t}
vanishes, since
\smash{\widetilde{\H}_{*}\left(\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}^{\sim};\mathbf{Q}\right)}
vanishes by
4.6.2
. We conclude that for
*\leq 2n-3
, the map
\H_{*}(\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{\infty})_{\ell};\mathbf{Q})\to\H_{*}(\smash{\text{{B}}\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{\infty}};\mathbf{Q})
is an isomorphism, hence also for the induced map after plus-construction as the homology remains unchanged.

We argue now that the latter is surjective for
*=2n-2,2n-1,
which uses the explicit computation of
\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}((\smash{\text{{B}}\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{\infty}})^{+})
from
[
22
, 1677]
as an input. Recall that the spaces
\smash{\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{\infty})^{+}_{\ell}}
and
\smash{(\text{{B}}\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g})^{+}}
are equivalent to the identity component of the group completion of the monoids given by the disjoint unions of the spaces
\{\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}\}_{g\geq 0}
and
\{\text{{B}}\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}\}_{g\geq 0}
, respectively—in particular, they are path-connected. Thus, the map
\pi:\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{\infty})^{+}_{\ell}\to(\smash{\text{{B}}\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{\infty}})^{+}
is equivalent to an
\mathbf{E}_{1}
-map of connected
\mathbf{E}_{1}
-spaces. However, by the Milnor-Moore theorem, we know that the
\mathbf{Q}
-algebra
\H_{*}(\mathbb{X};\mathbf{Q})
of a connected
\mathbf{E}_{1}
-space
\mathbb{X}
is isomorphic to the universal enveloping algebra of the Lie algebra
\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathbb{X})
, induced by the
\mathbf{E}_{1}
-structure on
\mathbb{X}.
Thus, we deduce that for any
k\geq 0
, the vector space
\H_{k}(\mathbb{X};\mathbf{Q})
is generated by products of classes in
\H_{l}(\mathbb{X};\mathbf{Q})
for
l<k
and
\pi_{k}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathbb{X}).
From
[
22
, 1677]
(together with
3.3.1
), it follows that
\pi_{*}((\smash{\text{{B}}\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{\infty}})^{+})
vanishes for
*=2n-2,2n-1
. Thus, the vector spaces
\H_{*}((\smash{\text{{B}}\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{\infty}})^{+};\mathbf{Q})
for
*\leq 2n-2,2n-1
are generated by products of classes in degrees
l\leq 2n-3
. However, those are hit by the map
\H_{l}(\pi;\mathbf{Q})
by the discussion above (and the fact that
\pi
is an
\mathbf{E}_{1}
-map). Thus, we conclude that the map
\H_{*}(\pi;\mathbf{Q})
is surjective for
*\leq 2n-1.

We finish by showing that the kernel of the map
\H_{2n-2}(\pi;\mathbf{Q})
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{L}_{X}
, which establishes the whole claim by applying
Lemma

4.6.3
. Let us return to the Serre spectral sequence of (
4.6.1
). Observe that, by
4.6.2
,
E^{2}_{0,2n-2}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{L}_{X}
. In degree
2n-2
, there is only one possible non-trivial differential hitting
E^{2}_{0,2n-2}
, namely the
d^{2n-1}
from
E^{2}_{2n-1,0}
. However, the surjective map
\H_{2n-1}(\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{\infty})_{\ell};\mathbf{Q})\to\H_{2n-1}(\smash{\text{{B}}\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{\infty}};\mathbf{Q})\cong E^{2}_{2n-1,0}
factors through the edge homomorphism
E^{\infty}_{2n-1,0}\hookrightarrow E^{2}_{2n-1,0}.
Hence the latter is surjective and thus an isomorphism. We conclude that there are no non-trivial differentials out of
E^{2}_{2n-1,0}.
From that, we see that the kernel of
\H_{2n-2}(\pi;\mathbf{Q})
is isomorphic to
E^{2}_{0,2n-2}
and hence, isomorphic to
\mathcal{L}_{X}
. Thus, we obtain an extension of
\mathbf{Q}[\langle\rho\rangle]
-modules

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H_{2n-2}(\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{\infty})_{\ell}^{+};\mathbf{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H_{2n-2}((\smash{\text{{B}}\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{\infty}})^{+};\mathbf{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H_{2n-2}(\pi;\mathbf{Q})}

which, by Maschke’s theorem, splits since
\langle\rho\rangle
is a finite group. Moreover, this splitting can be chosen of the form
\H_{2n-2}(\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{\infty})_{\ell}^{+};\mathbf{Q})\cong\H_{2n-2}((\smash{\text{{B}}\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{\infty}})^{+};\mathbf{Q})\oplus\mathcal{L}_{X}
, where the map to the first factor is
\H_{2n-2}(\pi;\mathbf{Q})
, as required in the assumption of
Lemma

4.6.3
. This finishes this proof.
∎

5
Self-embedding spaces of
(Y_{g};X_{g})

In this section, we study the rational homotopy type of
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell}
in degrees
*\leq 2n-2
(cf.
5.3.2
), and hence that of the plus construction
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(Y_{\infty};X_{\infty})^{+}_{\ell}
in
5.4.3
. We end with
5.5.1
, which describes some features of
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(Y_{\infty};X_{\infty})^{+}_{\ell}\to\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{\infty})^{+}_{\ell}
on
\smash{\pi_{2n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}}
.

The approach we take to understand the rational homotopy groups of
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell}
is completely analogous to that in the preceding section for
\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(X_{g})_{\ell}
. Namely, we consider the fibre sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

(5.0.1)

understand the left and right terms individually, and later worry about the connecting maps. For the left-hand term, we again appeal to the pseudoisotopy theory reviewed in
Section

4.1
. The splitting result
5.1.1
reduces the analysis of its rational homotopy groups as representations of
\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
, where
\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}\coloneq\pi_{1}(\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell})
and
\rho
is the reflection involution of
Section

2.4
, to the work already carried out in
Section

4.3
. This yields
5.1.5
. In
Section

5.3
we analyse the connecting maps in the long exact sequence of (
5.0.1
), which requires understanding the Frobenius lifting maps on both base and fibre—the latter is treated in
Section

5.2
.

5.1
Pseudoisotopy self-embeddings of
(Y_{g};X_{g})

We dedicate this section to the computation of the rational homotopy groups of the pseudoisotopy self-embedding space
\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})
in degrees approximately
*\leq 2n-2
, including their description as
\mathbf{Q}[\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle]
-modules. Note that, as in the case of
X_{g}
, there is a map of
\langle\rho\rangle
-equivariant fibre sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{{\cong}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\cong}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g}),}

and hence the action of
\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
on the homotopy groups of the fibre factors through the group homomorphism
\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle\to\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
, where
\check{\Gamma}_{g}\coloneq\pi_{1}(\smash{\BEmb_{\partial_{0}}^{{\cong}}}(Y_{g};X_{g}))
, so we restrict ourselves to describing the action of the latter semidirect product.

Fix a self-embedding of
4
-ads
\iota\in\smash{\Emb_{\partial_{0}}^{{\cong}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})
extending
\iota:X_{g}\hookrightarrow X_{g}
as fixed in
Section

4.1.6
, and write
\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})
for the homotopy fibre of the map
\smash{\Emb_{\partial_{0}}^{{\cong}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})\to\smash{\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\cong}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})
at
\iota
. It readily follows that there is an equivalence

\Omega\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})\simeq\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g}).

Given
([\phi],\rho^{i})\in\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
, represent
[\phi]
by a diffeomorphism
\phi
of
Y_{g}
fixing
\partial^{v}Y_{g}=\partial_{0}Y_{g}\cup\partial_{1}Y_{g}
, and let
\psi\coloneq\phi\rho^{i}
. Conjugation by
\psi
gives rise to an action of
\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
on the homotopy groups of
\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})
similar to (and compatible with) the one in
4.3.1
. Moreover, by an argument analogous to the one in
Lemma

4.3.3
, it follows that this action recovers the one on the homotopy groups of
\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})
under the above equivalence. Moreover, this latter space is connected, for it fits in a fibre sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{+}}(Y_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{2}}

where both base and fibre are connected by Hudson’s theorem. Consequently, we only need to describe the
\mathbf{Q}[\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle]
-modules
\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g}))
(under the conjugation action just described).

To this end, looping the above fibre sequence yields another fibre sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{+}}(Y_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{2}}

(5.1.1)

where the homotopy groups of the base and fibre admit natural actions of
\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
as follows: on the base, it is given by restricting the action of
\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
from
4.3.1
along the homomorphism
\partial_{2}:\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle\to\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
. On the fibre, it arises from the observation that conjugation by
\psi=\phi\rho^{i}
still makes sense on the spaces
\Emb_{\partial_{+}}(Y_{g})
and
\widetilde{\Emb}_{\partial_{+}}(Y_{g})
, and hence on the homotopy fibre space
\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{+}}(Y_{g})
. Both of these actions make the maps
j
and
\partial_{2}
in (
5.1.1
)
\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-equivariant on homotopy groups.

Proposition 5.1.1
.

In degrees
*\leq 3n-6
, there is a map

\sigma^{(\sim)}:\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\right)\longrightarrow\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})\right)

which is a
\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-equivariant section of
\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\partial_{2})
. In particular, there is an isomorphism of
\mathbf{Q}[\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle]
-modules

\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(j)+\sigma^{(\sim)}:\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{+}}(Y_{g})\right)\oplus\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\right)\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})\right).

Remark 5.1.2
.

This proposition also holds
\mathbf{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2}]
-locally—replace
\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(-)
by
\pi_{*}(-)\left[\tfrac{1}{2}\right]
in the proof below. In fact, an even more general statement is true: inverting
2
, there is an equivalence of truncated spaces

\tau_{\leq 3n-6}\big(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})\big)\simeq_{[\frac{1}{2}]}\tau_{\leq 3n-6}\big(\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{+}}(Y_{g})\times\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\big).

This is a consequence of an analogue of the embedding Weiss–Williams theorem (
4.1.3
) for relative embedding spaces, and the following general principle in orthogonal calculus: given an orthogonal functor
F:\mathcal{J}\to\mathrm{Spc}_{*}
, the first orthogonal derivative of the functor
F^{(1)}(-)\coloneq\hofib(F(-)\to F(-\oplus\mathbf{R}))
is equivalent to
\smash{\operatorname{Ind}_{e}^{\mathrm{O}(1)}\operatorname{Res}^{\mathrm{O}(1)}_{e}\Theta F^{(1)}\simeq_{[\frac{1}{2}]}\Theta F^{(1)}\oplus\Sigma^{\sigma-1}\Theta F^{(1)}}
. We will not need this, so we omit the details.

Before proceeding with the proof, let us introduce the
relative concordance embedding space

\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(Y_{g};X_{g})
, consisting of embeddings
\varphi:(Y_{g};X_{g})\times[0,1]\hookrightarrow(Y_{g};X_{g})\times[0,1]
such that

(i)

\varphi^{-1}(Y_{g}\times\{i\})=Y_{g}\times\{i\}
for
i=0,1
,

(ii)

\varphi
agrees with the identity on a neighbourhood of
Y_{g}\times\{0\}\cup\partial_{0}Y_{g}\times[0,1]
, and

(iii)

\varphi(X_{g}\times[0,1])\subset X_{g}\times[0,1]
.

Restricting such an embedding to
X_{g}\times[0,1]
gives a map
\partial_{2}:\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(Y_{g};X_{g})\to\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})
. Moreover, there is another map
\alpha:\Omega\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(Y_{g};X_{g})\to\Omega\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})
defined as the map induced on horizontal homotopy fibres by the right commutative subsquare in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(Y_{g};X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\Emb_{\partial_{0}}(Y_{g};X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Emb_{\partial_{0}}(Y_{g}\times I;X_{g}\times I)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bEmbo(Y_{g}\times I;X_{g}\times I)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\Embo(Y_{g};X_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\widetilde{\Emb}_{\partial_{0}}(Y_{g};X_{g}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{res}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Gamma}}

\scriptstyle\sim

There is a conjugation
\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-action on the homotopy groups of
\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(Y_{g};X_{g})
, and just like in
Lemma

4.3.7
, one easily verifies that
\alpha
is
\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-equivariant for these actions.

Proof of
5.1.1
.

In this proof, every
\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-representation is regarded as a
\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-representation by restriction along
\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle\to\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
. We first describe a space-level section of
\partial_{2}:\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(Y_{g};X_{g})\to\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})
which is
\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-equivariant on homotopy groups for the conjugation action just described on the domain, and for the restricted conjugation
\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-action on the codomain described in
4.3.4
. For this, observe that the
4
-ad
(Y_{g},\partial_{0}Y_{g},\partial_{1}Y_{g},\partial_{2}Y_{g})=(Y_{g},S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}_{+},S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}_{-},X_{g})
is diffeomorphic to a
4
-ad on
\overline{Y}_{g}\coloneq Y_{g}\cup_{X_{g}\times\{0\}}X_{g}\times[0,1]
given by

\partial_{0}\overline{Y}_{g}\coloneq S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}_{+}\cup\partial_{0}X_{g}\times[0,1],\quad\partial_{1}\overline{Y}_{g}\coloneq S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}_{-}\cup\partial_{1}X_{g}\times[0,1],\quad\partial_{2}\overline{Y}_{g}\coloneq X_{g}\times\{1\}.

(One has to smooth corners of
\overline{Y}_{g}
appropriately, but let us ignore this issue.) Then, the section

\sigma:\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})\longrightarrow\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(\overline{Y}_{g};X_{g})\simeq\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(Y_{g};X_{g})

can be described as follows: given a concordance embedding
\varphi\in\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})
, i.e. a self-embedding
\varphi:X_{g}\times I\hookrightarrow X\times I
such that
\varphi
is the identity on
X_{g}\times\{0\}\cup\partial_{0}X_{g}\times I
, left-stabilisation gives a self-embedding
\Sigma_{\ell}(\varphi):X_{g}\times[0,1]\times I\hookrightarrow X_{g}\times[0,1]\times I
such that

•

\Sigma_{\ell}(\varphi)
is pointwise fixed on
(X_{g}\times[0,1]\times\{0\})\cup(X_{g}\times\{0\}\times I)\cup(\partial_{0}X_{g}\times[0,1]\times I)
, and

•

\Sigma_{\ell}(\varphi)\mid_{X_{g}\times\{1\}\times I}=\varphi
.

Thus, extending
\Sigma_{\ell}(\varphi)
by the identity on
Y_{g}\subset\overline{Y_{g}}
gives a point
\sigma(\varphi)\in\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(\overline{Y}_{g};X_{g})
. Moreover, by construction, we get that
r\circ\sigma(\varphi)\coloneq\sigma(\varphi)\mid_{X_{g}\times\{1\}\times I}=\varphi
, i.e.,
\sigma
is a right section of
\rho
as desired.

To see that
\sigma_{*}
is
\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-equivariant, observe that given
([\phi],\rho^{i})\in\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
, we can represent
[\phi]
by some diffeomorphism
\phi
on
\overline{Y_{g}}
which, when restricted to
X_{g}\times[0,1]\subset\overline{Y}_{g}
, is given by a product diffeomorphism
\xi\times\Id_{[0,1]}
for some diffeomorphism
\xi\in\Diff_{\partial}(X_{g})
. Moreover, the involution
\rho
of
Y_{g}
extends to
\overline{Y}_{g}
by setting it to be
\rho\mid_{X_{g}}\times\Id_{[0,1]}
on
X_{g}\times[0,1]\subset\overline{Y}_{g}
. Then, since
\sigma(\varphi)
is only supported on
X_{g}\times[0,1]\times I
, region on which
\phi\rho^{i}\times\Id_{I}
is given by
\xi\rho^{i}\mid_{X_{g}}\times\Id_{[0,1]\times I}=\Sigma_{\ell}(\xi\rho^{i}\mid_{X_{g}}\times\Id_{I})
, it follows that conjugation with
\phi\rho^{i}\times\Id_{I}
commutes with
\sigma
, establishing
\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-equivariance.

Let us write
\lambda
for the
\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-equivariant isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda:\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathcal{CE}_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathbf{CE}_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})),\quad*\leq 3n-6.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{stab.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
(
4.1.14
)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Since both
\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})
and
\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})
are connected, we only need to describe the splitting map
\sigma^{(\sim)}
of the statement in degrees
1\leq*\leq 3n-6
. In such degrees, it is given by the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathbf{CE}_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})_{hC_{2}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathbf{CE}_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(Y_{g};X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}(\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\Emb}_{X_{g}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{(\sim)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

(5.1.2)

where
N:A_{hC_{2}}\to A^{hC_{2}}\to X
stands for the norm map of a
C_{2}
-spectrum
A
. All of the homomorphisms in this composition are
\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-equivariant:
\smash{\Phi^{\Emb}_{X_{g}}}
by
4.3.5
, the norm map
N
because the
\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-action on
\smash{\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathbf{CE}_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}))}
commutes with the
h
-cobordism involution
\iota_{\H}
(as explained in
4.3.4
), the isomorphism
\lambda
by definition,
\sigma
by the argument above, and
\alpha
by construction. To conclude,
\sigma^{(\sim)}
is indeed a section of
\partial_{2}:\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g}))\to\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}))
since

\partial_{2}\circ\alpha\circ\sigma\circ\lambda^{-1}\circ N\circ\Phi^{\Emb}_{X_{g}}=\alpha\circ\lambda^{-1}\circ N\circ\Phi_{X_{g}}^{\Emb}=\Id,

where, in the first equality we are using that
\partial_{2}
and
\alpha
commute by construction, and in the second equality we invoke the commutativity of the square in
4.3.9
and the fact that,
\mathbf{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2}]
-locally, the norm map
N:A_{hC_{2}}\to A
is a section of the quotient map
q:A\to A_{hC_{2}}
.
∎

Therefore, for
*\leq 3n-5
, we only need to understand the
\mathbf{Q}[\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle]
-modules

\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{+}}(Y_{g})\big)\cong\left[\frac{\H^{S^{1}}_{*}(LY_{g},LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})}{\H_{*+1}(Y_{g},S^{1};\mathbf{Q})}\right]_{\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}},\quad\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\big)\cong\left[\frac{\H^{S^{1}}_{*}(LX_{g},LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})}{\H_{*+1}(X_{g},S^{1};\mathbf{Q})}\right]_{-\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}},

where both of these isomorphisms follow from
4.1.5
(notice the absence of a minus sign in front of
\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}
on the right). The
\mathbf{Q}[\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle]
-module on the right-hand side of the right isomorphism was identified in
4.3.13
to be induced by naturality on the pair
(X_{g},S^{1})
. Exactly the same analysis applies to the isomorphism on the left:

Proposition 5.1.3
.

The action of
\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
on the homotopy groups of the space
\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{+}}(Y_{g})
factors through the homomorphism
\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle\to\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
. Under the isomorphism

\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{+}}(Y_{g})\big)\cong\left[\frac{\H^{S^{1}}_{*}(LY_{g},LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})}{\H_{*+1}(Y_{g},S^{1};\mathbf{Q})}\right]_{\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}},\quad*\leq 3n-3,

(5.1.3)

the action of
([\phi],\rho^{i})\in\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
(where
[\phi]
is represented by a diffeomorphism
\phi
of
Y_{g}
fixed on
\partial^{v}Y_{g}
) on the left-hand side corresponds, on the right-hand side, to the map

(\phi\rho^{i})_{*}:\left[\frac{\H^{S^{1}}_{*}(LY_{g},LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})}{\H_{*+1}(Y_{g},S^{1};\mathbf{Q})}\right]_{\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\left[\frac{\H^{S^{1}}_{*}(LY_{g},LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})}{\H_{*+1}(Y_{g},S^{1};\mathbf{Q})}\right]_{\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}}

induced by functoriality on
(Y_{g},S^{1})
.

It remains to compute the groups on the right-hand side of (
5.1.3
) as representations of the homotopy mapping class group
\mathrm{h}\Gamma_{g}\coloneq\pi_{0}(\Aut_{*}(Y_{g}))\cong\pi_{0}(\Aut_{*}(Y_{g},S^{1}))
. To this end, consider the
\mathbf{Q}[\mathrm{h}\Gamma_{g}]
-module

H_{Y_{g}}\coloneq\smash{H_{Y_{g}}^{\mathbf{Z}}}[-1]\otimes\mathbf{Q}\cong H_{n}(\widetilde{Y}_{g};\mathbf{Q})[-1],

which lives in degree
n-1
, defined analogously to
H_{X_{g}}
. As for
\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}
, the fundamental group
\pi_{1}Y_{g}=\pi\cong\mathbf{Z}\langle t\rangle
is a normal subgroup of
\mathrm{h}\Gamma_{g}
, and the restricted
\mathbf{Q}[\pi]
-module structure on
H_{Y_{g}}
is induced by the usual action of
\pi_{1}Y_{g}
on its higher homotopy groups. As
\mathbf{Q}[\pi]
-modules,

H_{Y_{g}}\cong\mathbf{Q}[\pi]\otimes H_{V_{g}},\quad\text{where $H_{V_{g}}\coloneq H_{n}(V_{g,1};\mathbf{Q})[-1]$.}

Finally, we will also regard
H_{V_{g}}
as a
\mathbf{Q}[\mathrm{h}\Gamma_{g}]
-module via the obvious isomorphism
H_{V_{g}}\cong[H_{Y_{g}}]_{\pi}
. The following is a complete analogue of
4.2.3
(iii), and its proof carries over verbatim.

Proposition 5.1.4
.

For
r\in\mathbf{Z}
, consider the graded
\mathbf{Q}[\mathrm{h}\Gamma_{g}]
-modules

A_{r}^{Y_{g}}\coloneq\frac{H_{Y_{g}}}{\langle 1-t^{r}\rangle},\qquad B_{r}^{Y_{g}}\coloneq\frac{S^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle(n-1)}(H_{Y_{g}})}{S^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle(n-1)}(1-t^{r})}.

Then, in degrees
*\leq 2n-2
, there is an isomorphism of
\mathbf{Q}[\mathrm{h}\Gamma_{g}]
-modules

\left[\frac{\H^{S^{1}}_{*}(LY_{g},LS^{1};\mathbf{Q})}{\H_{*+1}(Y_{g},S^{1};\mathbf{Q})}\right]_{\tau_{\mathrm{FLS}}}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\bigoplus_{r>0}{[A_{r}^{Y_{g}}]_{\pi}}\cong\bigoplus_{r>0}H_{V_{g}}&*=n-1,\\[6.0pt]
C^{Y_{g}}\oplus\bigoplus_{r>0}\left({\left[S^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle(n-1)}(A_{r}^{Y_{g}})\right]}_{\pi}\oplus[B_{r}^{Y_{g}}]_{\pi}\right)&*=2n-2,\\[6.0pt]
0&\text{otherwise},\end{array}\right.

where
C^{Y_{g}}
is some quotient of
[H^{\otimes 2}_{Y_{g}}]_{\pi}
.

All in all, we can describe the rational homotopy groups of
\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})
as a
\mathbf{Q}[\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle]
-module as follows. Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\Gamma}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{h}\Gamma_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\check{\Xi}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\Xi}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}

(5.1.4)

where
\nu([\phi],\rho^{i})=[\phi\rho^{i}]
in both cases.

Corollary 5.1.5
.

Let
H_{X_{g}}
,
A_{r}
,
B_{r}
and
C
be the
\mathrm{h}\Xi_{g}
-representations of
4.2.2
and
4.2.3
, and let
\smash{H_{Y_{g}}}
,
\smash{A_{r}^{Y_{g}}}
,
\smash{B_{r}^{Y_{g}}}
and
\smash{C^{Y_{g}}}
be the
\mathrm{h}\Gamma_{g}
-representations of
5.1.4
. Regard all of these representations as
\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle
-representations by pulling them back along (
5.1.4
). Then, for
*\leq 2n-2
, there is isomorphism of
\mathbf{Q}[\check{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{sfr},\ell}_{g}\rtimes\langle\rho\rangle]
-modules

\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})\big)\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\bigoplus_{r>0}\big([A_{r}]_{\pi}\oplus[A_{r}^{Y_{g}}]_{\pi}\big)\cong\bigoplus_{r>0}(H_{g}\oplus H_{V_{g}})&*=n-1,\\[4.0pt]
\parbox{207.0021pt}{$C\oplus C^{Y_{g}}\oplus\bigoplus_{r>0}\big([S^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle(n-1)}(A_{r})]_{\pi}\oplus[S^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle(n-1)}(A_{r}^{Y_{g}})]_{\pi}\oplus[B_{r}]_{\pi}\oplus[B_{r}^{Y_{g}}]_{\pi}\big)$}&*=2n-2,\\[4.0pt]
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Moreover, under this isomorphism and the one in
4.3.14
, the map
\partial_{2}:\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})\big)\to\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})\big)
is given by the evident projection onto the summands involving
H_{g}
,
A_{r}
,
B_{r}
and
C
.

5.2
The
\mathbf{Z}[\mathbf{N}^{\times}]
-module structures

As in the case of
X_{g}
, it will be useful to understand the actions of the Frobenii on the homotopy groups
\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})
and
\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})
in order to understand some of the connecting maps in the long exact sequence of homotopy groups of the fibration (
5.0.1
).

5.2.1
The
\mathbf{Z}[\mathbf{N}^{\times}]
-module structure on pseudoisotopy of
(Y_{g};X_{g})

The fibre sequence
\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{+}}(Y_{g})\xrightarrow{j}\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})\xrightarrow{\partial_{2}}\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g})}
of (
5.1.1
) is clearly compatible with the Frobenius maps
\varphi_{d}
. Moreover, we saw in
5.1.1
that this fibration splits on rational homotopy groups in the range
*\leq 3n-6
. The splitting
\sigma^{(\sim)}
is also compatible with the Frobenii:

Lemma 5.2.1
.

If
*\leq 3n-6
, the following square commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{dg}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{dg};X_{dg})).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{(\sim)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{(\sim)}}

Thus, for
*\leq 3n-6
and
m\geq 0
, the following isomorphisms are compatible with the Frobenius maps:

\displaystyle\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}((Y_{g};X_{g}))

\displaystyle\overset{\ref{splittingprop}}{\cong}\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(X_{g}))\oplus\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{+}}(Y_{g}))

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{iotaCeq}}{\cong}\left[\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}\times I^{2m}))\right]_{\iota_{\H}}\oplus\left[\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\CEmb_{\partial_{+}}(Y_{g}\times I^{2m}))\right]_{\iota_{\H}}.

Proof.

First observe that
\sigma:\pi_{*}\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g})\to\pi_{*}\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(Y_{g};X_{g})
as in the proof of
5.1.1
is Frobenius-preserving: indeed, it is clear
\Sigma_{\ell}\circ\varphi_{d}=\varphi_{d}\circ\Sigma_{\ell}
, where
\Sigma_{\ell}
is the left-stabilisation map involved in the construction of
\sigma
. As
\sigma
is obtained from
\Sigma_{\ell}
by extending by the identity on
Y_{g}\subset\overline{Y}_{g}
, the claim follows.

But now recall the definition of
\sigma^{(\sim)}
, given by the composition in (
5.1.2
). All the maps involved are compatible with the Frobenius: the norm map
N
since the Frobenius commutes with the
h
-cobordism involution, the maps
i
and
\alpha
by construction,
\sigma
by the argument above. Finally, the Weiss–Williams map
\Phi^{\Emb}_{X_{g}}
is also compatible with the Frobenius since the remaining maps in the commutative square of
4.3.9
are compatible with the Frobenius, and because the horizontal maps in that square are surjective on rational homotopy groups below the stable range
*\leq 3n-6
.
∎

For our purposes, we will only need to understand the action of the Frobenius
\varphi_{d}
on
\smash{\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\Emb^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g}))}
when
*=n-2
, and hence, by the previous result, that on
\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\CEmb_{\partial_{0}}(X_{g}\times I^{2m}))
and
\pi_{n-2}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\CEmb_{\partial_{+}}(Y_{g}\times I^{2m}))
for any
m\geq 0
. For sufficiently large
m
, the effect of
\varphi_{d}
on the former group was carefully analysed in
Section

4.4.1
(cf.
4.4.12
and
Lemma

4.4.13
), and the exact same analysis applies to the latter groups. The upshot is:

Proposition 5.2.2
.

For each
d\in\mathbf{N}
, the Frobenius map

\varphi_{d}:\pi_{n-1}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})\big)\longrightarrow\pi_{n-1}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{dg};X_{dg})\big)

is isomorphic to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{r>0}H_{g}^{(r)}\oplus H_{V_{g}}^{(r)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{s>0}H_{dg}^{(s)}\oplus H_{V_{dg}}^{(s)},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{r>0}\alpha_{r}\oplus\beta_{r}}

where the subscripts
(-)^{(r)}
and
(-)^{(s)}
only serve bookkeeping purposes, and
\alpha_{r}
and
\beta_{r}
:

(i)

are zero if
d
does not divide
r
;

(ii)

are injective if
r=di
, and factor as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{r}:H_{g}^{(di)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{j=0}^{d-1}H_{g}\cong H_{dg}^{(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{s>0}H_{dg}^{(s)},}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{r}:H_{V_{g}}^{(di)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{j=0}^{d-1}H_{V_{g}}\cong H_{V_{dg}}^{(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{s>0}H_{V_{dg}}^{(s)}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

Moreover, the embedding
\iota_{0}:X_{g}\xhookrightarrow{}X_{dg}
and the
C_{d}
-action on
X_{dg}
of
Lemma

4.4.13
extend to an embedding
\iota_{0}:(Y_{g};X_{g})\xhookrightarrow{}(Y_{dg},X_{dg})
and a
C_{d}
-action on
(Y_{dg},X_{dg})
. Under the above isomorphisms,

(a)

the map
(\iota_{0})_{*}:\pi_{n-1}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})\big)\to\pi_{n-1}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{dg};X_{dg})\big)
becomes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{r>0}H_{g}\oplus H_{V_{g}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{r>0}\bigoplus_{j=0}^{d-1}H_{g}\oplus H_{V_{g}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{r>0}H_{dg}\oplus H_{V_{dg}};}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j=0}

(b)

the
C_{d}
-action on
\pi_{n-1}^{\mathbf{Q}}\big(\smash{\big(\widetilde{\Emb}/\mathrm{Emb}\big)_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{dg};X_{dg})\big)
is the diagonal
C_{d}
-action on
\bigoplus_{r>0}H_{dg}\oplus H_{V_{dg}}
, where
C_{d}
acts on
H_{dg}\oplus H_{V_{dg}}\cong\bigoplus_{j=0}^{d-1}H_{g}\oplus H_{V_{g}}
by permuting the summands.

5.2.2
The
\mathbf{Z}[\mathbf{N}^{\times}]
-module structure on block embeddings of
(Y_{g};X_{g})

Recall from
2.1.1
that there is a rational equivalence

\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell}\xrightarrow{\simeq_{\mathbf{Q}}}\BAut_{\partial^{v}}^{\cong}(Y_{g};X_{g}),

which is compatible with the Frobenius maps. Thus, it suffices to study the effect of these on the homotopy groups of the codomain; this will be particularly easy given what we did in
Section

4.4.2
.

Proposition 5.2.3
.

The
d
-th Frobenius on
\pi_{n}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell})
is injective and given by

\varphi_{d}(y)=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}t^{i}\cdot(\iota_{0})_{*}(y),

where
(\iota_{0})_{*}
is the "extension-by-the-identity" map induced by the embedding
\iota_{0}:(Y_{g};X_{g})\hookrightarrow(Y_{dg};X_{dg})
, and the group
C_{d}=\langle t\rangle/\langle t^{d}\rangle
acts on
\smash{\mathrm{B}\widetilde{\Emb}{\vphantom{\Emb}}^{\mathrm{sfr},{\cong}}_{\partial_{0}}}(Y_{g};X_{g})_{\ell}
as conjugation by the rotation action on
(Y_{g};X_{g})
.

Proof.

By
2.3.2
, the map
\BAut_{\partial^{v}}^{\cong}(Y_{g};X_{g})\longrightarrow\BAutbp(X_{g})
is injective on rational homotopy groups. Since the
d
-th Frobenius on the target of this map is injective, we conclude the same for the source. By the discussion above, the claim for
