\Emb_{\partial D^{d-2}\times\frac{1}{2}D^{2}}(D^{d-2}\times\smash{\tfrac{1}{2}}D^{2},D^{d})_{\iota}\to\BDiffb(S^{1}\times D^{d-1})\to\BDiffb(D^{d}),

(6.8.1)

where the subscript
(-)_{\iota}
denotes the path component of the inclusion
\iota:D^{d-2}\times\tfrac{1}{2}D^{2}\hookrightarrow D^{d}
. This follows from the fact that
\pi_{0}(\Diffb(S^{1}\times D^{d-1}))\to\pi_{0}(\Diffb(D^{d}))
is surjective, as it admits a splitting induced by the inclusion of
D^{d}
into
S^{1}\times D^{d-1}.

2

Observe that
\iota|_{D^{d-2}\times\{0\}}
is the unknot, so restricting to this subspace defines a map

r:\Emb_{\partial D^{d-2}\times\frac{1}{2}D^{2}}(D^{d-2}\times\smash{\tfrac{1}{2}}D^{2},D^{d})_{\iota}\to\Emb_{\partial}(D^{d-2},D^{d})_{u}.

The homotopy fibre of
r
is equivalent to space of framings of the normal bundle of
D^{d-2}
in
D^{d}
extending the fixed framing on
\partial D^{d-2}
, which is equivalent to the space
\Map_{\partial}(D^{d-2},\mathrm{O}(2))\simeq\Omega^{d-2}\mathrm{O}(2)
. This space is contractible since
\mathrm{O}(2)\simeq S^{1}\sqcup S^{1}
is
1
-truncated and
d\geq 6
. Thus,
r
is a weak equivalence.

Proof of
G
.

We separate our proof by cases. Let
d=2n
be even and consider the fibre sequence (
6.8.1
). By
A
for
d=2n
, the rational homotopy groups of
\BDiffb(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1})
vanish in degrees
*<2n-3
and are infinitely generated in degree
*=2n-3
. By
[
60
, Thm. A]
, the rational homotopy groups of
\BDiffb(D^{2n})
vanish in degrees
*<2n-1
. We conclude, by the long exact sequence of (
6.8.1
), that the rational homotopy groups of the fibre of (
6.8.1
), and hence of
\Emb_{\partial}(D^{d-2},D^{2})_{u}
by

2

, vanish in degrees
*<2n-3
and are infinitely generated in degree
*=2n-3
.

We move now to the case
d=2n+1
odd. Consider the following commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BDiffb(S^{1}\times D^{2n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BDiffb(D^{2n+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BC(S^{1}\times D^{2n-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BC(D^{2n}).}

The left vertical map induces isomorphisms on rational homotopy groups in degrees
*\leq 2n-4
, by the proof of
A
for
d=2n+1
. By the vanishing results of the rational homotopy of
\BDiffb(D^{2n})
, we conclude that the right vertical map induces isomorphisms on rational homotopy groups in degrees
*\leq 2n-3
. The top map is surjective on homotopy groups in every degree
*\geq 2
by
[
19
, Thm.2, p.104]
(as in the proof of
6.4.1
but for diffeomorphisms instead of concordances). Thus for
k\geq 1
, the
k
-th rational homotopy groups of
\Emb_{\partial}(D^{2n-1},D^{2n+1})
is the kernel of the map induced by the top map on rational homotopy groups of degree
k
. For
k=2n-3
, the latter map has infinitely generated source and finitely generated target, by
[
55
, Thm. A]
, thus its kernel is infinitely generated. For
k\leq 2n-4
, this kernel is isomorphic to the kernel of the bottom map on rational homotopy groups, which we now calculate. By
6.4.1
, we know that the bottom map is surjective on rational homotopy groups in degrees
*\geq 2
. Since
\pi^{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}(\BC(D^{2n}))\cong\K^{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}(\mathbf{Z})=0
, then this map is also surjective on rational homotopy groups in degree
*=1.
Using the Bass–Heller–Swan splitting
\K^{\mathbf{Q}}_{i}(\mathbf{Z}[\mathbf{Z}])\cong\K_{i}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathbf{Z})\oplus\K_{i-1}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathbf{Z})
and the surjectivity of the bottom map on rational homotopy groups, we conclude that the kernel of the latter in degree
k
is abstractly isomorphic to
\K^{\mathbf{Q}}_{k}(\mathbf{Z}).
This finishes the proof.
∎

6.8.2
Orthogonal calculus of topological Stiefel manifold

In this section, we will prove
H
, which claims that
\Theta\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(2)}
is rationally
(-1)
-connective. But before we do, let us explain how the study of
\mathsf{Vt}_{2}
is related to that of spaces of codimension
2
long knots. Note that the space
\Emb_{\partial}(D^{d-2},D^{d})
is an
\mathbf{E}_{d-2}
-algebra under stacking in
(d-2)
-many interval directions in
D^{d-2}\cong I^{d-2}
, and hence
\pi_{0}(\Emb_{\partial}(D^{d-2},D^{d}))
is an abelian monoid if
d\geq 4
. Write
\Emb_{\partial}(D^{d-2},D^{d})^{\times}
for the set of invertible path-components, so that
\pi_{0}(\Emb_{\partial}(D^{d-2},D^{d})^{\times})
is now an abelian group. Consider the orthogonal functor
\mathsf{Vo}_{2}(V)\coloneq\mathrm{O}(V\oplus\mathbf{R}^{2})/\mathrm{O}(2)
of codimension
2
smooth Stiefel manifolds. By smoothing theory, and being extra careful with path-components, there is a homotopy cartesian square of
\mathbf{E}_{d-2}
-algebras

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Emb_{\partial}(D^{d-2},D^{d})^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Emb^{t}_{\partial}(D^{d-2},D^{d})_{u}\simeq*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{d-2}\mathsf{Vo}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d-2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{d-2}\mathsf{Vt}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d-2}),}

where
\Emb^{t}
denotes the space of locally flat topological embeddings (cf.
[
70
]
). The spaces on the bottom row can be identified as the spaces of smooth and topological immersions
D^{d-2}\looparrowright D^{d}
(rel boundary), and the vertical arrows as the smooth and topological derivatives, respectively. As explained in the proof of
[
14
, Prop. 5.11]
, for
d\geq 6
, a long knot
D^{d-2}\hookrightarrow D^{d}
is invertible if and only if it is a reparametrisation of the unknot—that is, the natural map
\Diff_{\partial}(D^{d-2})\to\Emb_{\partial}(D^{d-2},D^{d})^{\times}
is an isomorphism on
\pi_{0}
, and hence
\pi_{0}(\Emb_{\partial}(D^{d-2},D^{d})^{\times})\cong\Theta_{d-1}
for
d\geq 6
. In particular, the map
\Emb_{\partial}(D^{d-2},D^{d})^{\times}\to\Emb^{t}_{\partial}(D^{d-2},D^{d})
only hits the component of the unknot, which is contractible by the Alexander trick.
G
gives:

Corollary 6.8.1
.

For
d\geq 6
, the
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space
\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathsf{Vt}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d-2}))
is infinite-dimensional in degree
*=2d-4
if
d
is even, and in degree
*=2d-5
if
d
is odd.

We move on to higher derivatives of
\mathsf{Vt}_{2}
, which concern the spaces
\CEmb(D^{d-2},D^{d})
of codimension
2
concordance long knots. Note that for
d\geq 4
,
\pi_{0}(\CEmb(D^{d-2},D^{d}))
is again an abelian monoid under stacking. We will need the following analogue of Budney’s result, suggested to us by Oscar Randal-Williams.

Lemma 6.8.2
.

For
d\geq 5
, a concordance embedding in
\CEmb(D^{d-2},D^{d})
is homotopy invertible under stacking if and only if it is smoothly isotopic to the identity. That is,
\CEmb(D^{d-2},D^{d})^{\times}=\CEmb(D^{d-2},D^{d})_{u}
.

Proof.

One direction is clear. For the other, let
\varphi\in\CEmb(\nu D^{d-2},D^{d})\simeq\CEmb(D^{d-2},D^{d})
be homotopy invertible, with inverse
\psi
. Writing
C_{\varphi}
for the complement of
\varphi
in
D^{d}\times I
, we claim that the natural map
\mathbf{Z}\cong\pi_{1}(S^{1}\times D^{d-1})\to\pi_{1}(C_{\varphi})
is an isomorphism; if so, a Mayer–Vietoris argument shows that
C_{\varphi}
is an
h
-cobordism starting at
S^{1}\times D^{d-1}
. Since
\mathrm{Wh}_{1}(\mathbf{Z})=0
, it would follow from the
s
-cobordism theorem that
C_{\varphi}
is diffeomorphic to the trivial cobordism on
S^{1}\times D^{d-1}
; a choice of diffeomorphism determines, by isotopy extension, an isotopy from
\varphi
to the unknot. To establish the claim, we follow the argument in
[
14
, Prop. 5.11]
. By Seifert–Van Kampen, there is a pushout square of groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(C_{\varphi})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(C_{\psi})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Z},}

and both compositions
\mathbf{Z}\to\mathbf{Z}
are isomorphisms, so the right vertical map is surjective. It is also injective by the normal form theorem for amalgamated free products, so an isomorphism. The claim follows.
∎

Write
\CEmb^{t}
for the space of (locally flat) topological concordance embeddings. By the previous argument, a concordance embedding
\varphi
is smoothly isotopic to the identity if and only it is so topologically. Thus, by smoothing theory
[
62
, Cor. 2(C)]
, there is a homotopy cartesian square of
\mathbf{E}_{d-2}
-algebras

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CEmb(D^{d-2},D^{d})_{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CEmb^{t}(D^{d-2},D^{d})_{u}\simeq*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{d-2}\mathsf{Vo}_{2}^{(1)}(\mathbf{R}^{d-2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{d-2}\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(1)}(\mathbf{R}^{d-2}),}

(6.8.2)

so long as
d\neq 4
. First note that
\mathsf{Vo}_{2}^{(1)}(\mathbf{R}^{d-2})\simeq\Omega S^{d}
. By isotopy extension and the fact that
\BC(D^{d})
has finitely generated homotopy groups, it follows that
\smash{\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\CEmb(D^{d-2},D^{d}))}
is infinitely generated precisely when
\pi_{*-1}(\BC(S^{1}\times D^{d-1}))
is so. These observations together with
B
and
1.1.5
(i) imply claims (iii)–(v) in the following result:

Lemma 6.8.3
.

The stable and unstable first derivatives of the orthogonal functor
\mathsf{Vt}_{2}
satisfy the following:

(i)

For each
V\in\mathcal{J}
, the unstable derivative
\mathsf{Vt}^{(1)}_{2}(V)
is
|V|
-connected. Thus,
\Theta\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(1)}
is
1
-connective.

(ii)

There is an equivalence
\Omega^{\infty+1}(\Theta\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(1)})\simeq\Omega^{\infty}(\mathrm{K}(\mathbf{S}))
.

(iii)

For
d\geq 6
and
d-1\leq*\leq 2d-5
, the
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space
\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(1)}(\mathbf{R}^{d-2}))
is finite-dimensional.

(iv)

For
d\geq 6
, the
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space
\pi_{2d-4}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(1)}(\mathbf{R}^{d-2}))
is infinite-dimensional.

(v)

For
d\geq 7
odd, the
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space
\pi_{2d-3}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(1)}(\mathbf{R}^{d-2}))
is infinite-dimensional.

Proof.

We deal with (i) first. Consider the orthogonal functor
\mathsf{Bt}_{2}(V)\coloneq\BTop(V\oplus\mathbf{R}^{2},V)
which, by definition, fits in a fibre sequence of orthogonal functors
\mathsf{Vt}_{2}\to\mathsf{Bt}_{2}\to\mathsf{Bt}(-\oplus\mathbf{R}^{2})
. On unstable derivatives, we obtain fibre sequences
\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(1)}(V)\to\mathsf{Bt}_{2}^{(1)}(V)\to\mathsf{Bt}^{(1)}(V\oplus\mathbf{R}^{2})
. Both
\mathsf{Bt}_{2}^{(1)}(V)
and
\mathsf{Bt}^{(1)}(V\oplus\mathbf{R}^{2})
are
(|V|+1)
-connected: the former since the map
\BTop(2)=\mathsf{Bt}_{2}(0)\to\mathsf{Bt}_{2}(V)
is
(|V|+1)
-connected by
[
51
, Thm. B]
(see also
[
54
, Lem. 8.17(ii)]
). The latter space since the map
\BTop(V)\to\BTop
is
|V|
-connected.

We move on to part (ii). The square (
6.8.2
) is compatible with stabilisation in the sense that there are maps of homotopy fibre sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CEmb(D^{d-2},D^{d})_{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{d-2}\mathsf{Vo}_{2}^{(1)}(\mathbf{R}^{d-2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{d-2}\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(1)}(\mathbf{R}^{d-2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CEmb(D^{d-1},D^{d+1})_{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{d-1}\mathsf{Vo}_{2}^{(1)}(\mathbf{R}^{d-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{d-1}\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(1)}(\mathbf{R}^{d-1}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{D^{d-2},D^{d}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{d-2}s^{o}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{d-2}s^{t}}

(6.8.3)

where
\Sigma_{D^{d-2},D^{d}}
is the concordance embedding stabilisation map of (
4.1.12
), and
s^{o}
and
s^{t}
are the structure maps of the first derivative spectra
\Theta\mathsf{Vo}^{(1)}
and
\Theta\mathsf{Vt}^{(1)}
. Looping the base, and taking the colimit with respect to these stabilisation maps, we obtain another fibre sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty+1}(\Theta\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{CE}(*,D^{2})_{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(\Theta\mathsf{Vo}_{2}^{(1)})\simeq\Omega^{\infty-1}\mathbf{S},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{o}}

where
D^{o}
is the smooth (stable) derivative map. This map is nullhomotopic (it is so unstably) by the argument in
[
38
, p. 22]
, and hence it follows that
\Omega^{\infty+1}(\Theta\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(1)})
is equivalent to the product
\mathcal{CE}(*,D^{2})_{u}\times\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{S}
. Now by
Equation

4.1.18
, we have that

\mathcal{CE}(*,D^{2})_{u}\simeq\hofib(\Omega\Wh(S^{1})\to\Omega\Wh(*))\simeq\tohofib\Bigg(\hbox to107.43pt{\vbox to45.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 53.71664pt\lower-22.95947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\Bigg).

Since the unit map
\nu
is split injective, the fibre of
Q_{+}S^{1}\to QS^{0}
is
\Omega^{\infty-1}\mathbf{S}
, and the fibre of
A(S^{1})\to A(*)
is equivalent to
\Omega^{\infty-1}(\mathrm{K}(\mathbf{S}))
by the Bass–Heller–Swan splitting, claim (ii) follows. This finishes the proof.
∎

Finally, we derive
H
from
E
for
(M,N)=(D^{d-2},D^{d})
.

Proof of
H
.

Taking vertical fibres in
Equation

6.8.3
gives, for each
d\neq 4
, a fibre sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hofib(\Sigma_{D^{d-2},D^{d}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{d-2}\mathsf{Vo}_{2}^{(2)}(\mathbf{R}^{d-2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{d-2}\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(2)}(\mathbf{R}^{d-2}).}

Since the map
s^{o}
coincides with the loop-suspension map
\mathrm{O}(d+1)/\mathrm{O}(d)\simeq S^{d}\longrightarrow\Omega S^{d+1}\simeq\Omega\mathrm{O}(d+2)/\mathrm{O}(d+1)
looped once, it follows that
\mathsf{Vo}_{2}^{(2)}(\mathbf{R}^{d-2})
is
(2d-2)
-connected, and hence the middle term is
(d-2)
-connected. When
d\geq 6
, the left term is rationally
(d-5)
-connected (but not rationally
(d-4)
-connected) by
E
, and so
\Omega^{d-1}\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(2)}(\mathbf{R}^{d-2})
is also rationally
(d-5)
-connected but not rationally
(d-4)
-connected. (In fact, it follows from
Lemma

6.8.3
(i) and the map of homotopy long exact sequences induced by (
6.8.3
) that
\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(2)}(\mathbf{R}^{d-2})
is itself rationally
(2d-6)
-connected but not
(2d-5)
-connected.) Hence,

\pi_{*}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\Theta\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(2)})=\colim_{d}\pi_{*+2d-4}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(2)}(\mathbf{R}^{d-2}))=\colim_{d}\pi_{*+d-3}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\Omega^{d-1}\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(2)}(\mathbf{R}^{d-2}))

vanishes if
*+d-3\leq d-5
for
d\gg 0
, i.e. when
*\leq-2
, so
\Theta\mathsf{Vt}_{2}^{(2)}
is indeed rationally
(-1)
-connective.
∎

Appendix A
Weiss–Williams technicalities

A.1
The
h
-cobordism involution on concordance
embeddings

The purpose of this appendix is to give an explicit model of the
h
-cobordism involution
\iota_{\H}
on
\CEmb(P,M)
presented in
Item

(1)
of
Section

4.1.5
. For simplicity, we assume that
\iota:P\hookrightarrow M
is a codimension-zero embedding of compact manifolds with
d-p\leq 3
and
d\geq 5
15
15

15

A few words on the assumptions: the map
\CEmb(\nu P,M)\to\CEmb(P,M)
given by restriction to the zero-section
P\subset\nu P
is an equivalence, so no generality is lost by assuming the geometric codimension of
P
in
M
is zero. Regarding the handle codimension condition
d-p\leq 3
, this ensures
\CEmb(P,M)
is connected by Hudson’s theorem. Finally, we assume
d\geq 5
so that the path-components of the
h
-cobordism spaces
\H(M-\nu P)
and
\H(M)
appearing later are understood by the
s
-cobordism theorem; if
d\leq 4
, these should be replaced by their identity components, i.e. the classifying spaces
\BC(M-\nu P)
and
\BC(M)
.
. As a byproduct of the results in this section, we also model the fibre inclusion map
\kappa
in the fibre sequence (cf.
A.1.4
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CEmb(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H(M-\nu P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H(M),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}

and verify that the restriction map
\mathrm{res}_{P}:\operatorname{C}(M)\to\CEmb(P,M)
is
anti
-equivariant for the concordance involution
\iota_{\operatorname{C}}
on the domain and
\iota_{\H}
on the target (cf.
A.1.5
). To keep the notation simple, we will write
\nu P
for
P-\partial_{1}P
. Let us first introduce suitable models for
\CEmb(P,M)
,
\iota_{\H}
and
\kappa
.

Definition A.1.1
.

(i)  Consider the space
\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)
of tuples
(\varphi,\psi,\phi,\eta)
consisting of

•

a concordance embedding
\varphi:P\times[-1,0]\hookrightarrow M\times[-1,0]
from
\iota\times\{-1\}
to
\varphi\mid_{P\times\{0\}}
, a concordance embedding
\psi:P\times[0,1]\hookrightarrow M\times[0,1]
from
\varphi\mid_{P\times\{0\}}
to
\iota\times\{1\}
(both appropriately collared);

•

a diffeomorphism
\phi:(M-\nu P)\times[-1,1]\cong M\times[-1,1]-\big(\varphi(\nu P\times[-1,0])\cup\psi(\nu P\times[0,1])\big)
rel boundary;

•

an isotopy
\eta
in
\Diff_{\partial}(M\times[-1,1])
between the identity
\Id_{M\times[-1,1]}
and
\phi\cup(\varphi\cup_{P\times\{0\}}\psi)
.

(ii)  The
h
-cobordism involution
\iota_{\H}
on
\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)
sends
(\varphi,\psi,\phi,\eta)
to the tuple
(\overline{\psi},\overline{\varphi},\overline{\phi},\overline{\eta})
given as follows: write
r:[-1,1]\cong[-1,1]
for
r(t)=-t
, which restricts to a diffeomorphism
r:[-1,0]\cong[0,1]
. Then,

•

\overline{\psi}=(\Id_{M}\times\ r)\circ\psi\circ(\Id_{P}\times\ r)
and
\overline{\varphi}=(\Id_{M}\times\ r)\circ\varphi\circ(\Id_{P}\times\ r)
;

•

\overline{\phi}
is the composition of
\Id_{M-\nu P}\times\ r
,
\phi
and the diffeomorphism

M\times[-1,1]-\big(\varphi(\nu P\times[-1,0])\cup\psi(\nu P\times[0,1])\big)\cong M\times[-1,1]-\big(\overline{\psi}(\nu P\times[-1,0])\cup\overline{\varphi}(\nu P\times[0,1])\big)

induced by the restriction of
\Id_{M}\times\ r
to the domain;

•

\overline{\eta}(t)=(\Id_{M}\times\ r)\circ\eta(t)\circ(\Id_{M}\times\ r)
for
t\in[0,1]
.

(iii)  The map
\kappa:\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)\to\H(M-\nu P)
sends
(\varphi,\psi,\phi,\eta)
to the partition
(W,F,V)\in\H(M-\nu P)
with

W=\phi^{-1}\big(M\times[-1,0]-\varphi(\nu P\times[-1,0])\big),\quad F=\phi^{-1}\big(M\times\{0\}-\varphi(\nu P\times\{0\})\big),\quad V=\phi^{-1}\big(M\times[0,1]-\psi(\nu P\times[0,1])\big).

One verifies that
\kappa:\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)\to\H(M-\nu P)
is
\iota_{\H}
-equivariant (see
(1)
for the definition of
\iota_{\H}
on
\H(M-\nu P)
).

(iv)  There is a forgetful map
u:\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)\to\CEmb(P,M)
sending
(\varphi,\psi,\phi,\eta)
to
\varphi
, and a restriction map
\underline{\mathrm{res}}_{P}:\operatorname{C}(M)\to\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)
sending a concordance diffeomorphism
f:M\times I\cong M\times I
to the tuple
(\varphi,\psi,\phi,\eta)
given as follows:

•

Identifying
I
with
[-1,0]
,
\varphi
is
f\mid_{P\times[-1,0]}
.

•

Identifying
I
with
[0,1]
and writing
f_{1}
for the diffeomorphism
f\mid_{M\times\{1\}}
,
\psi
is
(f_{1}\times\Id_{[0,1]})\circ f^{-1}\mid_{P\times[0,1]}
.

•

Consider the diffeomorphism
\Phi\in\Diff_{\partial}(M\times[-1,1])
given by stacking
f
on
M\times[-1,0]
with
(f_{1}\times\Id_{[0,1]})\circ f^{-1}
on
M\times[0,1]
. Then
\phi=\Phi\mid_{M-\nu P\times[-1,1]}
.

•

The diffeomorphism
\Phi
can alternatively be described as the composition
(f\cup f_{1}\times\Id_{[0,1]})\circ(\Id_{[-1,0]}\cup f^{-1})
. The isotopy
\eta
is given, at time
t\in[0,1]
, by the diffeomorphism
\eta(t)=(f_{t}\cup f_{1}\times\Id_{[t,1]})\circ(\Id_{[-1,-t]}\cup f^{-1}_{t})
, where
f_{t}
and
f^{-1}_{t}
are the diffeomorphisms of
M\times[-1,t]
and
M\times[-t,1]
obtained from
f
and
f^{-1}
by linearly identifying
I
with
[-1,t]
and
I
with
[-t,1]
, respectively.

By construction, these maps fit in a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{C}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CEmb(P,M).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{res}}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{res}_{P}}

Remark A.1.2
.

There is a simplicial model for
\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)
, whose
p
-simplices are (roughly) families of tuples
(\varphi,\psi,\phi,\eta)
parametrised by the geometric simplex
\Delta^{p}
. This model is implicitly used in the definition of the map
\kappa
in
A.1.1
(iii), and we will often use both models interchangeably.

Lemma A.1.3
.

The map
u:\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)\to\CEmb(P,M)
of
A.1.1
(iv) is an equivalence.

Proof.

Consider the based space
X
consisting of triples
(\varphi,\psi,\phi)
, where
\varphi
,
\psi
and
\phi
are as in
A.1.1
(i), and with basepoint the identity tuple
(\iota\times\Id_{[-1,0]},\iota\times\Id_{[0,1]},\Id_{(M-\nu P)\times\ [-1,1]})
, which we shall also denote by
\iota
. This space fits in between
\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)
and
\CEmb(P,M)
as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CEmb(P,M),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

where
\beta(\varphi,\psi,\phi,\eta)\coloneq(\varphi,\psi,\phi)
and
\alpha(\varphi,\psi,\phi)\coloneq\varphi
—in the simplicial versions of these three spaces, both
\alpha
and
\beta
are Kan fibrations. We obtain a homotopy fibre sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hofib_{\iota}(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hofib_{\iota}(\alpha\circ\beta)=\hofib_{\iota}(u)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hofib_{\iota}(\alpha),}

(A.1.1)

and, since
\CEmb(P,M)
is connected under our assumption
d-p\leq 3
, it suffices to show that the middle term in this fibre sequence is contractible. The left term
\hofib_{\iota}(\beta)
is visibly the loop space
\Omega\Diff_{\partial}(M\times[-1,1])
(i.e. the space of self-isotopies of the identity), whilst the right term
\hofib_{\iota}(\alpha)
is the homotopy fibre of the complement map
\kappa:\Emb_{\partial}(P\times[-1,1],M\times[-1,1])\to\BDiffb(M-\nu P\times[-1,1])
; by the isotopy extension theorem, it is equivalent to
\Diffb(M\times[-1,1])
. One verifies that, under these equivalences, the connecting map
\delta:\Omega\hofib_{\iota}(\alpha)\to\hofib_{\iota}(\beta)
is the identity map of
\Omega\Diffb(M\times[-1,1])
. It remains to argue that
\pi_{0}(\hofib_{\iota}(u))
is a singleton. For this, consider the long exact sequence of homotopy groups for (
A.1.1
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(\hofib_{\iota}(\alpha))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\hofib_{\iota}(\beta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\hofib_{\iota}(u))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\hofib_{\iota}(\alpha)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

The map
i
only hits the trivial component, for it sends (the component of) a tuple
(\iota\times\Id_{[0,1]},\psi,\phi,\eta)
to
(\iota\times\Id_{[0,1]},\psi,\phi)
, and
\eta
(and isotopy extension) provides a path from the latter to the basepoint. Now one easily sees that the map
a
is a pointed group action. But since
\delta
is an isomorphism, the claim follows.
∎

Proposition A.1.4
.

Let
\H^{s}(Q)\subset\H(Q)
denote the component of the trivial
h
-cobordism on
Q
, which is equivalent to the classifying space
\BC(Q)
. If
d-p\leq 3
, there is an
\iota_{\H}
-equivariant homotopy fibre sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H^{s}(M-\nu P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H^{s}(M).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}

(If
d\geq 5
, the above remains a fibre sequence if we replace
\H^{s}(-)\simeq\BC(-)
by the full
h
-cobordism space
\H(-)
.) Thus,
\iota_{\H}
on
\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)
models the
h
-cobordism involution on
\CEmb(P,M)
.

Proof.

Recall the fibration
\operatorname{C}(Q)\overset{a}{\to}E(Q)\overset{p}{\to}\H(Q)
of (
4.1.24
), where
E(Q)
is the space of embeddings
\Emb_{Q\times\{-1\}}(Q\times[-1,0],Q\times[-1,1])
, and hence contractible as it is a space of collars. We construct (up to wrong-way equivalences) a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{C}(M-\nu P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{C}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{P}\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(M-\nu P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H^{s}(M-\nu P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\H^{s}(M),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}

(A.1.2)

where the left column is the usual path-space fibration for
\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)
, the middle and right columns are the fibre sequences (
4.1.24
), and the middle and top rows are homotopy fibre sequences: the middle one only involves contractible spaces, and the top one is obtained from the isotopy extension fibre sequence
\operatorname{C}(M-\nu P)\to\operatorname{C}(M)\xrightarrow{\underline{\mathrm{res}}_{P}}\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)
using
Lemma

A.1.3
. In particular, the bottom row becomes a homotopy fibre sequence upon looping once, and hence is itself a homotopy fibre sequence for the homotopy fibre of
\H^{s}(M-\nu P)\to\H^{s}(M)
is connected by Hudson’s theorem, and
\pi_{0}(\underline{\CEmb}(P,M))\cong\pi_{0}(\CEmb(P,M))=*
again by Hudson’s theorem and
Lemma

A.1.3
.

The right part of (
A.1.2
) is clear, so let us construct the left subsquares. Consider the restriction map
r_{P}:E(M)\to E(P,M)=\Emb_{P\times{-1}}(P\times[-1,0],M\times[-1,1])
, which is compatible with both
\mathrm{res}_{P}
and
\underline{\mathrm{res}}_{P}
. For a compact manifold
Q\subset\mathbf{R}^{\infty}
, we model
\BC(Q)
as the moduli space of manifolds

\BC(Q)\simeq\Emb_{Q\times\{-1\}}(Q\times[-1,0],\mathbf{R}^{\infty}\times[-1,+\infty))/\Diff_{Q\times\{-1\}}(Q\times[-1,0]).

(The map
\operatorname{C}(Q)\to\Diff_{Q\times\{-1\}}(Q\times[-1,0])
is an equivalence). Then the left subsquares in (
A.1.2
) are

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega\CEmb(P,M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hofib_{\iota}(\mathrm{res}_{P})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hofib_{\iota}(\underline{\mathrm{res}}_{P})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{C}(M-\nu P)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{P}\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hofib_{\iota}(E(M)\to E(P,M))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(M-\nu P)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\BC(M-\nu P)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\H^{s}(M-\nu P),}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega u}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where notation is as follows: an arrow being unlabelled means that the arrow is the obvious map. The maps
\delta
and
\underline{\delta}
are the evident connecting maps for the isotopy extension fibre sequences. The map
i
sends a path of tuples
\{(\varphi_{s},\psi_{s},\phi_{s},\eta_{s})\}_{s\in[0,1]}
to
(\phi_{1}\cup\varphi_{1},\eta_{1}\mid_{P\times[-1,0]})
. The map
c
sends a pair
(\phi,\eta)
to the complement
M\times[-1,0]-\phi(\nu P\times[-1,0])
. The map
k
sends a tuple
(\varphi,\psi,\phi,\eta)
to the complement
M\times[-1,0]-\varphi(\nu P\times[-1,0])
. Finally, the map
w
sends a partition
(W,F,V)
to
W\subset\mathbf{R}^{\infty}\times[-,+\infty)
(where we have fixed beforehand an embedding
M-\nu P\subset M\subset\mathbf{R}^{\infty}
). All of the subsquares in this diagram are commutative on the nose, except for the bottom subtriangle, which is homotopy commutative (the homotopy being provided by the data of
\phi
and
\eta
in a tuple
(\varphi,\psi,\phi,\eta)\in\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)
). The map
w
is clearly an equivalence—both domain and codomain are models for the classifying space of
\operatorname{C}(M-\nu P)
. The middle horizontal wrong-way map is an equivalence as domain and codomain are contractible. The top wrong-way map is an equivalence since the top diagonal map is an equivalence by isotopy extension, and the top-middle vertical map is an equivalence by
Lemma

A.1.3
. Finally, that the fibre sequence in the statement is
\iota_{\H}
-equivariant is clear. This concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary A.1.5
.

The restriction map
\underline{\mathrm{res}}_{P}:(\operatorname{C}(M),\iota_{\operatorname{C}})\to(\underline{\CEmb}(P,M),\iota_{\H})
is anti-equivariant. More concretely, the diagram of spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{C}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{C}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\CEmb}(P,M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{inv}\ \circ\ \iota_{\operatorname{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{res}}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{res}}_{P}}

is commutative, where
\mathrm{inv}(\phi)=\phi^{-1}
. Thus, up to equivalence, so is
\mathrm{res}_{P}:(\operatorname{C}(M),\iota_{\operatorname{C}})\to(\CEmb(P,M),\iota_{\H})
.

Proof.

This follows by a straightforward verification. Note that
\mathrm{inv}:G\to G
on any topological group
G
acts by
-1
on homotopy groups based at the identity.
∎

A.2
Proof of
4.3.10

The aim of this subsection is to prove
4.3.10
, thus establishing
4.3.9
, and hence
4.3.5
. We recall the statement:

Proposition

(

)
.

There is a homotopy commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\operatorname{C}(Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}\left(\frac{\Diff^{b}_{\partial}(Q\times\mathbf{R}^{\infty})}{\Diff_{\partial}(Q)}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\mathcal{C}(Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}\mathcal{H}(Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty+2}(\mathbf{H}(Q))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty+2}\big(\mathbf{H}(Q)_{hC_{2}}\big),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{stab}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\circ\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\mathsf{Bd}_{Q}}_{\infty}}

\scriptstyle\sim

(
4.3.8
)
(
4.1.14
)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

which is natural in codimension-zero embeddings.

This proposition will be a consequence of the following general result in orthogonal calculus—see
Section

4.1.2
for a (rather) brief overview of the theory.

Lemma A.2.1
.

For
F:\mathcal{J}\to\mathrm{Spc}_{*}
an orthogonal functor, the following commutes up to natural homotopy:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{(1)}(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{hofib}(F(0)\to F(\mathbf{R}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{(1)}(\mathbf{R}^{\infty})\coloneq\mathrm{hofib}(F(0)\to F(\mathbf{R}^{\infty}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega^{\infty}(\Theta F^{(1)})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega^{\infty}\big(\Theta F^{(1)}_{h\mathrm{O}(1)}\big),}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coloneq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{stab.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{F}_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

(A.2.1)

where the left vertical arrow is the evident inclusion into the colimit
\Omega^{\infty}(\Theta F^{(1)})\simeq\hocolim_{k}\Omega^{k}(F^{(1)}(\mathbf{R}^{k}))
,
q:\Theta F^{(1)}\to\Theta F^{(1)}_{h\mathrm{O}(1)}
denotes the natural quotient map, and
\Phi^{F}_{\infty}
is the map from (
4.1.6
).

Proof.

In
[
66
, Prop. 2.2]
, the following was established: for each
k\geq 0
, there are maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{k}^{F}:\hofib(F(0)\to F(\mathbf{R}^{k}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hofib(T_{1}F(0)\to T_{1}F(\mathbf{R}^{k}))\simeq\Omega^{\infty}(S(\mathbf{R}^{k})_{+}\wedge_{\mathrm{O}(1)}\Theta F^{(1)}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{1}}

where
S(V)
denotes the unit sphere of
V
, which give rise to maps of homotopy fibre sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hofib(F(0)\to F(\mathbf{R}^{k}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hofib(F(0)\to F(\mathbf{R}^{k+1}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hofib(F(\mathbf{R}^{k})\to F(\mathbf{R}^{k+1}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(S(\mathbf{R}^{k})_{+}\wedge_{\mathrm{O}(1)}\Theta F^{(1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(S(\mathbf{R}^{k+1})_{+}\wedge_{\mathrm{O}(1)}\Theta F^{(1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(\Sigma^{k}\Theta F^{(1)}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{k}^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{k+1}^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{stab.}}

(A.2.2)

The top-right term is the unstable derivative
\Theta F^{(1)}_{k}
, and the rightmost vertical map “
\mathrm{stab.}
” is again the natural inclusion
\smash{\Theta F^{(1)}_{k}\xhookrightarrow{}\operatorname{hocolim}_{m}\Omega^{m}(\Theta F^{(1)}_{k+m})}
. Then, the map
\smash{\Phi^{F}_{\infty}}
of (
4.1.6
) is simply
\smash{\hocolim_{k}\Phi_{k}^{F}}
.

The claim follows almost immediately from the diagram (
A.2.2
). For
k=0
, it yields the left subsquare in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{(1)}(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hofib(F(0)\to F(\mathbf{R}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hofib(F(0)\to F(\mathbf{R}^{\infty}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\Theta F^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(S(\mathbf{R})_{+}\wedge_{\mathrm{O}(1)}\Theta F^{(1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(\Theta F^{(1)}_{h\mathrm{O}(1)}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{stab.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{1}^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{F}_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Since the action of
\mathrm{O}(1)
on
S(\mathbf{R})=\{\pm 1\}
is free, the map
S(\mathbf{R})_{+}\wedge_{\mathrm{O}(1)}X\to X
is in fact an isomorphism for every
\mathrm{O}(1)
-spectrum
X
. Composing its inverse with the inclusion map
S(\mathbf{R})_{+}\wedge_{\mathrm{O}(1)}X\to S(\mathbf{R}^{\infty})_{+}\wedge_{\mathrm{O}(1)}X=X_{h\mathrm{O}(1)}
gives the quotient map
q:X\to X_{h\mathrm{O}(1)}
.
∎

We claim that the diagram of
4.3.9
(looped once) is equivalent to the diagram (
A.2.1
) (looped twice) for the functor
F=\mathsf{E}_{X_{g}}\coloneq\mathsf{E}_{(\iota(X_{g}),X_{g})}
of
4.1.1
(ii) which, we recall, is the homotopy fibre
\hofib(\mathsf{Bd}_{S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}}\to\mathsf{Bd}_{X_{g}})
(see
4.1.1
(i) for the definition of
\mathsf{Bd}_{Q}
). In order to show this, we need to establish the analogous claim for the functor
\mathsf{Bd}_{Q}
for
Q=S^{1}\times D^{2n-1}
and
X_{g}
, which is what we do now.

Proof of
4.3.10
.

We show that the diagram in the statement is naturally equivalent to (
A.2.1
) looped once for
F=\mathsf{Bd}_{Q}
—naturality of the square in the statement will then be clear. Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega^{2}\mathsf{Bd}_{Q}^{(1)}(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega^{2}\hofib(\mathsf{Bd}_{Q}(0)\to\mathsf{Bd}_{Q}(\mathbf{R}^{\infty}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega\operatorname{C}(Q)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega^{2}\left(\frac{\Diff_{\partial}^{b}(Q\times\mathbf{R}^{\infty})}{\Diff_{\partial}(Q)}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega\mathcal{C}(Q)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega^{\infty+2}(\mathbf{H}(Q))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega^{\infty+2}\big(\mathbf{H}(Q)_{hC_{2}}\big)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega^{\infty+2}\big(\Theta\mathsf{Bd}_{Q}^{(1)}\big)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega^{\infty+2}\big((\Theta\mathsf{Bd}_{Q}^{(1)})_{h\mathrm{O}(1)}\big).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{alex}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{stab.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\mathsf{Bd}_{Q}}_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\circ\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{stab.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\mathsf{Bd}_{Q}}_{\infty}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eqref{cCvscH}+\eqref{StableCEmbIETSeq}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

The right and bottom subsquares are commutative by definition. The left subsquare is (
4.1.15
), and is commutative by
[
76
, Lem. 1.12 & Cor. 1.13]
(from which the name of the map “alex” is borrowed, and whose construction we will recall later). It remains to show that the top subsquare is also homotopy commutative; that is, we need to construct a homotopy commutative diagram (top subsquare itself commutes by definition)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\mathsf{Bd}^{(1)}(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\hofib(\mathsf{Bd}(0)\to\mathsf{Bd}(\mathbf{R}^{\infty}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}\frac{\Diff^{b}_{\partial}(Q\times\mathbf{R})}{\Diff_{\partial}(Q)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}\frac{\Diff^{b}_{\partial}(Q\times\mathbf{R}^{\infty})}{\Diff_{\partial}(Q)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}\frac{\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}^{b}_{\partial}(Q\times\mathbf{R}^{\infty})}{\Diff_{\partial}(Q)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\operatorname{C}(Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}\left(\frac{\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}}{\Diff}\right)_{\partial}(Q)}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{alex}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

(A.2.3)

that is natural in codimension-zero embeddings of compact manifolds
Q\xhookrightarrow{}Q^{\prime}
. The bottom square in (
A.2.3
) is itself obtained as the homotopy fibre of the map from the outer square to the inner square in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega\Diff_{\partial}(Q)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega\Diff_{\partial}(Q)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega\Diff^{b}_{\partial}(Q\times\mathbf{R})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega\Diff^{b}_{\partial}(Q\times\mathbf{R}^{\infty})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}^{b}_{\partial}(Q\times\mathbf{R}^{\infty})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Diff_{\partial}(Q\times I)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}_{\partial}(Q\times I)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}_{\partial}(Q)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega\Diff_{\partial}(Q)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega\Diff_{\partial}(Q).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\times\Id_{\mathbf{R}}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{alex}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}

(A.2.4)

It remains to show that this diagram is homotopy commutative—this is clear for all subsquares except the left and center ones.

We first recall the definition of the Alexander trick-like map “alex”: given a diffeomorphism
\phi\in\Diff_{\partial}(Q\times I)
, we obtain a bounded diffeomorphism
\overline{\phi}
of
Q\times\mathbf{R}
by extending
\phi
by the identity on
M\times\mathbf{R}-M\times I
. Then, the based loop
\mathrm{alex}(\phi)
is given by

\mathrm{alex}(\phi):[-\infty,+\infty]\longrightarrow\Diff^{b}_{\partial}(Q\times\mathbf{R}),\quad s\longmapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\Id_{Q\times\mathbf{R}}&s=\pm\infty,\\[4.0pt]
\tau_{s}\circ\overline{\phi}\circ\tau_{-s}&s\in(-\infty,+\infty),\end{array}\right.

where
\tau_{s}:Q\times\mathbf{R}\cong Q\times\mathbf{R}
is the translation map
\tau_{s}(x,t)\coloneq(x,t+s)
.

We now describe an explicit homotopy
\mathrm{alex}\circ\Gamma\sim(-)\times\Id_{\mathbf{R}}
, showing that the left subsquare in (
A.2.4
) is homotopy commutative. Fix once and for all a smooth identification
\psi:[0,+\infty]\cong[0,1]
. Then, such homotopy is given by a map

H:[0,+\infty]\times[-\infty,+\infty]\times\Omega\Diff_{\partial}(Q)\longrightarrow\Diff^{b}_{\partial}(Q\times\mathbf{R}),\quad(t,s,\{\phi_{r}\}_{r\in[0,1]})\longmapsto H_{t}(s,\{\phi_{r}\})

where
H_{t}(s,\{\phi_{r}\})
is the bounded diffeomorphism of
Q\times\mathbf{R}
given by

H_{t}(s,\{\phi_{r}\})\equiv\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\tau_{s-t}\circ\overline{\Gamma(\{\phi_{r}\})}\circ\tau_{t-s}&\text{on $(-\infty,s+\psi(t)-t]$},\\[4.0pt]
\phi_{\psi(t)}\times\Id_{\mathbf{R}}&\text{on $[s+\psi(t)-t,s+\psi(t)+t]$},\\[4.0pt]
\tau_{s+t}\circ\overline{\Gamma(\{\phi_{r}\})}\circ\tau_{-s-t}&\text{on $[s+\psi(t)+t,+\infty)$.}\end{array}\right.

There are some issues regarding the smoothness of
H_{t}(s,\{\phi_{r}\})
at the points
Q\times\{s+\psi(t)\pm t\}
, but these can easily be overcome by choosing a suitable smoothing of the piecewise linear function

[0+\infty)\times\mathbf{R}\to\mathbf{R},\quad(t,x)\longmapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}x+t,&x\leq-t,\\
0,&|x|\leq t,\\
x-t,&x\geq t.\end{array}\right.

To see that the central subsquare in (
A.2.4
) is also homotopy commutative, note that it factors as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega\Diff^{b}_{\partial}(Q\times\mathbf{R})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega\Diff^{b}_{\partial}(Q\times\mathbf{R}^{\infty})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}_{\partial}^{b}(Q\times\mathbf{R})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}^{b}_{\partial}(Q\times\mathbf{R}^{\infty})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Diff_{\partial}(Q\times I)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}_{\partial}(Q\times I)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega\smash{\mathrm{\widetilde{\Diff}}}_{\partial}(Q)}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{alex}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\times\Id_{\mathbf{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{alex}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Gamma}}

All sub-parts of this diagram now homotopy commute either by definition, or by setting up the homotopy
H
above simplicially, which is routine. This finishes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Remark A.2.2
.

As a consequence of this proof and that of
4.3.9
, it follows that the square in that statement looped once is equivalent to that in (
A.2.1
) for
F=\mathsf{E}_{X_{g}}
when looped twice.
